The IISS Strategic Dossier UAVs: ISR, Deterrence and War considers the key role of 
intelligence, surveillance and reconnaissance (ISR) uninhabited aerial vehicles (UAVs) in 
both peace and war. This volume reviews how select states in Europe, the Middle East 
and Asia employ UAVs for ISR in support of deterrence and combat. It also assesses 
UAV use in Russia’s war in Ukraine and the broader lessons this may oﬀ  er. The volume, 
additionally, explores how the selected countries are approaching the development 
and/or acquisition of what are commonly known as collaborative combat aircraft (CCA). 
Space is of ever-growing importance for ISR, but UAVs will continue to oﬀ  er a 
key utility. For some, they provide a supporting capability to space-based systems, for 
others, they may be the primary source of ISR provision. 
The war in Ukraine has underscored the utility of UAVs while also exposing their 
vulnerability. Emerging classes of UAVs have been employed by both belligerents, 
including ﬁ  rst-person view and one-way att  ack UAVs. Medium-altitude long-endurance 
UAVs continue to be used, though the tactics, techniques and procedures for their 
employment have been adapted to reduce their exposure to ground-based air defence.   
This volume provides a valuable resource for understanding the critical role 
UAVs play in ISR and how these platforms are increasingly used to deliver other 
capabilities. It oﬀ  ers insights and emerging lessons from the use of uninhabited 
systems in a peer-on-peer war. Additionally, it assesses the roles now being 
considered for CCAs, as these countries each look to augment their inventories of 
crewed combat aircraft.

5UAVS: ISR, DETERRENCE AND WAR 
Executive Summary
Intelligence, surveillance and recon-
naissance (ISR) remains a funda-
mental military capability in peace, 
war and the grey zone in-between. 
Uninhabited aerial vehicles (UAVs), 
in increasingly diverse shapes and 
sizes, are now nearly ubiquitous 
among states with armed forces, with 
ISR often playing a central role.
UAVs arguably came of age, 
and began to be propelled into the 
wider public consciousness, with 
the deployment of the General 
Atomics MQ-1 Predator over Bosnia-
Herzegovina in 1995. In the 30 
years since, UAVs have gone from 
a niche capability for ISR to a core 
element in the inventories of many 
armed forces. Those three decades 
have seen roles expand beyond 
ISR – designs and operational con-
cepts have multiplied as the utility 
of airborne uninhabited systems 
has been reinforced repeatedly in 
combat operations.
This IISS Strategic Dossier, UAVs: 
ISR, Deterrence and War, considers 
developments in Europe, the Middle 
East and the Indo-Pacifi  c, and reviews 
the roles, impact and implications of 
UAV use in Russia’s war of aggres-
sion in Ukraine. In Ukraine, UAVs 
have been used within extended kill 
chains and also networks, as the elec-
tromagnetic spectrum has become 
increasingly contested. ISR today has 
a foundational role in deterrence, and 
UAVs play a signifi  cant part in its 
provision. Within alliances or secu-
rity partnerships, sharing ISR off  ers 
a multiplier eff  ect while also rein-
forcing cohesion. Meanwhile, ISR 
remains an essential element of the 
spectrum of capabilities required to 
support deterrence. Should deter-
rence fail, then ISR is a critical ele-
ment of combat.
All of the regions considered are 
now actively pursuing uninhabited-
systems projects and acquisitions for 
ISR and combat roles. Deteriorating 
security environments and concerns 
about the risk of war have been key 
drivers. Furthermore, eff  orts to build 
national development and manu-
facturing capacity are refl  ected in 
all regions. Many countries, how-
ever, are trying to reconcile the 
competing demands of national 
capacity-building with military 
needs. The former requires time, the 
latt  er immediacy.
This is all too evident in Europe, 
where the latest multinational 
eff  ort to develop a medium-altitude 
long-endurance (MALE) UAV is fal-
tering. Given the continent’s history 
in this endeavour, it would be unsur-
prising were this project to unravel. 
The combat roles of uninhabited 
systems continue to expand. Many 
MALE UAVs capable of ISR are 
=== PAGE 7 ===
AN IISS STRATEGIC DOSSIER6
now also armed, combining the sensor and the 
shooter. Perhaps a more significant shift, how-
ever, is the development of combat platforms 
dedicated to operating in concert with crewed 
combat aircraft. In Europe, the Middle East and 
the Indo-Pacific, there are multiple projects at 
different stages of development with varying 
degrees of ambition for collaborative com-
bat aircraft (CCA). One goal is to supplement 
limited numbers of crewed combat aircraft with 
a greater mass of uninhabited systems, while a 
second is to reduce the risk to the former. CCAs 
could begin to appear in some of these regions 
no later than the early 2030s. Their arrival, how-
ever, will not presage the end of the crewed 
combat aircraft; rather, CCAs will be a comple-
ment to at least one more generation of inhab-
ited multi-role fighters.

7UAVS: ISR, DETERRENCE AND WAR 
KEY TAKEAWAYS
DETERRENCE AND ISR 
Intelligence, surveillance and reconnaissance (ISR) 
is part of the deterrence toolkit and a fundamental 
capability should deterrence fail. 
MASS IS A MULTIPLIER/uni00A0
In alliance or security-partner constructs shared 
ISR capabilities and the resulting material oﬀ  ers a 
multiplier eﬀ  ect, while reinforcing cohesion.  
INDICATORS OF INTENT 
Detection by ISR does not always equate to dissuasion, 
but it has an inherent value nonetheless by providing 
early indicators of intent. 
LACK OF SPACE 
Within Europe there is limited capacity for geospatial 
ISR collection, similarly with airborne crewed and  
uninhabited-aerial-vehicle ISR.

CHAPTER ONE
‘I know what you are thinking of 
doing, so don’t do it’ is a common 
admonition, but it seldom has the 
desired result. In the absence of a 
credible threat, even when unde-
sirable or disruptive behaviour is 
identifi  ed, the chances of  changing 
behaviour are low. Russian President 
Vladimir Putin’s decision to launch 
a full-blown invasion of Ukraine has 
ruptured Europe’s security architec-
ture, regardless of cautions from the 
United States and some allies – fi  rst in 
private, then in public – that his inten-
tion to invade was known.1.
Russia’s ill-judged, poorly executed
full-scale invasion of Ukraine, that 
began on 24 February 2022, has 
ensnared Moscow in a brutal and 
grinding war. Western intelligence, 
surveillance and reconnaissance (ISR) 
contributed signifi  cantly to the evi-
dence of Putin’s intent in the months, 
weeks and eventually days before the 
war, but it did not stop his invasion. 
So, did deterrence fail?
Revealing publicly the informa-
tion supported by ISR was not, in 
isolation, going to deter Russia. The 
limits of US and European allies’ 
deterrence when it came to Russian 
ambitions in Ukraine were made 
apparent to the Russian president 
by 2014, with the West’s ineff  ectual 
response to Russia’s annexation 
of Crimea and partial invasion of 
Ukraine.2 Ukraine had a greater 
value to Moscow than it had to 
Washington, and Putin was willing to 
place a far larger wager than the US 
and its allies gave the impression of 
being prepared to match. However, 
in 2022 the use of ISR to support the 
case against Russia was not without 
deterrent value. This was a second-
ary eff  ect in that it publicised Putin’s 
duplicity and began to raise wider 
awareness in Europe of the increas-
ing challenge posed by an aggressive 
Russia. The use of ISR-based mate-
rial, furthermore, helped provide 
an assurance factor to US allies as 
to the nature of Moscow’s activities. 
There had been initial scepticism 
about the United States’ and United 
Kingdom’s warnings among some 
states.3 This was likely based on the 
legacy of the failure to fi  nd weapons 
of mass destruction following the 
2003 invasion of Iraq. In that case, 
partial intelligence was seen to have 
been misused, overtly politicised and 
as such devalued.4  
Despite the US and the UK hav-
ing been proved correct concerning 
Moscow’s intentions, there remains 
an open question, underscored by 
European concerns over the future 
of the transatlantic relationship, over 
the extent to  which countries will be 
willing to rely on the US for  strategic 
ISR capabilities.
=== PAGE 9 ===
CHAPTER  ONE
AN IISS STRATEGIC DOSSIER8
This dossier considers the role that ISR, and 
specifically the capacity provided by uninhab-
ited aerial vehicles (UAVs), plays in deterrence 
and in supporting the initial intelligence prepa-
ration of the battlespace should hostilities break 
out. It will, in part, use the prism of recent events 
in Ukraine, both prior to Russia’s invasion and 
during the subsequent fighting, to help illumi-
nate the roles UAVs have played throughout 
the war, and any broader implications thereof. 
This volume will review UAV developments in 
Europe, the Middle East and the Indo-Pacific, 
focusing on their use in ISR in their respective 
regions at ‘peace’ and war. It does not focus on 
the US beyond the inevitable wider influence of 
American developments, nor on China or Russia, 
each alone of which is worthy of a volume.
As ISR ranges from the strategic to the tac-
tical realms, so too do the UAVs associated 
with these roles. The ‘peacetime’ deterrent 
contribution of UAV-derived ISR has effect at 
the tactical level, but it is most apparent at the 
strategic level.   
Developing deterrence 
Long associated mainly with nuclear policy, 
deterrence is now viewed broadly in public dis-
cussion as encompassing all aspects of military 
capability, and to some extent even more widely 
as a whole-of-government capacity. Some now 
view national resilience, the protection of criti-
cal infrastructure and defence-industrial capac-
ity as contributing to a deterrence posture that 
has moved far beyond the nuclear realm. 
The latest in an ever-growing list of terms is 
integrated deterrence. This term found favour 
during the administration of US president Joe 
Biden, with this language featuring in a trium-
virate of 2022 security-policy documents: the 
National Defense Strategy, the Nuclear Posture 
Review and the Missile Defense Review. 5 The 
defence-strategy document described integrated 
deterrence as ‘using every tool at the [Defense] 
Department’s disposal in close collaboration 
… across the U.S. Government and with Allies 
… to ensure that potential foes understand 
the folly of aggression’. 6 Similar language was 
adopted in the UK’s Integrated Review Refresh 
2023, an early update of its 2021 Integrated 
Review, with the policy document underscoring 
an ‘ongoing shift to an integrated approach to 
deterrence and defence’. 7 This theme was con-
tinued in the UK’s 2025 Strategic Defence and 
Security Review.8 US President Donald Trump’s 
far more transactional approach to international 
relations, however, places in doubt the degree to 
which allies may continue to simply assume US 
support as part of their own ‘integrated deter-
rence’ strategies.
John J. Mearsheimer’s Cold War study 
‘Conventional Deterrence’, published in 1983, 
defined deterrence as ‘persuading an opponent 
not to initiate a specific action because the per-
ceived benefits do not justify the estimated costs 
and risks’. 9 Putin’s apparent perception of the 
perceived costs and risks of a full-blown inva-
sion of Ukraine were outweighed by the benefits 
he appears to have believed he would gain by 
seizing Ukraine. Returning Kyiv to Moscow’s 
orbit, replacing Ukrainian President Volodymyr 
Zelenskyy with a pro-Russian puppet, and 
preventing Ukraine from joining the European 
Union and potentially NATO was a greater prize 
by far than any punishment signalled by the 
US and its allies. The willingness and ability to 
impose ‘unacceptable costs’ – a phrase common 
in the Cold War language of nuclear deterrence 
– needs to be credible in the thinking of those 
one intends to deter. Arguably, the capacity to 
execute rather than the guarantee is most use-
ful in deterrence terms. A declaratory warning 
of retribution that is not then acted upon courts 
the undesirable outcome of calling into question 
the willingness to enforce, irrespective of hav-
ing the capacity to do so. ISR, including UAV 
capabilities, had provided the US and its allies 
with pre-war insight into Russia’s intention, but 
alone was never enough to dissuade Putin. 
Russia’s full-blown invasion of Ukraine, 
while illegal, posed no immediate threat 
to Europe and none to the US. The goal of 
Washington and many European capitals was 
not to dissuade Moscow from using military 
force against them, but to try to stop it being 
wielded against Kyiv. They aimed to uphold the 
United Nations Charter’s Article 2 and the rules-
based order that emerged from the debris of the 
Second World War, and to block any use of force 
to redraw national boundaries. 10 For Putin, this 
likely only served to flag what he had already 
maintained was the United States’ and its allies’ 
hypocrisy when it came to armed intervention 
and regime change. 11  
=== PAGE 10 ===
INTRODUCTION
9UAVS: ISR, DETERRENCE AND WAR 
The US and several other NATO members 
increasingly used their ISR capabilities to mon-
itor the build-up of Russian forces in order to 
paint a picture of their activity and provide the 
evidential base with which to confront Moscow 
and induce Russian de-escalation. Initially car-
ried out privately using diplomatic channels, 
when a behind-closed-doors approach did not 
have the desired outcome, intelligence assess-
ments that drew on ISR-derived material were 
increasingly aired in public. 12
The ABCs of ISR
The ISR acronym comprises two activities and one 
product: surveillance and reconnaissance being 
the former, and intelligence the latter. The phrase 
only entered the military lexicon in the 1990s but 
has since been adopted widely to describe a broad 
range of activities and capabilities. The basic 
activities and the product, however, are as old as 
warfare itself. What has changed are the collection 
methods, the type and volume of data collected, 
how data is exploited and the speed at which this 
is carried out and the information disseminated. 
While for centuries it was only possible to 
effectively carry out surveillance and reconnais-
sance in visible light, today collection occurs across 
the electromagnetic spectrum. This, of course, 
creates its own problems. Surveillance for most 
of the twentieth century produced only limited 
amounts of data; for example, the early genera-
tions of photo reconnaissance satellites used wet 
film that had to be returned from low-earth orbit 
for exploitation. 13 Now, electro-optical satellites 
can gather multi-spectral imagery that can be 
transmitted to a ground station in near-real time. 
INTELLIGENCE VS ISR
The terms intelligence and ISR are often used 
interchangeably. In many contexts their diﬀerences are 
subtle enough. However, with respect to this doctrine 
there are important distinctions worth noting and it 
becomes increasingly important to diﬀerentiate between 
the two./uni00A0
Joint Publication 2-0, Joint Intelligence, provides the 
following de/f_initions:/uni00A0
INTELLIGENCE
1. The product resulting from the collection, processing, 
integration, evaluation, analysis, and interpretation of 
available information concerning foreign nations, hostile 
or potentially hostile forces or elements, or areas of actual 
or potential operations. 
2. The activities that result in the product. 
3. The organizations conducting such activities./uni00A0/uni00A0
ISR
1. An integrated operations and intelligence activity
that synchronizes and integrates the planning and 
operation of sensors; assets; and processing, 
exploitation, and dissemination systems in direct 
support of current and future operations. 
2. The organizations or assets conducting such activities./uni00A0
Both terms refer to activities and the organizations that 
conduct them. To discern between the two, it is important 
to identify their purpose or aim. ISR activities produce 
information and data. Through the joint intelligence process, 
information and data is then used to produce intelligence. 
From this perspective, ISR is a subordinate intelligence activity 
conducted to gather information and data necessary to meet 
intelligence requirements. With this understanding, uses of 
each term in this publication are intended to be as deliberate 
and accurate as possible.
Source: US Air Force, ‘Doctrine Publication 2-0: Intelligence’ , 1 June 2023, https://www.doctrine.af.mil/Portals/61/documents/AFDP_2-0/2-0-AFDP-INTELLIGENCE.pdf

CHAPTER  ONE
AN IISS STRATEGIC DOSSIER10
The challenge is no longer one of being compara-
tively data-poor but rather that of being data-rich.
ISR collection capabilities widened consider-
ably with the advent of powered flight and the 
subsequent ability to place objects into orbit. The 
air had already been used for ISR with balloons, 
but the development of aircraft provided far more 
flexibility as well as increasingly greater speed 
and range. Coverage then became global with the 
introduction of ISR satellites.
Crewed ISR aircraft were used widely by the 
US and some allies from the outset of and for 
the duration of the Cold War, providing mate-
rial that supported strategic, theatre and tactical 
decision-making.14 The vulnerability of aircraft to 
attack also led to the development of dedicated 
ISR aircraft, most notably the US Lockheed U-2 
and then the Lockheed A-12 and SR-71 Blackbird. 
The first relied on high-altitude flight for protec-
tion until Soviet surface-to-air missiles (SAMs) 
were able to engage aircraft at these levels 
of flight. 
The US Air Force’s (USAF) SR-71, a follow-
up to the CIA’s A-12, could be flown at even 
greater altitudes than the U-2 and, like the A-12, 
was far faster. Unlike the U-2, neither the A-12 
nor the SR-71 were successfully intercepted 
using a SAM. Both the U-2 and A-12/SR-71 air-
craft had features introduced to manage their 
radar signature.
ISR DEFINITIONS
Intelligence, surveillance and reconnaissance 
(ISR) is an integrated activity that receives 
operational tasking, provides direction to ISR 
capabilities, collects data and information, translates 
this into a useable format and sends it for use by 
decision-makers, eﬀectors and intelligence analysts. 
The ISR process delivers three primary outputs: 
support to operations; support to intelligence; and 
support to targeting.
Intelligence. The Intelligence component of 
JISR [joint ISR] refers to all intelligence collection 
disciplines, including their collection, processing, 
exploitation and dissemination capabilities/assets 
and the results they can deliver to the commander 
and staﬀ elements.
Surveillance. Surveillance refers to systematic 
observation across all operational domains, the 
information environment and across the 
cognitive, physical and virtual dimensions of 
places, persons or objects by visual, electronic, 
photographic or other means. Surveillance may 
occur over a wide area or can focus upon a 
particular location, object or actor. 
Reconnaissance. Reconnaissance is a 
mission undertaken to obtain, by visual 
observation or other detection methods, 
information about the activities and resources 
of an adversary or to obtain data concerning 
the meteorological, hydrographical or 
geographic characteristics of a particular area. 
Reconnaissance is directed observation in time, 
space and across the electromagnetic spectrum 
to obtain speci/f_ic data or information required 
for a speci/f_ic purpose.
Source: UK Ministry of Defence, ‘Integrated Warfare Centre Joint Doctrine (former DCDC) 1/23: Intelligence, Surveillance and Reconnaissance’ , 19 January 2023, https://assets.publishing.service.gov.uk/
media/641c27e15155a200136ad4df/JDN_1_23_ISR_web.pdf
TYPES OF INTELLIGENCE
Acoustic Intelligence (ACINT) results include 
the collection and exploitation of acoustic 
signals or emissions.
Human Intelligence (HUMINT) results are 
based on information which is collected and 
provided by human sources.
Imagery Intelligence (IMINT) results are 
based on the collection, processing and 
exploitation of image sequences.
Measurement and Signature Intelligence 
(MASINT) results are based on the collection 
of scienti/f_ic and technical information in 
order to obtain distinctive and 
diﬀerentiating features.
Open Source Intelligence (OSINT) results 
are based on openly available or restricted 
access information.
Signals Intelligence (SIGINT) delivers results 
by collecting and exploiting electromagnetic 
signals or emanations. The main subcategories 
of SIGINT are communications intelligence and 
electronic intelligence.
Source: NATO, ‘ AJP-2.7: Allied Joint Doctrine for Joint Intelligence, Surveillance and 
Reconnaissance’ , 7 October 2022, https://web.archive.org/web/20250728155207/https://
jadl.act.nato.int/ILIAS/data/testclient/lm_data/lm_152845/Linear/JISR04222102/
sharedFiles/AJP27.pdf
=== PAGE 12 ===
INTRODUCTION
11UAVS: ISR, DETERRENCE AND WAR 
Risk reduction
Even in ‘peacetime’, ISR collection using crewed 
aircraft was a risk. Long before the shootdown 
of the U-2 flown by Francis Gary Powers on 1 
May 1960, US aircraft and air crews were lost 
periodically.15 Nor were these losses incurred 
only during overflights of Soviet territory; there 
were multiple instances of reconnaissance aircraft 
being attacked in airspace bordering the USSR or 
China. The Powers incident resulted in the end of 
US overflights of the Soviet Union, but these types 
of flights continued covering southern China into 
the early 1970s. 16 The SR-71 was a high-altitude, 
high-speed occasional visitor to Cuban skies into 
the mid-1980s.17
The political and personnel risks that came 
with crewed ISR flights prompted efforts from 
the late 1950s onward to secure the same infor-
mation without the associated hazards. The 
United States’ Corona reconnaissance- satellite 
programme followed the earlier and more ambi-
tious WS-117L project. A UAV codenamed Red 
Wagon, meanwhile, had begun to be developed in 
the aftermath of the U-2 shootdown, but it lost out 
to the A-12.18
UAVs were operated by the US during the 1960s 
in the ISR role alongside crewed aircraft and satel-
lites. They remained a niche capability, however, 
until after the end of the Cold War. 19 The break-
out project in the 1990s arguably was the General 
Atomics RQ-1 Predator, which met an increased 
US demand for tactical rather than strategic recon-
naissance. UAVs were used for decades solely 
in the ISR role. The USAF carried out weapons-
integration trials using the Ryan BGM-34 Firebee in 
the early 1970s, but these did not lead to an opera-
tional capability.20 Only with the integration of the 
Lockheed Martin AGM-114 Hellfire short-range 
air-to-surface missile on the General Atomics 
RQ-1 did an armed UAV enter the US inventory 
as the MQ-1.21 Since then, medium-altitude long-
endurance (MALE) UAVs have increasingly been 
designed with the capacity to carry air-to-surface 
munitions as well as a variety of sensor payloads. 
The export of MALE UAVs is now often accompa-
nied by that of a weapons package. 
While this study will concentrate mainly on 
the continuing ISR role for UAVs, their combat 
capability will also inevitably be touched upon. 
Boundaries between some classes of UAVs and 
weapons have also increasingly blurred, most 
obviously with one-way attack UAVs. These have 
many characteristics of a long-range land-attack 
cruise missile, though with a smaller warhead, 
far slower speed and considerably less cost. Most 
MALE UAVs now in service or development can 
carry weapons. It is more often down to the cus-
tomers’ choice as to in what role or roles the UAV 
will be employed, rather than any inherent design 
limitation of the air vehicle, its onboard systems 
or associated ground station.
From the late 1990s, there was also renewed 
interest in developing low-observable (LO) 
or very LO UAVs for the ISR role. 22 Lockheed 
Martin’s Skunk Works produced the RQ-170 
Sentinel LO ISR UAV, which had entered service 
by 2006–07. 23 Northrop Grumman is associated 
with an air vehicle with the RQ-180 designation.24
While the USAF now acknowledges that it fields 
the RQ-170, it had yet to confirm as of early 2026 
that the larger RQ-180 is also in its inventory or 
that this is an actual designation.
Detected, not dissuaded
‘We are one people. Kiev is the mother of Russian 
cities. Ancient Rus is our common source and 
we cannot live without each other’, Putin said 
after Russia annexed Crimea in 2014. 25 Viewed 
in the light of what has happened since then, his 
comments can be read as a declaration of intent. 
However, it was only with the massing of Russian 
forces on Ukraine’s borders in April 2021, described 
by then Russian defence minister Sergei Shoigu as 
a response to external threats, that the actual threat 
to Kyiv arguably was fully recognised.26
US and allied ISR was employed to monitor 
the 2021 Russian build-up. USAF RQ-4 Global 
Hawk UAVs were operated in the Black Sea around 
Crimea and also within southeastern Ukraine. 
The US Navy (USN) Lockheed EP-3E Aries II air-
craft equipped with communications intelligence 
and electronic intelligence (ELINT) suites was 
deployed, as was the Boeing P-8A Poseidon mar-
itime-patrol and anti-submarine-warfare aircraft, 
the latter also employed in the ISR role. 27 The 
EP-3E was finally retired at the beginning of 2025, 
with the role now met by the MQ-4C Triton variant 
of the Global Hawk.28 The Royal Air Force’s (RAF) 
RC-135W Rivet Joint SIGINT aircraft were flown 
on multiple missions from the start of April 2021, 
being operated in the western Black Sea and off  
the Crimean coast. Underscoring the continuing 
=== PAGE 13 ===
CHAPTER  ONE
AN IISS STRATEGIC DOSSIER12
risk to ISR aircraft, in September 2022, an RAF 
RC-135 was engaged by a Russian Su-27 Flanker B, 
with the firing of two beyond-visual-range air-to-
air missiles, neither of which hit the target.29
Having amassed 56 battalion tactical groups 
and some 110,000 troops on Ukraine’s border, 
on 22 April 2021, Shoigu said these units would 
return to their home bases from the start of May.30
The nature of Russia’s April 2021 large-scale 
deployment, whether meant to intimidate Kyiv, 
to act as a caution to NATO, as a deployment 
rehearsal for the following year’s invasion or as 
a precursor to an operation that was cancelled, 
remains unknown. Its goals may have been sev-
eral of these. It did provide Moscow with the 
opportunity to gauge what the West could do in 
terms of ISR. Moreover, it allowed Russia to mon-
itor the broader Western response, against which 
it could calibrate its behaviour. 
Any relief in Kyiv and multiple Western 
capitals following Moscow’s ‘mission rehearsal’ 
was temporary. In a July 2021 article, Putin pro-
claimed, ‘I am confident that true sovereignty 
of Ukraine is possible only in partnership with 
Russia’.31 The launch of the full-scale invasion of 
Ukraine on 24 February 2022 finally clarified the 
Russian leader’s intentions.
The prelude 
ISR was widely utilised before and during Russia’s 
full-scale invasion of Ukraine. The USAF, USN 
and US Army carried out most of the airborne ISR 
activities over Eastern Europe and nearby areas 
of importance. The UK’s RAF, NATO and the 
Swedish Air Force supported the United States’ 
efforts. At the same time, various other NATO 
states have, in limited capacities, also supported 
Ukraine with airborne ISR operations.
Russian forces were, unsurprisingly, particu-
larly active in the regions neighbouring Ukraine 
in the run-up to the full-scale invasion. Russian 
troops were deployed to Russia’s border with 
Ukraine throughout 2021 and 2022, latterly again 
under the guise of a joint military exercise with 
Belarus in January 2022.32
The US and some allies, including the UK, 
had become convinced that Moscow intended to 
seize Ukraine and overthrow the government of 
President Zelenskyy. 
From no later than November 2021, diplomatic 
efforts were under way in an attempt to dissuade 
Putin from progressing with his plans. The then-
CIA director, William Burns, and the former US 
ambassador to Russia, John Sullivan, met with a 
member of Putin’s inner circle, Nikolai Patrushev, 
to inform him that they were aware of Moscow’s 
intentions to invade Ukraine.33 Diplomatic efforts 
continued up until the invasion on 24 February 2022.
Much of the intelligence in Western officials’ 
hands was collected either by airborne or geospa-
tial ISR. In the three weeks before Russia invaded 
Ukraine, airborne ISR operations in support of 
Kyiv intensified across three main areas: Crimea 
and the Black Sea, Kaliningrad and the Baltic Sea, 
and Ukraine/Eastern Europe.34

CHAPTER  ONE
AN IISS STRATEGIC DOSSIER14
The USAF, USN and US Army conducted 
around two-thirds of the operations across all loca-
tions. Moreover, the US had a much broader range 
of airborne ISR capabilities across the three domains 
mentioned. Hence, the reliance on the US was a 
matter not only of quantity, but also of quality.35
The USAF predominantly used the 
RC-135V/W Rivet Joint and RC-135U Combat Sent
aircraft over the Black Sea and Crimea. On occa-
sions, the Rivet Joint aircraft was used to monitor 
Ukraine’s border with Russia, though this tended 
to be more often the remit of the USAF’s RQ-4 
Global Hawk. The USAF also operated the E-8C 
Joint Surveillance Target Attack Radar System
(JSTARS) over Ukraine and less frequent flights 
of the MQ-9A Reaper, E-11A, U-2S and WC-135 
Constant Phoenix aircraft. 
The US Army maintained a near-constant 
presence around Kaliningrad with at least one 
RC-12X Guardrail SIGINT aircraft. The US Army’s 
efforts were bolstered by the redeployment of 
its Challenger 650 Airborne Reconnaissance and 
Targeting Multi-mission Intelligence System 
(ARTEMIS) from an army exercise to Europe in 
November 2021.36
The USN flew the P-8A Poseidon and EP-3E 
Aries II. One role of these aircraft was to moni-
tor Russian vessels in European waters. The P-8s 
operated across a range of locations, from as far 
south as the Black Sea up to the Barents Sea and 
the Danish Strait.
The UK and Sweden were the only other states 
that had a regular airborne ISR presence in the 
prelude to Russia’s invasion. The RAF utilised its 
fleet of three RC-135W Rivet Joints across all three 
of the focus areas, at times in tandem with its US 
counterpart. The USN’s P-8s were used predomi-
nantly to focus on the Black and Barents seas. 
Despite operating a fleet of just four ISR-
capable aircraft in the prelude to the conflict, 
comprised of two S-102B Korpen ELINT and two 
S100D Argus Airborne Early Warning & Control 
aircraft, the Swedish Air Force flew regular sor-
ties, though solely in the Baltic region.37
As Russian troops once again began to mass 
along the border in early 2022, crewed ISR air-
craft were pulled back from operations close to 
Russian territory and the region was delegated to 
UAVs. NATO provided limited support, operat-
ing its fleet of five RQ-4D Phoenix UAVs near the 
Ukraine–Russia border. The RQ-4D was oper-
ated out of Naval Air Station Sigonella, Italy, 
with extended-duration missions often flown. 38
NATO’s RQ-4Ds were supplemented by the 
Ukrainian Air Force’s Baykar Bayraktar TB2 UAVs 
in the days prior to the Russian invasion. Canada, 
Germany, Greece, Norway and Turkiye were very 
occasional contributors to ISR operations.
Alliance reliance 
Despite the number of states that conducted air-
borne ISR missions, European reliance on the US 
was evident. In the two months prior to Russia’s 
invasion, Washington operated more than 30 air-
craft, while the cumulative NATO effort, exclud-
ing the US, came to 21 aircraft. 
Furthermore, the US also provided a greater 
breadth of capabilities. The USAF’s WC-135 
Constant Phoenix was the only ISR aircraft in the 
region capable of detecting nuclear radiation. 
Not only did Europe rely on the US to conduct 
airborne ISR missions, but states also relied on 
the US as a supplier of their own ISR capabilities. 
Of the estimated 21 non-US-operated airborne 
ISR aircraft, only six were manufactured outside 
the US. The RAF enjoys the benefits of having its 
RC-135s nested within the larger USAF fleet. Its 
aircraft also receive all – or almost all – of the same 
upgrades as the USAF. It also means, however, 
that key elements of the support of this platform’s 
SIGINT systems are dependent on the US. 
This level of dependence, combined with 
Europe’s overall lack of airborne ISR numbers, an 
issue that is not exclusive to this region, is refocus-
ing attention in Europe on addressing capability 
shortfalls, particularly given renewed uncertainty 
over the durability of the previous transatlantic 
consensus over European security. 39 Europe, fur-
thermore, is no longer Washington’s overarching 
security concern.  
Addressing Australia’s parliament in 2011, 
President Barack Obama described the US as 
being ‘all in’ in the Asia-Pacific during the twenty-
first century.40 President Biden followed suit and 
endorsed the Indo-Pacific Strategy of the United 
States in February 2022. 41 Irrespective of the out-
come of the November 2024 election, the United 
States’ course was inexorably toward the Asia-
Pacific region. President Trump and Defense 
Secretary Pete Hegseth have claimed repeatedly 
that China poses the greatest threat to the US. 
Hegseth is only the latest senior defence official to 
describe Beijing as the ‘pacing threat’.42
=== PAGE 16 ===
INTRODUCTION
15UAVS: ISR, DETERRENCE AND WAR 
The Asia-Pacific will continue to draw on 
US ISR resources, airborne or orbital, at the cost 
of their availability for Europe, even if there is 
renewed US focus on the Western Hemisphere. 
Should a crisis emerge in that region, and 
Washington reprioritise its ISR capabilities, the 
challenge for Europe will become more acute. 
China, while the focus, is not the only country 
of concern for Washington, with another being 
the nuclear-armed Democratic People’s Republic 
of Korea (DPRK). The US regularly carries out 
ISR missions around the Korean Peninsula, and 
should the threat from the DPRK be perceived 
as increasing, these would inevitably become 
more frequent.
Kadena Air Base, Japan, has been the pre-
dominant forward-operating base in the Asia-
Pacific for the 82nd Reconnaissance Squadron 
since January 1968. The squadron operates 
the RC-135V/W and its related variants from 
the base. Until recently, Kadena was the only 
site in the Asia-Pacific where the RC-135V/
Ws were permanently based, posing a single 
point of failure for the USAF. Recognising this, 
the air force has set up the 55th Operations 
Group, Detachment 1 at Joint Base Elmendorf–
Richardson, Alaska. 43 Joint Base Elmendorf–
Richardson is the second permanent home 
of the RC-135V/W in the Asia-Pacific region. 
Kadena has also hosted the MQ-9, operated by 
both the USAF and US Marine Corps, as well as 
the MQ-4C Triton.
Chinese expansion, literally
Chinese activity in the South and East China seas 
has long been a cause of friction with several of its 
neighbours. Beijing’s assertive territorial claims 
combined with island-building and the militari-
sation of sites have become an increasing source 
of conflict over the past two decades, but this has 
remained largely diplomatic. ISR has and will 
continue to play a significant role in addressing 
Beijing’s territorial ambitions, for the US and its 
regional allies and partners. ISR alone will not 
temper these, but it is a valuable contributor to the 
deterrence spectrum.44
China’s ‘nine-dash line’ territorial claims 
impinge on the exclusive economic zones of 
Indonesia, Malaysia, the Philippines and Vietnam.45
The US has also shown its disapproval of China’s 
claims by using freedom-of-navigation ship transits 
and overflights in disputed areas.46 Chinese activ-
ity extends well beyond economic expansionism; 
numerous claimed islands and atolls have been 
developed with military installations, some with 
runways capable of combat-aircraft operations.47
As previously indicated, the US continues 
to provide the majority of the ISR in the Asia-
Pacific, watching Chinese activities, but even US 
resources are finite. Greater regional capacity 
among its allies would be of benefit to the US 
ISR picture, while improved national capabilities 
would provide a greater hedge against any loss of 
access to US support.48

CHAPTER  ONE
AN IISS STRATEGIC DOSSIER16
Inside or outside
The balance and the nature of space-based and 
air-based ISR is shifting, most notably in the US. 
Some of the USAF’s ‘big-wing’ ISR aircraft have 
been retired with the aim to increasingly migrate 
their capability to orbital systems. The Northrop 
Grumman E-8C was retired from the USAF’s 
inventory in November 2023. 49 The E-8C’s pri-
mary role was to provide ground moving target 
indication (GMTI). While a space-based GMTI 
architecture may be the goal, in the interim, the 
USAF retains the Block 40 version of the RQ-4 
Global Hawk, which can provide GMTI. The 
Lockheed Martin RQ-170 Sentinel may also be fit-
ted with a GMTI sensor, although the USAF has 
not confirmed this.50
Over time, orbital ISR has expanded from the 
strategic to the operational realm and from exclu-
sively state-owned and -operated satellites to the 
private sector. Tactical ISR needs require persis-
tence and immediacy. This has previously been 
beyond orbital systems, given the limited number 
of satellites in orbit. The advent of commercial 
large constellations means these can now address 
much of the need for persistence, while digital 
downlinks offer the immediacy required in the 
tactical environment.
One attraction of a space-based architecture is 
a comparative lack of vulnerability when viewed 
against today’s airborne platforms. This is doubly 
apparent when considering non-LO crewed or 
uninhabited aircraft. Non-stealthy platforms are 
at considerable risk in any attempt to penetrate 
contested or highly contested airspace in a con-
flict.51 This does not necessarily obviate their use, 
but it requires the recognition that they would 
probably be lost within a few missions.
Greater stand-off, LO designs, or potentially 
mass numbers are three routes to addressing 
the use of ISR UAVs in contested airspace. Using 
extended-range sensors may allow UAVs to oper-
ate outside the missile-engagement zone of the 
main threat systems, but at the cost of limiting 
the area of coverage or leaving areas out of sensor 
coverage because of the topography. LO design 
will improve the UAV’s survivability in contested 
airspace, but this will be at increased development 
and acquisition cost compared to an equivalent 
non-LO UAV. Multiple small or mini-UAVs could 
provide an alternative in some cases where the 
required operational radius is limited, or where 
there is a means of transporting them to the area 
of interest in a timely and reliable fashion.  
Space-based ISR, however, is likely to be tar-
geted in a peer-on-peer war. Counter-space capa-
bilities are now a feature of the Chinese, Russian 
and US inventories; the satellite element of any 
ISR architecture is at potential risk.52 Ground and 
launch infrastructure presents further possible 
vulnerabilities as they are liable to be the focus of 
jamming and spoofing efforts. Space-based ISR, 
while offering great utility, is not a panacea, and 
therefore within-atmosphere ISR collectors will 
continue to have a significant – if changing – role.
Collaborative and almost here
An ‘exquisite’ class of UAV, often referred to as 
an uninhabited combat air vehicle (UCAV), has 
been on the apparent cusp of entering service for 
well over two decades. The promise, however, of 
this type of UAV has yet to be realised.53 While the 
term UCAV has sometimes been used to simply 
describe a UAV capable of carrying weapons, in 
this context the UCAV label is nearly meaning-
less since many – likely most – UAVs would now 
be UCAVs. A more restrictive and more valuable 
definition of this category of UAVs describes a 
LO, turbofan or turbojet-powered, comparatively 
high-speed air vehicle, designed with at least one 
internal weapons bay. 
Despite the potential value of introducing 
UCAVs into the inventory, there have been sev-
eral barriers to progress. Overestimating the 
maturity of the required technologies; under-
estimating development difficulties; differing 
interests, constituencies and priorities within air 
forces; and development and acquisition costs 
have all proved obstacles. 54 The USN’s Boeing 
MQ-25 Stingray uninhabited tanker is the closest a 
service has come to fielding an air vehicle with the 
characteristics outlined directly above.
The return of great-power competition and 
the renewed risk of peer-on-peer or near-peer war 
has acted as a further spur to their development. 
Initially viewed as having an air-to-surface role 
with the ability to be used to penetrate contested 
airspace, more recently there has been a keener 
interest, at least in the US, in using this class of LO 
platform in the air-to-air role. In keeping with the 
fungible language used to describe uninhabited 
systems, the now preferred term in the US is col-
laborative combat aircraft (CCA). In the UK it is 
=== PAGE 18 ===
INTRODUCTION
17UAVS: ISR, DETERRENCE AND WAR 
autonomous collaborative platform (ACP). France, 
Germany and Spain have adopted the term remote 
carrier (RC) within the Future Combat Air System/
Next-Generation Weapon System (SCAF/NGWS) 
programme.55 While the names may be different, a 
factor common to all is that they have a direct asso-
ciation with a crewed combat aircraft programme.
The USAF’s Boeing F-47 crewed element of 
its Next Generation Air Dominance family of 
systems has been designed to operate alongside 
uninhabited adjuncts from the outset. The same 
applies to the Italian, Japanese and British Global 
Combat Air Programme (GCAP) and the SCAF/
NGWS. However, this class of uninhabited sys-
tems will not have to wait on the entry into service 
of the next generation of crewed combat aircraft. 
The intent is to introduce some level of capability 
with crewed aircraft already in inventory, includ-
ing the Eurofighter Typhoon, Lockheed Martin 
F-35 Lightning II and Dassault Rafale.  
In the US, Anduril and General Atomics have 
been selected to develop CCAs for the next phase 
of the USAF’s so-called Increment 1, with the YFQ-
42A and YFQ-44A, respectively. 56 A production 
decision between the two is scheduled for Fiscal 
Year 2026. Increment 1 CCAs are projected to have 
a unit cost in the USD20 millions, with the more 
capable Increment 2 initially anticipated to cost in 
the low USD30 millions.57 However, as Increment 
2 requirements have developed, these have shifted 
at least in part to the lower end of the capability, 
with a likely concomitant reduction in cost.58  
The UK’s ACP Strategy identified three broad 
categories of air vehicle: Tier 1 systems were 
to be low-cost single or near-single use, Tier 2 
were described as attritable, and Tier 3 would be 
designed to have a high level of survivability, and 
as such be the most expensive.59
The SCAF/NGWS team is working with four 
categories of system: a ‘Loyal Wingman’ equiv-
alent broadly to the UK’s ACP Tier 3, and light, 
medium and heavy RCs. Some have perceived the 
‘Loyal Wingman’ as misleading in that it suggests 
– at least in lay language – air-platforms operat-
ing in close formation. Crewed aircraft and this 
uninhabited system will operate in concert, but 
most often with considerable separation. These 
distances could be from tens to the low hundreds 
of kilometres depending on the mission.
In the Asia-Pacific, Australia, India, Japan 
and South Korea are all pursuing CCA (or 
UCAV) class projects. Canberra’s efforts are the 
furthest advanced, with the Boeing Australia 
MQ-28 Ghost Bat. Eight test-and-evaluation 
MQ-28s have been built, with three Block 2 ini-
tial production-standard aircraft to be delivered 
by the end of 2025. 60 The USAF has also tested 
the MQ-28, with the USN paying attention to the 
project.61 Japan, a GCAP partner, is at an earlier 
stage in determining its CCA requirements, as 
are India and South Korea. 
The varieties, performance characteristics and 
capabilities of the uninhabited systems that will 
eventually emerge to meet the panoply of national 
requirements remain open. In terms of exquisite 
platforms, there will be a choice as to whether the 
design and signature-management treatment is 
for low-observability or very low-observability.

YFQ-42A production

=== PAGE 19 ===
CHAPTER  ONE
AN IISS STRATEGIC DOSSIER18
The latter has cost, design and potential perfor-
mance implications. Whether any of the exquisite 
platforms have a speed envelope that extends to 
the supersonic-flight regime will also only become 
apparent over time. Still, some countries are at 
least considering this option. Additionally, there 
will be a choice of whether a design is intended to 
be single- or multi-role. Again, if the latter, then 
this will have the same implications. A multi-
role CCA/ACP would have wider utility but be 
less expendable. 
Autonomous, after a fashion
The coming generation of uninhabited systems 
will make far greater use of, and have their 
mission sets built on, software algorithms and 
machine learning. A higher level of automation 
will be intended to reduce crewing requirements 
and allow multiple air vehicles to be ‘operated’ by 
a single mission manager. The latter could be in a 
ground station or an aircraft. The human role will 
likely be that of mission-tasking and oversight. 
The decision to use lethal force and the authori-
sation to engage a positively identified target for 
the moment remains in the human realm for at 
least some countries.62 Whether all belligerents in 
a peer-on-peer or near-peer war would adhere to 
such a restraint remains an open question.
In some cases, human intervention might be 
unnecessary. In the air domain, the use of CCAs 
as a fighter screen in a peer-on-peer war would 
almost certainly occur in an environment where 
only combatants’ aircraft were being operated. 
A mix of identification friend or foe (IFF), addi-
tional means of positive target identification and 
the broader recognised air picture could allow for 
a weapons-free environment where a CCA was 
authorised in advance to engage a target deemed 
to be legitimate. The air vehicle, or air vehicles, 
could be given a ‘kill box’ within which any track 
that was not positively identified as a friendly and 
that did not respond to IFF interrogation could 
be engaged. 
This could require that the CCA be fitted 
with a capable and costly onboard sensor suite, 
datalinks and a mission computer with a threat 
database for sensor-track identification – all of 
which would come at a cost. Alternatively, a 
crewed combat aircraft could be used as the sen-
sor platform, with target data handed off to the 
uninhabited system to facilitate an engagement, 
cutting the overall cost of the CCA.
Conclusion
The roles for uninhabited systems continue to 
expand, as do the size and shape of platforms. 
Addressing the need for ISR will, however, remain 
a constant in an otherwise changing picture, as 
will the place of ISR in helping to inform deter-
rence and to provide assurance at the national, 
bilateral, multilateral and alliance levels.  
There will continue to be a requirement for 
dedicated uninhabited ISR platforms, even with 
the widespread adoption of multi-role UAVs. In 
‘peacetime’, non-LO ISR-only UAVs offer a means 
of surveillance while signalling a minimal threat; 
in wartime, very LO UAVs provide a vehicle for 
penetrating highly defended airspace. The lat-
ter would provide a complementary capability 
to space-based ISR, while offering the assurance 
that, should space-based systems be compro-
mised or degraded, a backup remained in place.

23UAVS: ISR, DETERRENCE AND WAR 
Uninhabited Europe
CHAPTER TWO
Europe has long recognised the value 
of uninhabited aerial vehicles (UAVs) 
for the intelligence, surveillance and 
reconnaissance (ISR) role. It has, how-
ever,  generally  struggled to nurture 
domestic industrial programmes to 
meet its requirements for medium 
and large-size UAVs. The same could 
be said for similar ambitions for unin-
habited combat air systems, which 
are described by diff  erent countries as 
autonomous collaborative platforms 
(ACPs), collaborative combat aircraft 
(CCA) or remote carriers (RCs). 
European countries, except for 
Turkiye, have until now relied on buy-
ing in systems to meet their medium-
altitude long-endurance (MALE) UAV 
needs, often envisaged as an interim 
requirement, with the United States 
the main benefi  ciary. Numerous mul-
tilateral and bilateral eff  orts among 
European partners to develop ISR 
or multi-role MALE UAVs have 
unravelled over the past two dec-
ades. Meanwhile, the US and Israel 
have been able to step in to address 
European needs.
This chapter will consider 
European developments through 
the prism of French, German, 
Italian, Swedish, Turkish and United 
Kingdom programmes and aspira-
tions for ISR MALE UAVs and unin-
habited combat air systems. It will 
look at the numerous failures and 
limited successes and review options 
for improving progress in the realms 
of both ISR and uninhabited combat 
air systems.
The MALE realm 
All but one of the countries assessed 
in this chapter either fi  eld or aspire 
to fi  eld a MALE UAV. Of those, three 
(France, Italy, UK) already operate 
a US platform, the General Atomics 
MQ-9 Reaper, while Germany intends 
to purchase the MQ-9B Sky Guardian 
for maritime surveillance. NATO 
classifi  es a MALE UAV within its 
Class III category of uninhabited sys-
tems, that is, an air vehicle weighing 
greater than 600 kilograms. The sys-
tems considered in this section will 
fall broadly into this group.1
France, Italy and the UK all count 
variants of the MQ-9 in their respec-
tive inventories, while it is also being 
operated in the Netherlands, Poland 
and Spain. In August 2025, Denmark 
became an additional customer for 
the type. The MQ-9B was also being 
pitched, as of December 2025, to 
Norway – with Northrop Grumman’s 
MQ-4 Global Hawk also being consid-
ered by Oslo – and had been selected 
by the German navy. 
Like the US, European interest in 
UAVs was focused fi  rst on the ISR role. 
Unlike Washington, however, this 
KEY TAKEAWAYS
MALE FRUSTRATIONS 
Europe has long harboured ambitions to domestically 
develop medium-altitude long-endurance (MALE) 
uninhabited aerial vehicles (UAVs). Except for Turkiye, 
it has so far failed to do so, despite two decades of 
sporadic eﬀ  ort. The United States remains the default 
provider for European states looking to acquire oﬀ  -
the-shelf MALE UAVs.
TOO MANY COOKS 
Defence aerospace industrial fragmentation has 
hampered the development of European MALE UAVs, 
as has placing such UAV projects within broader 
defence-industrial collaborative frameworks.
DEMAND SIGNALS 
The use of uninhabited systems to collect 
intelligence, surveillance and reconnaissance will 
remain a key requirement and core capability for 
European operators. The demands of operating in 
a contested air environment, however, will require 
shifts in tactics and the addition of diﬀ  erent types of 
uninhabited platforms.
WHAT’S IN A NAME? 
Uninhabited combat air vehicles, irrespective of 
naming conventions, will likely be adopted by 
European air forces from the early 2030s.

UNINHABITED  EUROPE
25UAVS: ISR, DETERRENCE AND WAR 
was initially primarily at the tactical level for bat-
tlefield applications, often with artillery spotting 
as a core function. Many of the types fielded in 
the 1990s, however, suffered from poor reliabil-
ity, which contributed to the high accident and 
loss rate. The lack of reliability often coloured 
armed services’ views of UAVs and acted as a 
brake on more rapid and broader adoption.
The 1991 collapse of the Soviet Union and the 
resulting emergence of a more benign security 
environment in Europe brought with it a reduced 
emphasis on military capability required for 
peer-on-peer warfare. Instead, these ‘small wars’ 
of choice and counter-insurgency operations 
became the norm for the US and its allies rather 
than state-on-state warfare. The ISR requirement 
for such types of conflicts was to strongly influ-
ence MALE UAV research and development up 
to the 2010s.
The use of the General Atomics RQ-1 
Predator during the 1995 NATO intervention 
in the former Yugoslavia presaged two 
decades where the MALE UAV was to almost 
become synonymous in the public eye with 
Western military intervention in general and 
US-led counter-insurgency and counter-
terrorism campaigns in particular. Initially 
used only in the ISR role, from 2001 onward, 
use in Afghanistan meant that armed UAVs 
increasingly became the norm.
The French connection
During the 1990s, the French Armed Forces 
regularly used tactical UAVs for ISR roles. 
These included the SAGEM Crécerelle and the 
Canadair-Dornier CL-289, the latter produced 
in partnership with Canada and West Germany, 
although Paris would also purchase off the shelf 
in the export market to meet capability gaps. The
Crécerelle programme was begun in the early 
1990s with the SAGEM proposal selected in 1993 
to meet a battlefield-surveillance requirement. 
The Crécerelle was deployed at the brigade level, 
while the CL-289 was a division-level asset. 
The latter was a turbojet-powered system 
that could be flown at speeds of up to 500 miles 
per hour. If UAVs in the class of Crécerelle were 
dependent on their comparatively small size for 
survivability, the CL-289 relied on speed. Sensor 
limitations on both types meant they had to be 
flown relatively close to the areas of interest, 
exposing them to air-defence-missile engage-
ment zones. In the artillery-spotting role, UAVs 
would have been operated generally near the 
line of contact. The CL-289, however, would have 
been used to conduct high-speed dash flights to 
provide ISR in the deep.
In the UK, the Ministry of Defence’s (MoD) 
Phoenix programme was, like Crécerelle, intended 
to support battlefield target location for artillery 
A US Predator UAV armed 
with a missile sets oﬀ 
from its hangar at Bagram 
Air Base in Afghanistan, 
27 November 2009

=== PAGE 27 ===
CHAPTER  TWO
AN IISS STRATEGIC DOSSIER26
and multiple-launch rocket system batteries. The 
Phoenix was introduced into service in 1998 but 
retired only a decade later in 2008. 2 Out of an 
operational fleet of 89 airframes, 36 were either 
lost or damaged during British Army operations 
in Iraq from 2003 until the UAV’s retirement. 3
Phoenix’s successor, Watchkeeper, faired only mar-
ginally better, remaining in service from 2010 to 
2025, with a replacement capability now sought 
under Project Corvus.4
A medium-altitude UAV able to provide per-
sistent ISR, with an airframe endurance far greater 
than the likes of the Crécerelle or Phoenix, was still 
absent from the French, UK and other European 
inventories. Such a platform would also have to 
be able to carry multiple and more capable sensor 
types, not only small electro-optical or infrared 
line-scan systems. 
In the early 2000s, France and the UK moved 
to fill this capability gap with respective off-
the-shelf contracts. In 2001, the French Defence 
Procurement Agency, the Directorate General 
for Armaments (DGA), opted to acquire a ver-
sion of Israel Aerospace Industries (IAI)’s Heron 
MALE UAV under the Système Interimaire de 
Drone MALE (SIDM) programme. The SIDM 
contract was for three UAVs, with the first antic-
ipated to enter service in May 2003. Multiple 
issues, however, delayed the programme. A 
first flight in France took place only in 2006, 
and it entered service in 2008, a five-year delay. 5
The Israeli UAV datalink did not meet the 
French requirement, with work required to 
remedy this. 6 A new aerial module for the line-
of-sight datalink was developed, free from the US 
International Traffic in Arms Regulations. At the 
same time, integration problems with the satel-
lite link required additional flights to obtain the 
necessary qualifications.7
The Harfang MALE UAV, as the SIDM came 
to be known, was intended in part to provide a 
replacement capability to that offered by the 
Dassault Mirage IV aircraft in the ISR role until 
the type was retired in 2005. France ordered a 
fourth Harfang and a third ground control station 
in December 2009. The systems were used inten-
sively, with 5,000 combined flight hours during 
the deployment to Afghanistan between 2009 and 
2012, and the support to the intervention in Libya 
in 2011.8
In 2006 – one year after the Mirage IV’s retire-
ment – the UK’s Royal Air Force (RAF) retired 
the Canberra PR.9, a dedicated ISR aircraft, like 
France, without a direct-successor capability in 
place. By then, however, London had already 
been prompted to seek an interim ISR capability 
to support ongoing UK operations in Afghanistan. 
RAF personnel had been embedded with the US 
Air Force (USAF) at Creech Air Force Base in 
Nevada since 2004, gaining experience with the 
MQ-1 through the Combined Joint Predator Task 
Force. In 2006, the UK ordered two MQ-9s using 
the urgent operational requirement mechanism, 
with delivery in 2007.9
In the run-up to the retirement of the 
Canberra PR.9 London had begun to consider a

a Sperwer SDTI UAV at a 
French forward military 
base in eastern Afghanistan, 
18 December 2008

=== PAGE 28 ===
UNINHABITED  EUROPE
27UAVS: ISR, DETERRENCE AND WAR 
replacement as part of Project Dabinett. This pro-
ject was intended to consider how to collect ISR 
to support the RAF’s Future Offensive Air System 
(FOAS) programme, later to become its Deep and 
Persistent Offensive Capability (DPOC). FOAS 
was superseded by DPOC in 2005, with the lat-
ter then cancelled in 2010. Dabinett, in turn, was 
de-scoped and eventually renamed Solomon, 
with the Scavenger  requirement within Solomon
intended to provide a MALE UAV. The BAE 
Systems Mantis MALE UAV demonstrator was 
flown for the first time in 2009 and partly funded 
by the MoD. Aimed at the Scavenger programme, 
the Mantis instead fell prey to MoD funding con-
straints and was superseded by the Anglo-French 
Telemos project. The latter, however, would even-
tually fare no better than Mantis.
Common malaise
This symptomised the prolonged uncertainty 
over the ISR capabilities that were required and 
the extent to which these could be financed, at a 
time when the immediate focus and funding were 
on counter-insurgency operations. Nor was this 
difficulty limited to London; Paris was also grap-
pling with a similar problem set, as indeed was 
Berlin. ISR remained a critical requirement, and 
uninhabited systems were sure to play a central 
role in meeting this, but the devil remained in the 
MALE UAV definitional detail.  
Paris had begun to consider future ISR needs 
beyond Mirage IV no later than 2002, with what 
the DGA dubbed the EuroMALE project. A ten-
der was issued to EADS (now Airbus) in February 
2005, with France and Germany looking to coop-
erate on the programme. Initially, the concept was 
to build on the Harfang design and experience. 
Germany, however, was unenthusiastic about 
this approach and also set additional reconnais-
sance requirements. A 2006 French parliamentary 
report was critical of the design compromises 
being suggested, their operational impact and 
the growing costs.10 In June 2007, nonetheless, the 
DGA announced that France, Germany and Spain 
had agreed ‘on a technical arrangement for a risk 
reduction phase of an Advanced-UAV’. France 
and Germany would each buy six systems, and 
Spain three, totalling 45 air vehicles. The even-
tual EADS design, dubbed Talarion, continued to 
struggle for support, particularly from Paris. The 
DGA said Talarion did not meet the needs of the 
French military: there were issues with the indus-
trial workshare in the sensor production, and the 
estimated procurement costs were double that 
of a comparable MQ-9 acquisition. Without firm 
commitment from the three core partners, the 
project limped on until March 2012, when it was 
shelved.11 The EuroMALE had originally been 
intended to enter service that year. 
Rather than Berlin, Paris was now looking 
across the Channel for a partner to develop a 
MALE UAV. Following the 2010 Lancaster House 
agreement on defence cooperation, a joint MALE 
UAV programme office was established in 2011, 
followed by BAE Systems and Dassault setting 
up an industry team under the Telemos banner to 
address the Franco-British requirement. The pro-
ject, however, survived only a little over a year 
before being cancelled: the preference was to pur-
sue a joint uninhabited combat air vehicle rather 
than a MALE UAV, given available funding.
France once again turned to Germany for a 
MALE partner. In September 2012, the two agreed 
on a high-level common operational requirement 
for a MALE UAV, paving the way in May 2015 
for the launch of a two-year study phase of the 
project. France and Germany were joined by Italy 
and Spain, with the industry lead split between 
Spain’s Airbus, France’s Dassault and Italy’s 
Leonardo. The programme was focused on the 
ISR role, in no small part because of German polit-
ical sensitivity to arming UAVs.12
German MALEs
Berlin had similarly struggled in its efforts to 
acquire ISR UAVs. Like France and the UK, it 
envisaged a UAV replacing a crewed intelligence-
gathering platform, though in Germany’s case, 
this was to meet the need for a signals intelli-
gence (SIGINT) replacement for the Breguet 1150 
Atlantic. In 2007, Germany decided to acquire a 
version of the Northrop Grumman RQ-4 Global 
Hawk high-altitude long-endurance (HALE) 
UAV.13 The German version of the Global Hawk, 
featuring a domestic SIGINT suite, was named 
Euro Hawk and was based on the RQ-4B Global 
Hawk Block 20. 14 Certification issues, however, 
were to become an increasing headache from 
2011. The Euro Hawk was not certified for use in 
civilian airspace and, crucially, lacked an auto-
mated anti-collision system required for such cer-
tification. Cost and US classification issues only 
=== PAGE 29 ===
CHAPTER  TWO
AN IISS STRATEGIC DOSSIER28
worsened the problems, and in mid-2013, the 
German government pulled the plug on the Euro 
Hawk programme.15 Then, in 2014, Berlin looked 
to the MQ-4C Triton as an alternative, with an 
acquisition programme approved in 2017. 16 This 
effort fared no better than its predecessor, with 
the same cost and certification concerns lead-
ing to Germany cancelling the MQ-4C purchase 
in 2020.17
At the same time as apparently committing 
to the Euro Hawk HALE UAV, Berlin was also 
considering its own MALE UAV needs, with 
the MQ-9 and the Heron TP the final contenders. 
The latter design was chosen, though to expedite 
delivery, the less capable Heron 1 was ordered 
amid concerns over the deteriorating security sit-
uation in the north of Afghanistan, where German 
forces were deployed as part of the International 
Security Assistance Force. 18 In 2009 Germany 
leased three Heron 1 UAVs from IAI.19 The Heron
TP, however, remained an ambition, and in 2018 
a contract for five was signed, again using the 
lease model.20
The Heron remained only an interim solution 
for Germany. In 2014, then German defence min-
ister Ursula von der Leyen expressed her convic-
tion that the country had to participate in a joint 
European development programme.
Following the 2015 launch of the Eurodrone 
by France, Germany and Italy, political momen-
tum for the programme was sustained during 
a July 2017 ministerial meeting between French 
President Emmanuel Macron and then German 
chancellor Angela Merkel. The detailed declara-
tion endorsed Germany as the project lead and its 
preference for a twin-engine design. The meeting 
also agreed on cooperation on a next-generation 
combat aircraft in which France would take the lead.  
After negotiations over cost, participating 
states eventually signed a contract in November 
2020. Germany’s Airbus Defence and Space 
GmbH would manage the project, with Dassault 
Aviation, Leonardo and Airbus’s Spanish subsid-
iary as the main subcontractors. The agreement 
covered a EUR7 billion (worth USD8bn at the 
time) budget for the acquisition of 20 systems to 
include 60 air vehicles and 40 ground stations. 
France initially committed to four systems only. 
The programme had already been given a wider 
European stamp of approval when the pro-
ject was brought within the European Union’s 
Permanent Structured Cooperation (PESCO) 
defence-industrial cooperation architecture. The 
Czech Republic also joined the consortium, with 
the project known within PESCO as the MALE 
Remotely Piloted Air System.
In February 2022, Airbus and the Organisation 
for Joint Armament Cooperation signed a contract 
officially initiating the development phase. The 
contract confirmed a EUR7.1bn budget (worth 
USD7.6bn at the time) for the procurement of 
60 drones, with Airbus Spain, Dassault and 
Leonardo assigned as subcontractors.21  
Delay upon delay 
In the interim, the extended failure to secure 
a European MALE programme had forced 
Paris, while a staunch supporter of its national 
defence-industrial base, to buy American to meet 
pressing operational needs.
By 2010, France was considering an off-the-
shelf purchase to provide additional ISR for its 
forces in Afghanistan to supplement the Harfang.22
In mid-2013, France was reviewing buying either 
the MQ-9 or a variant of the IAI’s Heron. The MQ-9 
was, in turn, selected, with the first two air vehi-
cles delivered and used in Mali from late 2013.
The acquisition of a US system sparked debate 
in France, where the pursuit of ‘strategic auton-
omy’ remains a core tenet of its defence-industrial 
policy. Operational requirements and the lack of 
an acceptable European alternative trumped any 
political reservations. The repeated delay of the 
intended European replacement has since seen 
Paris push back the MQ-9’s out-of-service date. 
The Military Programming Law 2014–2019 
set the goal of fielding 12 MQ-9s by 2019. 23 The 
system was initially intended to remain in service 
until no later than 2025, when a European devel-
opment would replace it. Subsequent delays to 
the intended successor have meant the MQ-9s will 
remain in the French inventory, likely until the 
end of this decade at least. The French operation 
of the MQ-9 was also notable in that it marked the 
first armed UAV to be fielded by Paris. First used 
exclusively in the ISR role, in September 2017 
then-defence minister Florence Parly said the 
MALE UAVs would be fitted with weapons.
When launched in 2015, it was anticipated that 
the Eurodrone would enter service in 2025; this 
then slipped to 2028, and by 2025 the date had 
again shifted to 2031. Such were the delays to the 
Eurodrone that, by the middle of 2025, Paris was 
=== PAGE 30 ===
UNINHABITED  EUROPE
29UAVS: ISR, DETERRENCE AND WAR 
reconsidering its commitment to the programme, 
although it remained a partner in the programme 
as of the end of 2025. A French withdrawal would 
be a blow, possibly fatal, to yet another European 
MALE endeavour. A critical design review was 
supposed to be conducted in May 2025 but was 
not concluded until October.
Rather than being in a position to start intro-
ducing the Eurodrone into service in 2025, France 
was instead embarking on a national MALE UAV 
development programme. Five companies were 
chosen to produce demonstrator systems to be 
flown from 2026. The DGA in June 2025 signed 
contracts with AURA AERO, Daher, FLY-R, SE 
Aviation and Turgis Gaillard. The goal of the pro-
ject is to develop a long-endurance but compara-
tively low-cost system, with entry into service by 
2030. The programme is not being portrayed pub-
licly as an alternative to Eurodrone, but it is indic-
ative of the problems the latter continues to face.
A further problem, though not of Eurodrone’s 
creation and one faced by nearly all the current 
generation of MALE UAVs, is that of the return 
of contested airspace. Recent conflicts, including 
Moscow’s 2022 full-scale invasion of Ukraine, 
besides illustrating the utility of UAVs, have 
also highlighted their limitations when faced 
with credible air defence. None of the MALE 
UAVs in service in Europe, nor in development, 
have been designed with the requirement to 
survive in a highly contested air environment. 
Today, however, in a peer-threat environment, 
low- to medium-altitude airspace up to 40 kilo-
metres from the line of contact, as apparent in 
Ukraine, is a very high-threat environment where 
conventional MALE UAVs would be vulnerable.
The Eurodrone consortium, cognisant of this, 
has already begun to explore alternative roles for 
the UAV. These include airborne early warning, 
SIGINT, maritime patrol, being used as a stand-off 
weapons carrier, and operation as a counter one-
way attack UAV (OWA UAV) platform fitted with 
low-cost air-to-air missiles. Berlin, meanwhile, is 
using Heron TPs for new roles by stationing them 
in Germany and using them for surveillance mis-
sions in the framework of NATO’s Baltic Sentry
mission. MALE UAVs may be highly vulnerable 
in a high-threat or peer-on-peer war, but they 
continue to offer considerable utility in peacetime 
surveillance as well as in grey-zone or hybrid-
threat environments.24
MALE pattern failure
The Eurodrone is only the latest in a growing 
list of problematic European MALE UAV devel-
opments. Turkiye aside, the rest of the continent 
has yet to field a domestically nurtured pro-
gramme, and instead either Israel or, more often, 
the US has been required to fill the gap. The con-
tinent’s efforts to produce a home-grown MALE 
UAV capture many of the difficulties, often self-
inflicted, that European countries and their 
respective industries have faced and continue to 
A model of the Eurodrone 
is displayed prior to 
then German chancellor 
Olaf Scholz’s visit at the 
Airbus Defence and Space 
manufacturing plant in 
Manching, Germany, 
19 January 2024 
Photo by Johannes Simon 
via Getty Images
=== PAGE 31 ===
CHAPTER  TWO
AN IISS STRATEGIC DOSSIER30
face in cooperating on defence development and 
procurement. Reconciling military requirements 
with domestic political, industrial and economic 
imperatives is understandably difficult, but for 
MALE projects these have so far proved terminal.
Numbers alone do not tell the whole story, 
but they are a significant part. The USAF has, 
since the MQ-9 was brought into service in 
2007, purchased more than 300. The initial pro-
curement total for the three-nation EuroMALE 
was 45 airframes, while for the four-nation 
Eurodrone it was 60. Amortising research and 
development costs across a far smaller produc-
tion run inevitably places pressure on the unit 
cost, in turn making the product less attractive 
in the export arena. Multiple partners also bring 
with them differing requirements, bureaucra-
cies and budgetary cycles. Resolving the differ-
ences of the first can take time, while aligning 
the funding-approvals process across different 
finance and procurement departments is an 
additional challenge.
The ready availability of off-the-shelf options 
that meet operational, if not defence-industrial, 
requirements also remains a challenge. European 
programmes have, in the face of emerging tech-
nical difficulties or broader funding issues, been 
shelved in preference for proven MALE UAV sys-
tems from external providers. Operational needs 
are more rapidly met and at reduced risk, given 
that the systems being purchased are most often 
already in service with the domestic customer. 
The trade-off is that the purchaser’s national 
industry will have limited to no involvement in 
the programme.  
While ISR and UAV technologies are rec-
ognised as central to future military capability, 
they can sometimes lack a single-service cham-
pion. This is particularly the case where there are 
budgetary pressures, with each service inclined 
to protect its own key projects. This risk, further-
more, is greater when, from an operator’s per-
spective, a MALE UAV meeting its requirement 
could be imported and introduced into service 
more quickly.  
Development timescales have also become a 
hazard. The threat environment of 2025 is very 
different, and far more severe, than that of 2005 or 
even 2015, which is reflected in the forced changes 
to the Eurodrone mission. And this threat envi-
ronment is also influencing other key uninhabited 
developments in the air domain.
Uninhabited combat mass
Armed MALE UAVs are now the norm rather than 
the exception, though ISR often remains a funda-
mental role. Weaponisation has sometimes seen 
armed MALE UAVs described as uninhabited 
combat air vehicles (UCAVs); such a broadening 
of categories, however, reduces the description 
to being of little value. What it does capture, if 
inadvertently, is the malleable nature of the lan-
guage being used around uninhabited systems 
as research, development and designs change in 
response to the demands of sometimes rapidly 
shifting requirements.
In the US, the term UCAV has fallen out 
of favour, with CCA now preferred, while in 
Europe, ACP, RC and ‘Loyal Wingman’ are now 
more prevalent. All reflect a change in the roles 
foreseen for some classes of uninhabited systems, 
and how they will be operated. UCAVs were gen-
erally first viewed as providing an air-to-surface 
weapon platform being operated independently 
of crewed combat aircraft. This emphasis has now 
shifted in part to the air-to-air realm, with unin-
habited systems being operated in concert with 
crewed combat aircraft.
Combat mass, or the perceived lack thereof, 
has become a forcing factor in the shift in empha-
sis from the air-to-surface only to including 
the air-to-air role, not only in the US but also in 
Europe. Uninhabited combat air systems more 
broadly can contribute to both deterrence by 
denial and deterrence by punishment if acquired 
in credible numbers. In the case of deterrence by 
denial, the additional combat mass would help 
convince a potential aggressor that any attempt 
to initiate hostilities would fail to achieve its 
aims in the face of superior airpower. In the case 
of deterrence by punishment, the provision of 
additional airpower in the form of uninhabited 
systems would be aimed at supporting the abil-
ity to inflict wider damage on an aggressor as a 
means of dissuasion. Some of the systems now 
under consideration or development may also be 
able to be operated in highly defended airspace to 
gather ISR, with a benefit for deterrence. Europe 
has neither the space capabilities of the US nor 
dedicated low-observable (LO) ISR UAVs such as 
the Lockheed Martin RQ-170, all of which makes 
the use of CCA-type UAVs for ISR more attractive.
Uninhabited adjuncts are part of European 
next-generation combat-aircraft programmes, 
=== PAGE 32 ===
UNINHABITED  EUROPE
31UAVS: ISR, DETERRENCE AND WAR 
while they are also intended for use with the main 
combat-aircraft types now fielded. The Franco-
German Spanish Future Combat Air System/
Next-Generation Weapon System (SCAF/NGWS) 
and the Italian, Japanese and British Global 
Combat Air Programme (GCAP) both foresee 
their respective crewed combat-aircraft develop-
ments operating with uninhabited combat plat-
forms. The programmes, however, have so far 
adopted different approaches to the development 
of the uninhabited adjuncts. The SCAF/NGWS 
programme includes what it terms RCs as part 
of the tri-national programme, while GCAP until 
now has been focused only on a crewed combat 
aircraft. Each of the three partners is in parallel 
pursuing nationally what the UK terms as ACPs.
The European requirements have also gained 
the attention of US manufacturers: Anduril, 
Boeing, General Atomics and Kratos are all offer-
ing designs to meet at least near-term needs. 
Germany and the UK appear to be of particular 
interest. Anduril has teamed with Rheinmetall 
to offer Germany the YFQ-44A Fury, selected as 
part of the USAF’s CCA Increment One acquisi-
tion; Boeing is pitching the MQ-28 Ghost Bat to the 
UK, developed for the Royal Australian Air Force; 
General Atomics is pitching a version of the YFQ-
42, also part of the USAF’s Increment 1 acquisi-
tion; and Kratos is aiming to develop with Airbus 
a version of the former’s XQ-58A Valkyrie.
Demonstrate to vacillate
Europe has been exploring the development of 
UCAVs for over 25 years, though without any 
being introduced into service. At the end of the 
last century, Dassault began to explore the config-
urations suitable for a UCAV, while the UK was 
also embarking on research.25 Similar, if arguably 
smaller, efforts were also under way in Germany 
and Sweden.
In France, the LogiDuc or Aeronef Validation 
Experimental (AVE) project was aimed at demon-
strating UCAV-applicable technology. The AVE-
Discretion (D for stealthy), also known as the Petit 
Duc, was first flown in July 2000. This was a small 
subscale airframe demonstrator exploring LO 
characteristics.26 This was followed by the larger 
Moyen Duc, which weighed 500 kg compared to 
the Petit Duc’s 50 kg. The Moyen Duc was flown 
for the first time in November 2002, with a fully 
tail-less configuration of the same air vehicle 
flown in 2003.27
Dassault looked to leverage its LogiDuc work 
within the 2001 five-nation European Technology 
Acquisition Plan, which was intended to explore 
‘next generation strike platform technology’. 28
Dassault’s proposed Grand Duc air vehicle was 
intended to act as a demonstrator for a UCAV, but 
this effort was to be superseded by the multina-
tional nEUROn project.   
The nEUROn UCAV – a 
European programme led 
by France’s Dassault –

Istres, France
