CHAPTER  TWO
AN IISS STRATEGIC DOSSIER32
In 2003, Paris launched a further UCAV 
demonstrator project with the aim also of involv-
ing other European countries, the first of which 
was Sweden. By 2005, Greece, Italy, Spain and 
Switzerland had joined France and Sweden 
in the programme, now known as nEUROn. 29
Dassault Aviation was the prime contractor for 
the programme. 
Parallel to the French-led effort, London 
was pursuing a UK-only classified research pro-
gramme into LO uninhabited systems. Begun in 
1997 as part of wider future combat air studies, by 
2003, the UK had flown the BAE Systems Raven 
subscale UCAV demonstrator. This formed one 
of the building blocks for the Taranis programme,
formally launched in December 2006. Described 
by the then-Labour government as a ‘key deliver-
able of the [2005] Defence Industrial Strategy’, the 
document placed far greater emphasis on UCAV 
development in sustaining the defence-aerospace 
industry, and in meeting future airpower needs.30
Taranis was flown for the first time in August 
2013, while nEUROn’s debut flight had been 
nine months earlier on 1 December 2012. 31 Both 
projects, however, had to some extent been over-
taken by events. On 2 December 2010, France and 
the UK had inked the Lancaster House Treaties 
on defence and security cooperation. As part of 
the overarching cooperation, the two countries 
in 2012 signed a memorandum of understanding 
covering the initial phase of what was known as 
the Future Combat Air System (FCAS), deemed to 
be a UCAV.32 Six years later, however, the project 
was dead, the victim of diverging requirements, 
likely combined with France and Germany’s 2017 
decision to pursue future combat air require-
ments jointly and the UK’s 2016 decision to leave 
the EU.
In the early 2000s, Germany had pursued 
national research into UCAV-applicable tech-
nology, culminating in the EADS Barracuda
demonstrator, which was first flown in June 2006. 
Further to this, irrespective of exploratory discus-
sions in 2007 on jet-powered UAV development 
with Paris, Berlin remained outside the nEUROn 
programme and continued to pursue national-
only work. 33 This included the Low Observable 
UAV Testbed programme, begun in 2007 but only 
made public in 2019.34 The 2017 tie-up with France 
on SCAF/NGWS apportioned the lead on crewed 
combat aircraft to Dassault, with Airbus to lead on 
remote carriers supported by MBDA Germany.
A diﬀerent kind of uninhabited
The emergence of two European combat-aircraft 
development projects, the Franco-German–Spanish 
SCAF/NGWS and the British–Italian–Japanese 
GCAP, brought with them companion projects 
for a range of uninhabited systems. The former 
included the uninhabited systems within the over-
all project to provide a range of developments to 
meet partner requirements; the latter kept the 
uninhabited systems separate at the national level. 
Underlying all the European projects was the rec-
ognition of the need for additional combat mass at 
affordable levels to bolster the numbers of crewed 
combat aircraft.
The Franco-German–Spanish SCAF/NGWS 
encompassed RCs spanning from a large UCAV-
style air vehicle to smaller systems, some of the 
latter equating to what are now called OWA UAVs 
or OWA effectors. The largest RC was the purview 
of Airbus, with MBDA Germany the lead for the 
smaller expendable air vehicles.
Inherent tensions within the Franco-German–
Spanish programme, inevitable though they may 
be in a collaborative effort, as of December 2025, 
appeared to be threatening the integrity of the 
project. In July 2025, Dassault CEO Éric Trappier 
reportedly described dealing with industry part-
ner Airbus as ‘very, very difficult’.35 Furthermore, 
although a family of RCs was meant to be devel-
oped under the umbrella of SCAF/NGWS, in 
October 2024, France had also launched a national 
uninhabited combat air system, to be led by 
Dassault, building on the nEUROn programme. 
The outcome of this effort is intended to be fielded 
with the Rafale F5 by the middle of the next decade.
Berlin, while a partner in SCAF/NGWS, has 
also been exploring national requirements, with 
at least two nascent projects under consideration. 
The first, sometimes referred to as a fighter-bomber 
UAV, appears intended for the air-to-surface role. 
Options being explored as of mid-2025 included 
an off-the-shelf purchase of an existing platform to 
be introduced into service from 2029. The nascent 
requirement outside the SCAF/NGWS workstream 
is for a CCA-class system. In this case, domestic 
development will be considered alongside the pur-
chase of a foreign system. As a future Lockheed 
Martin F-35A Lightning II operator, the German air 
force may well consider US CCA options as the eas-
iest route to acquiring an uninhabited combat air 
system able to be tasked from the US crewed aircraft.
=== PAGE 34 ===
UNINHABITED  EUROPE
33UAVS: ISR, DETERRENCE AND WAR 
In keeping with Berlin and Paris, London is 
also looking to introduce an uninhabited combat 
air system in advance of the arrival of the Tempest 
crewed combat aircraft, as the UK refers to its 
GCAP aircraft, into front-line service in 2040. Like 
Germany, the UK is aiming to operate some of its 
uninhabited systems in concert with the F-35 and 
with the Eurofighter Typhoon from around the end 
of this decade. As with its European contemporar-
ies, the requirements and roles seen for uninhab-
ited combat air systems have shifted over time. 
Following the failure of the Anglo-French 
UCAV programme under the FCAS ban-
ner in 2018, the next year saw the RAF Rapid 
Capabilities Office start the Lightweight 
Affordable Novel Combat Aircraft (LANCA) 
project with a demonstrator air vehicle, Project 
Mosquito, at its heart.36 Work on the broad concept 
had been under way within the MoD’s Defence 
Science and Technology Laboratory since 2015. 
The demonstrator project was awarded to the 
then-Bombardier Northern Ireland site, teamed 
with Callen-Lenz and Northrop Grumman UK. 
LANCA, as the name suggests, was intended to 
focus in part on affordability. Mosquito, however, 
was cancelled in June 2022, with cost understood 
to have been an issue.37
Irrespective of the cancellation of Mosquito, 
related RAF work continued, including con-
cluding and finally publishing its ‘ Autonomous 
Collaborative Platform (ACP) Strategy’ in 
March 2024. 38 The RAF released the report a 
month after the MoD’s less substantial ‘Defence 
Drone Strategy’.39
Three classes of system were outlined in the 
ACP Strategy: Tier 1, disposable or single to few 
missions; Tier 2, more mission survivable, with 
acceptable losses; and Tier 3, systems designed 
for high survivability. 40 The first system to be 
brought into service following the strategy’s pub-
lication was the StormShroud, a small UAV fitted 
with Leonardo UK’s BriteStorm electronic-warfare 
package and intended to be used for the suppres-
sion of enemy air defence. Based on a Tekever 
AR3 airframe, the air vehicle’s modest speed and 
range performance will determine how it must 
be operated when supporting crewed combat-
aircraft packages.
In July 2025, the MoD issued what it termed 
a preliminary market engagement notice entitled 
Autonomous Collaborative Aircraft Tranche 2. 41
This was effectively a scoping document iden-
tifying four areas of further interest, covering 
electromagnetic-warfare effects, signature-
managed vehicles, one-way air vehicles, 
electromagnetic-warfare payloads and autonomy. 
Given the indicative funding of GBP25 million 
(worth USD32.2m at the time) and a two-year 
contract period, the work package appears to 
be exploratory. 
BAE Systems has been the industry lead on 
UK Ministry of Defence medium and large-size 
UAV research, much of it classified, from the early 
1990s. Adopting the ministry’s language, BAE 
Systems, as of late 2025, planned to fly an ACP 
demonstrator in 2026. The demonstrator may be a 
Tier 2-class air vehicle, including some signature-
management features.

Swedish and Turkish outlook
Sweden and Turkiye are also worthy of note, if for 
differing reasons. Stockholm has long explored the 
technology and application of medium to large 
uninhabited air systems, but has not yet pursued 
full development so far. Turkiye, on the other 
hand, has committed fully to a range of develop-
ments, some of which are already in widespread 
service, while others remain in trials.  
Sweden has never operated a MALE UAV, but 
in June 2025, Saab and General Atomics announced 
a potential collaboration to use the MQ-9B as the 
basis for an airborne early-warning platform. Saab 
is also working on its Ruby UAV demonstrator and 
is the industry lead on an initial two-year study for 
the Swedish Defence Ministry on future combat air 
requirements. The study, which concludes in 2026, 
considers crewed and uninhabited elements and 
will inform follow-on work that may include one 
or more demonstrator programmes. 
In keeping with US interest in the CCA as an 
air-to-air platform, Saab has also looked at this 
role. At the beginning of this decade, it began wind-
tunnel tests of a ‘Loyal Wingman’, a design capable, 
unusually for a CCA concept, of supersonic flight. 
Sweden, however, shares this interest in a 
supersonic uninhabited combat air platform with 
Turkiye. While the version of the Baykar Bayraktar
Kizilelma now being flight-tested is subsonic, the 
company has ambitions to develop a supersonic 
variant. Turkiye is arguably an outlier when it 
comes to Europe and UAVs, but it has nurtured 
a thriving domestic industry and has secured a 
large export market, most notably with the Baykar 
Bayraktar TB2.
Turkiye began to develop armed UAVs after 
observing US success with its systems, such as the 
MQ-1 Predator and MQ-9 Reaper, from the early 
2000s against non-state armed groups. As the US 
has maintained tight restrictions on the export of 
armed UAVs, Turkiye’s requests to procure those 
systems were rebuffed. Instead, at the 14 May 2004 
meeting of the Savunma Sanayii İcra Komitesi 
(Defense Industry Executive Committee), chaired 
by then-prime minister (now President) Recep 
Tayyip Erdoğan, several licensed-production pro-
grammes were cancelled in favour of pursuing 
indigenous designs.42 One of these was a MALE 
UAV for the Turkish Air Force, which became the 
Anka, with series production beginning in 2018. 
Later, the army would pursue its own programme 
from 2011, and it began receiving its first Bayraktar
TB2 UAVs in 2014, with exports starting in 2018. 
With over 500 TB2s built, Baykar is now Turkiye’s 
largest defence exporter. Both Baykar and Turkish 
Aerospace Industries (TUSAŞ) have also devel-
oped larger armed MALE UAVs, the Bayraktar 
Akıncı and the Aksungur respectively, and again 
both have been exported. 
Baykar describes the Kizilelma as a ‘Fighter 
UAV’, indicative of its potential air-to-air as well 
as an air-to-surface capability. TUSAŞ, meanwhile, 
has been working on a more common aerody-
namic design for an uninhabited combat air sys-
tem, a flying wing under the guise of its Anka III 
project. First flown in December 2023, in January 
2025, the first weapon release from the Anka III’s 
internal weapons bay was carried out.
Baykar has also reached a potentially far-
reaching agreement with Italy’s Leonardo on a 
50:50 joint venture, LBA Systems, with an initial 
focus on MALE UAVs. The Italian company will 
provide sensor and electronics packages for Baykar 
UAVs. Future cooperation could involve the likes of 
the Kizilelma.
MALE-related problems
Some of the issues that have often thwarted 
Europe’s MALE UAV ambitions are also apparent 
in the difficulties faced in developing uninhabited 
combat air systems. In considering what might be 
described as the classical UCAV, volume has again 
been a problem. The research and development 
costs of a high-end LO air vehicle and the associ-
ated subsystems, when amortised across compar-
atively low production numbers, risk a prohibitive 
unit cost. In terms of the Franco-British FCAS pro-
gramme, furthermore, the then threat environment 
offered limited near-term justification for the pur-
suit of such a project. 
As with MALE UAVs, protracted development 
timescales have also brought problems, notably the 
worsening security environment and associated 
shifting requirements. There has been a move away 
from the early-2000s notion of a UCAV in favour of 
a range of uninhabited platforms to be operated by 
or along with crewed combat aircraft. The notional 
roles of the UCAV are, in a sense, being disaggre-
gated across multiple platforms of differing capa-
bility and cost. At the same time, as explored in 
detail in the following chapter, lessons are being 
offered from Russia’s war of aggression in Ukraine.
=== PAGE 36 ===
UNINHABITED  EUROPE
35UAVS: ISR, DETERRENCE AND WAR 
This disaggregation has been accompanied in 
some areas by a blurring of categories, perhaps 
most notably between cruise missiles and UAVs. 
OWA UAVs in some respects fulfil the same role 
as land-attack cruise missiles, but at considerably 
lower unit cost. However, if Ukraine represents 
a template, the number of OWA UAVs required 
to successfully prosecute an engagement is far 
greater when faced with credible ground-based 
air defence.
Conclusion 
Deteriorating European security has created both 
greater requirements and more funding for unin-
habited systems, but the types needed and the 
force mix required are less certain. Uninhabited 
systems will continue to be used to provide ISR, 
but the survivability, or lack thereof, of the current 
generation of MALE UAV designs in a high-
threat environment poses questions. These can be 
addressed only in part by longer-range sensors, 
defensive aids, and changing tactics, techniques 
and procedures. A mix of signature-managed 
designs and far lower-cost attritable or near-
single-mission UAVs offers one approach, and 
this is already at least in part reflected in the 
uninhabited elements of European combat 
air programmes.
MALE UAV projects, however, are also 
emblematic of much that has in the past been 
wrong with multinational European programmes. 
Painfully slow procurement processes, political 
and industrial wrangling, and requirements drift 
have all contributed to programme failures. One 
of the many lessons offered by the war in Ukraine 
is the need for agility and adaptability that has so 
far often been absent.

39UAVS: ISR, DETERRENCE AND WAR 
The Uninhabited War 
in Ukraine
CHAPTER THREE
When Russia launched its full-scale 
invasion of Ukraine in 2022, both bel-
ligerents were operating uninhabited 
aerial vehicles (UAVs) in a broadly 
traditional manner, utilising lim-
ited inventories of medium-altitude 
long-endurance (MALE) systems and 
smaller tactical systems. Three years 
later, in what has become an att  ri-
tional fi  ght, the use and nature of the 
UAVs employed have transformed in 
both role and volume. What remains 
in question is whether UAV use in 
Russia’s war of aggression in Ukraine 
will remain sui generis , or the extent 
to which this confl  ict prefi  gures what 
may come.
Ukraine had fewer than 100 UAVs 
in its inventory on 24 February 2022. 
In contrast, it aimed to produce 4.5 
million in 2025, with Russian targets 
only marginally lower. 1 From their 
traditional roles as intelligence, sur-
veillance and reconnaissance (ISR) 
enablers and niche air-to-surface 
weapon delivery platforms, UAVs 
have become the most lethal type 
of system in the war. By 2025, they 
had surpassed artillery as the lead-
ing cause of front-line casualties, 
accounting for up to 80% of losses.2
The steep rise in UAV numbers 
used in Ukraine has, furthermore, pro-
vided an alternative, non-traditional 
approach to ISR collection. The 
extraordinary number – in the 
thousands – of small and mini-UAVs 
being operated concurrently pro-
vides voluminous data contributing 
to arguably an unprecedented level 
of situational awareness for both 
Russia and Ukraine. The small- and 
mini-UAV capacity to collect data in 
Ukraine is the gift of their sheer num-
ber: their individual ISR footprint is 
constrained only by their payload 
and performance limitations.
This approach has required the 
development of new command-
and-control (C2) tools capable of 
processing, integrating and exploit-
ing this data in near-real time. This, 
when combined with UAV-delivered 
ordnance, refl  ects elements of what 
was once dubbed network-centric 
warfare. Ukraine has, in eff  ect, used 
uninhabited systems to create a 
tactical reconnaissance-strike com-
plex, which is proving eff  ective on 
the batt  lefi  eld.
Initial stages
Both Russia and Ukraine had 
employed UAVs for ISR and limited 
ground-att  ack purposes in the period 
between Moscow’s illegal annexation 
of Crimea in 2014 and the full-scale 
Russian invasion eight years later. 
While the scale of UAV use during 
this period remained limited, par-
ticularly on the Ukrainian side, these 
KEY TAKEAWAYS
OPERATIONAL ENVIRONMENT 
Ukraine has provided a combat incubator for the 
operational development of uninhabited aerial 
vehicles (UAVs), with new classes of such vehicles 
emerging. First-person view and one-way attack UAVs 
have become ubiquitous in the war. 
DANGER CLOSE  
The present generation of medium-altitude long-
endurance UAVs has been shown to be vulnerable 
when operated within range of ground-based air 
defence. The extent to which this threat can be 
mitigated remains to be seen.  
WIRELESS PROBLEMS 
Radio frequency (RF) oﬀ  -board links, irrespective of the 
class of UAV, are a potential vulnerability in a contested 
electromagnetic environment, making jamming-
resistant RF links more essential. 
OBJECT LESSONS 
Russia’s war of aggression in Ukraine oﬀ  ers lessons 
for the uninhabited-systems realm, but the extent to 
which it more broadly represents a template for UAV 
use is debatable.  
The Leleka light UAV is employed by Ukrainian forces in the ISR role
Photo by Viacheslav Madiievskyi/Ukrinform/NurPhoto via Getty Images
=== PAGE 41 ===
CHAPTER  THREE
AN IISS STRATEGIC DOSSIER40
efforts laid the groundwork for developments 
to come. They served as early points of depar-
ture for many of the capabilities and operational 
practices that would later become central to 
the war.
Although now largely overlooked, the pre-
2022 conflict in the Donbas also featured a sig-
nificant third-party UAV operator in the form of 
the Organization for Security and Co-operation 
in Europe (OSCE) Special Monitoring Mission 
to Ukraine. Between 2014 and 2021, this OSCE 
mission conducted over 13,000 UAV flights, 
using the Schiebel Camcopter S-100 rotary-wing 
system and commercially available quadcopters 
to monitor the ceasefire. UAV-derived intelli-
gence played a central role in the mission, with 
58% of the observed violations that involved 
heavy weapons detected by uninhabited plat-
forms. The UAVs also provided high-quality 
imagery that improved analytical confidence 
and increased public awareness of the mission’s 
findings. Additionally, UAVs enabled intelli-
gence gathering in hazardous or inaccessible 
areas, extending operational reach while reduc-
ing risk to personnel. The contested nature of 
the environment was evident in the frequency of 
interference, with the mission’s UAVs reportedly 
jammed more than 1,600 times and fired upon 
on approximately 200 occasions. 3
Electronic warfare (EW) has been a con-
stant factor over the past decade and has been 
increasingly prevalent in the wake of Russia’s 
full-scale invasion. Following the annexation of 
Crimea, by 2015 Russia had already deployed in 
Ukraine EW systems capable of global position-
ing system (GPS) jamming. 4 These posed a chal-
lenge to Western UAVs, many of which had been 
designed for counter-insurgency operations and 
lacked resilience against high-end jamming envi-
ronments. Ukrainian forces praised the RQ-11B 
Raven for its high-quality sensors but found it 
nearly unusable near the front line due to its lim-
ited resistance to electronic interference.5
Whereas current UAV operations in Ukraine 
are dominated by small and mini-UAVs, 
Ukraine’s MALE UAVs played a notable role 
during the opening stages of the war. Ukraine 
appears to have acquired at least 20 Turkish 
Baykar Bayraktar TB2 MALE UAVs for its navy 
and air force before the conflict. Shortly after 
Russia’s full-scale invasion, Kyiv took fur-
ther deliveries of TB2s, with then-defence 
minister Oleksii Reznikov suggesting it might 
have received as many as 50 more in the first few 
months of the war.6
Russia began its campaign with few forward-
deployed ground-based air defence (GBAD) or 
EW capabilities, operating under the flawed 
assumption that there would be little Ukrainian 
resistance to its attack. Consequently, even 
the potentially vulnerable TB2s could be 
operated by Ukrainian forces at short range 
against ground targets. Russian air defences 
proved so ineffective initially that not only 
were TB2s not intercepted, they were used to 
attack Russian surface-to-air missile systems. 7
Ukraine also made use of the TB2’s potential 
for naval operations, targeting several Russian 
patrol boats. 8
Beyond its immediate military utility, the TB2 
also became a useful propaganda tool. Ukraine 
capitalised on high-resolution strike footage cap-
tured by the TB2’s Wescam sensors, releasing 
these videos to the public. 9 This tactic not only 
highlighted Ukraine’s defiance but also helped 
sustain domestic morale.
However, following the failure to secure a 
quick victory and the ensuing disarray, Russian 
forces succeeded in consolidating their air-
defence and EW systems, heightening consider-
ably the threat to TB2 operations. Open-source 
data suggests that Russian forces downed at least 
16 TB2s between March 2022 and April 2023. 10
This resulted in Ukraine gradually withdrawing 
it from front-line combat roles, with Ukrainian 
officials acknowledging the platform’s dimin-
ished utility. In response to the growing threat, 
TB2 employment was revised. TB2s began to be 
used for stand-off ISR since, under favourable 
atmospheric conditions, the TB2’s electro-optical 
and infrared sensors allowed it to operate up to 
50 kilometres behind the front line, well beyond 
the reach of Russian short-range air defence.11
In addition to Turkish MALE UAVs, Ukraine 
sought to acquire General Atomics MQ-1C Gray
Eagle UAVs as early as April 2022. 12 The United 
States government declined these requests. There 
were suggestions that the US decision was influ-
enced by concerns that the UAVs might be used 
to strike targets within Russian territory, poten-
tially leading to further escalation. Additionally, 
there were fears that some sensitive technologies 
could fall into Russian hands, risking technologi-
cal exploitation by an adversary.13
=== PAGE 42 ===
THE UNINHABITED  WAR IN UKRAINE
41UAVS: ISR, DETERRENCE AND WAR 
Russia, for its part, entered the war with 
a small arsenal of domestically developed 
Kronstadt Inokhodets MALE UAVs. Designed first 
as an ISR asset but later armed, the Inokhodets had 
previously been used in Syria. Russia’s Inokhodets 
was complemented by a small number of Iranian-
made Mohajer 6 UAVs equipped with Ghaem
glide bombs.14
Although Russia had fallen behind leaders 
like Israel, Turkiye and the US in UAV develop-
ment, it did launch the war with a more estab-
lished industry than Ukraine. Israeli technology 
transfers, agreed upon in 2010, had enabled the 
production of a local version of Israel Aerospace 
Industries’ Searcher Mk II tactical UAV, known 
in Russian service as the Forpost.15 In parallel, 
Russia had developed the Special Technology 
Center Orlan-10 tactical fixed-wing UAV, which 
entered widespread use alongside a range of 
smaller ISR platforms. Both the Orlan-10 and the 
Forpost were employed during the pre-2022 con-
flict in the Donbas primarily for artillery spotting 
and target acquisition. 16 While Russia was also 
experimenting with armed MALE UAVs such as 
the Inokhodets and loitering munitions, including 
the ZALA Lancet (testing these systems in Syria), 
Russian and separatist small-UAV activity in 
Ukraine during this pre-2022 period remained 
focused on ISR roles, with only limited evidence 
of armed quadcopter use.17
Much like Ukraine’s experience with the
Bayraktar TB2 UAVs, Russia’s Inokhodets and
Mojaher 6 proved vulnerable to Ukrainian 
air defence. Subsequently, neither has been 
employed over or near the front line for the most 
part.18 Moscow, however, did press the TB2 and 
the Inokhodets back into front-line service during 
Ukraine’s incursion into Russia’s Kursk oblast
in 2024. Several, unsurprisingly, were lost. 19
The rationale for the Russian use is not known, 
although some Russia-based commentators sug-
gested that Ukrainian air defence in the area was 
weak enough to allow for the Inokhodets deploy-
ment. Conversely, some Ukrainian observers saw 
the move as one of necessity as Russia attempted 
to slow the Ukrainian advance, even at the cost of 
UAV losses.20
Small advantage
Relatively early in the conflict, Ukraine began 
to supplement its MALE UAVs with far less 
expensive small and mini-UAVs. These were 
acquired by regular military formations, often 
through irregular channels, as well as by newly 
established specialist units, like the Aerorozvidka 
group, formed in 2014. 21 The primary focus of 
these UAV operations was ISR along the largely 
static front line, with artillery spotting emerging 
as a core mission. Ukrainian forces and Russian-
backed separatists, over time, also began to mod-
ify commercial UAVs to carry small munitions, 
marking the beginning of improvised air-to-
surface attack in the war.22
Most small UAVs in Ukrainian service were 
commercially available, such as the DJI Mavic fam-
ily, and were often bought directly by individual 
commanders or by units using their own funds. 
Even before 2022, however, Ukraine had begun 
to leverage its large and technically skilled work-
force to foster a growing number of small- and 
mini-UAV-focused companies. The innovative 
but fragmented and horizontally structured char-
acter of the industry, which continues to define 
Ukraine’s UAV sector, was already evident.23
Several private Ukrainian firms developed 
lightweight fixed-wing ISR platforms such as 
the DeViRo Leleka and the Athlon Avia Furia, 
both of which remain in operational use. Larger 
state-owned defence enterprises, including the 
Luch Design Bureau and Antonov, in contrast, 
presented concepts for MALE UAVs, but none 
advanced beyond the design phase.24
Ukraine’s state-led UAV development and 
procurement efforts were initially modest but 
gradually expanded in the years preceding 
the full-scale war. In 2016, the US provided 72 
Raven systems to Ukraine as part of a non-lethal-
assistance package. 25 Beginning in 2018, Ukraine 
ordered TB2s, with the first deliveries commenc-
ing in 2019 and additional batches being acquired 
shortly thereafter. 26 The TB2s were deployed by 
both the Ukrainian Air Force and the Ukrainian 
Navy, the latter indicating an early interest in 
using MALE UAVs in naval operations and 
using them as ISR platforms in conjunction with 
ground-based anti-ship missile systems. 27 In 
October 2021, Ukrainian TB2s went into action 
for the first time, striking a separatist artillery 
position in the Donbas.28
At least as significant, Ukraine also moved 
quickly to integrate UAV-based ISR into a flexible 
tactical battle management system. In 2016, the 
Aerorozvidka group initiated the development of

the Delta system, which sought to fuse multiple 
ISR sources, including UAVs, fixed CCTV installa-
tions and satellite imagery, into a real-time digital 
map.29 The aim was to provide comprehensive sit-
uational awareness to both rear-area commanders 
and front-line units. Development of the system 
reportedly received support from NATO, and 
small-scale testing began in 2017.30
Personal view
The war has become synonymous with the use 
of first-person view (FPV) UAVs, by far the most 
numerous on the battlefield. This concept emerged 
from commercial racing drones, and they are com-
paratively fast and manoeuvrable rotary-wing 
platforms equipped with a fixed forward-facing 
=== PAGE 44 ===
THE UNINHABITED  WAR IN UKRAINE
43UAVS: ISR, DETERRENCE AND WAR 
A UAV operator of the 42nd 
Mechanized Brigade’s 
Aerial Reconnaissance 
Unit in Lyptsi village, 
Kharkiv oblast, Ukraine, 
19 May 2024 
Photo by Y an Dobronosov/
Global Images Ukraine via 
Getty Images
camera that provides the operator with a real-time 
line-of-sight image. These systems are typically fit-
ted with small, adapted or purpose-designed war-
heads and are manually guided to the target. 
While more challenging to operate than 
standard quadcopters, FPV UAVs still require rel-
atively limited training when compared to tradi-
tional, larger UAVs. They also have low personnel 
requirements, being typically operated by a small 
crew consisting of a pilot, a navigator and a muni-
tions handler. Assembled locally from cheap, 
commercially available components, standard 
FPVs generally cost no more than a few hundred 
dollars each. In Ukraine, annual FPV production 
reportedly increased from 600,000 units in 2023 to 
2.2m in 2024, with further growth in 2025.31
Ukraine and Russia are not only scaling up 
FPV production capacity but also working on 
increasing their survivability and quality. This 
has included the introduction of fibre-optic cable-
guided FPV UAVs. Although less manoeuvra-
ble and more vulnerable to physical obstacles 
than FPVs that use radio links, they are immune 
to electromagnetic jamming and can transmit 
high-resolution video until the point of impact. 32
While Ukraine initially showed limited interest 
in this guidance method, Russia has adopted it at 
scale, reportedly resulting in significant Ukrainian 
materiel losses, including during the incursion 
into Kursk oblast.33
One-way journey 
While small UAVs, including FPVs, multicopters 
and fixed-wing ISR UAVs, have most visibly influ-
enced Ukraine’s battlefield at the tactical level, 
the widespread use of one-way attack (OWA) 
systems has also had an impact. Only months 
after February 2022, Russia began acquiring and 
using the Iranian Shahed 131 and Shahed 136 long-
range OWA UAVs. This purchase was soon fol-
lowed by an effort to establish local production 
of the Shahed 136 under the designation Geran-2. 
Since then, Moscow has committed substantial 
resources to scaling up domestic production. It 
expanded its original Geran production facility 
in Yelabuga and has established a second line in 
Izhevsk.34 These efforts have gradually reduced 
dependency on Iranian-supplied components. 
By mid-2025, Russia may have been producing as 
many as 170 Geran-class UAVs per day, enabling 
strike and decoy waves encompassing hundreds 
of UAVs.35
Moscow has continued to refine its OWA-
UAV tactics, techniques and procedures. These 
have included varying flight speeds and alti-
tudes, coordinated simultaneous approaches 
from multiple directions and, most notably, inte-
grating UAVs with land-attack cruise missiles in 
combined strikes.36 Russia is also making greater 
use of low-cost decoy UAVs, such as the Gerbera
=== PAGE 45 ===
CHAPTER  THREE
AN IISS STRATEGIC DOSSIER44
or Parodiya, which in some cases appear to con-
stitute more than half of the UAVs deployed in 
long-range strike waves. 37 Russia appears to be 
increasingly employing Geran UAVs in tactical 
and operational-level strikes. 38 Video evidence 
suggests that, in at least some cases, these UAVs 
are equipped with onboard cameras and radio 
links for direct operator control.39
Ukraine, for its part, began converting com-
mercially sourced fixed-wing UAVs such as the 
Chinese-made Mugin-5 into OWA platforms 
within the first months of the war. 40 Since then, 
these have been augmented by foreign-built sys-
tems, such as the US-made AEVEX Disruptor, 
and the YIHA-III and Dart 250, manufactured in 
Turkiye and the United Kingdom, respectively. At 
the same time, Ukrainian companies have made 
significant efforts to develop their own long-range 
OWA systems such as the UJ-26 Bober, the Destinus 
Lord, the Terminal Autonomy AQ 400 Scythe and 
the Antonov An-196 Lyutyy.41 Ukraine has also 
developed ultra-long-range UAVs by converting 
light sports aircraft into OWA UAVs. Examples 
include the A22 Foxbat and the Skyranger Nynja, 
with some of these systems reaching ranges of up 
to 3,000 km and being used against targets deep 
inside Russia, such as the Russian Geran factory 
in Yelabuga.42
Ukraine is also operating turbojet-powered 
systems that further blur the line between cruise 
missiles and OWA UAVs. Examples include 
the already in service Palianytsia, Peklo and the 
Destinus Ruta.43 Russia is also experimenting with 
jet-powered variants of the Geran.44 These systems 
offer improved speed and survivability but entail 
trade-offs, including reduced range and higher 
engine costs.
UAVs were employed at the start of the 
war to provide tactical ISR, collecting target-
ing information for weapons. OWA UAV mis-
sions reverse this relationship, with the UAV no 
longer serving as the collector but rather as the 
delivery mechanism. The use of OWA UAVs in 
Ukraine also requires extensive ISR to support

an attack. The reason, in part, lies in the com-
plexity of mission planning. Long-range systems 
such as the Lyutyy or Foxbat are not particularly 
survivable. Instead they are dependent on care-
fully designed flight paths and countermeasures 
to penetrate Russia’s layered air-defence and 
EW systems. These routes rely on detailed, near-
real-time intelligence about the location and sta-
tus of enemy defence. The challenge increases 
further when the intended targets are relocatable 
or mobile. Ukrainian OWA UAV operators, as a 
result, remain in close contact with intelligence 
teams and continue to receive near-real-time ISR 
updates until the moment of launch. 45  
Loitering munitions
Loitering munitions have been employed since the 
outset of the conflict. Russia’s Lancet was already 
in service, while Ukraine rapidly acquired foreign 
systems, including the Polish WB Group Warmate 
and the US-made AeroVironment Switchblade 300.

Since then, Ukraine has also made progress in the 
local development of such systems, for instance, 
the RAM II and the RAM-2X. Russia’s Lancet has 
demonstrated reasonable battlefield effectiveness, 
particularly against artillery systems. 46 Thus, 
Russia has expanded production and is steadily 
improving the system’s capabilities.47
The use of loitering munitions has increased, 
with up to 7,000 Lancets employed by Russian 
forces as of November 2024. 48 The figure, how-
ever, remains well below the volume of FPV UAVs 
used in the conflict. The disparity is likely due 
to the limited number of manufacturers and the 
higher unit cost of loitering munitions. A Lancet is 
estimated to cost approximately USD35,000, and a 
Switchblade around USD53,000, compared to a few 
hundred dollars for an FPV UAV.49
Originally designed to loiter over the battle-
field and wait for targets to appear, in practice 
they are too scarce and expensive to be used in 
this way in Ukraine. They are instead typically 
cued by a separate ISR platform and launched 
only once a target has been identified. Their 
onboard sensors and loitering capacity are then 
used to guide them to the pre-identified target 
rather than to acquire new targets.50
Ukraine and Russia are seeking to develop 
cheaper and simpler variants of loitering muni-
tions, sometimes referred to as fixed-wing FPV 
UAVs. An example is Russia’s Molniya UAV, a 
rudimentary system constructed from alumin-
ium tubing and plywood, incorporating low-
cost, commercially available components and 
operated in a manner similar to an FPV UAV. 51
Ukraine’s Terminal Autonomy is pursuing a simi-
lar approach with the AQ100 Bayonet, which has a 
reported unit cost of only about USD2,000.52
Autonomous activity
Swarms of autonomous UAVs may remain an 
ambition, but it is evident from the war that 
greater automation is a growing feature of 
uninhabited-system operations. One such ele-
ment widely employed by both Russian and 
Ukrainian forces is the automated target-
acquisition system, also known as an automated 
target lock. This allows an operator to desig-
nate a target, after which the UAV completes the 
approach independent of any further command 
input. The main benefit is the ability to maintain 
accuracy even if communication links are lost 
during the terminal phase, which can occur at low 
altitude due to ground interference or short-range 
jamming.53 While optical target lock has existed 
for decades, in the likes of the Raytheon AGM-65 
Maverick, improvements in software and minia-
turised hardware have made similar capabilities 
viable for small, low-cost UAVs.
More advanced automatic target recogni-
tion (ATR) has reportedly also been adopted by 
Ukraine using a system called Avengers, integrated 
with the Delta situational-awareness platform. 
Avengers is said to be capable of identifying up to 
70% of enemy vehicles and equipment in video 
feeds.54 The inclusion of ATR on a UAV would 
offer increased independence from a human oper-
ator. However, multiple challenges, technical and 
ethical, remain.
The integration of ATR directly into UAVs 
could enable fully autonomous strike operations. 
More advanced levels of automation, however, 
require greater onboard computing power and 
more expensive hardware. There also remain 
concerns over the delegation of lethal decision-
making to machines. Ukrainian officials have said 
the country remains committed to maintaining 
human control over such decisions, either through 
human-in-the-loop mechanisms or by restrict-
ing autonomous engagements to predefined 
geographic areas. In the context of the Russia–
Ukraine war, where both sides often employ 
similar types of equipment, fully autonomous tar-
geting systems would also carry a heightened risk 
of fratricide.55
The extent to which ATR has already been 
adopted by Russian or Ukrainian UAVs is difficult 
to determine. Operational security constraints, 
the inherent challenge of inferring software capa-
bilities from battlefield debris, and the broad 
application of the term ‘artificial intelligence’ to 
relatively simple automated functions, such as 
target locks, all obscure the picture. Nevertheless, 
certain indicators suggest that relevant capabil-
ities may be emerging. Russia’s V2U loitering 
munition, first documented in 2025, was found to 
contain a Jetson Orin Nano Developer Kit. 56 This 
advanced edge-computing module is optimised 
for artificial-intelligence and machine-learning 
applications and could support either ATR, visual 
navigation in global navigation satellite system 
(GNSS)-denied environments, or both. In the 
same year, a Jetson Orin unit was also identified 
in an experimental Shahed variant equipped with 
=== PAGE 48 ===
THE UNINHABITED  WAR IN UKRAINE
47UAVS: ISR, DETERRENCE AND WAR 
electro-optical sensors and reportedly delivered 
from Iran. 57 On the Ukrainian side, there have 
been claims that the GOGOL-M FPV mothership 
is capable of autonomous target identification and 
engagement, although confirmed technical details 
remain limited.58
Ukrainian officials have emphasised that 
autonomous swarms are unlikely to be realised 
soon given ongoing software and hardware chal-
lenges.59 Nevertheless, there are indications that 
some of the elements required for swarming might 
already be emerging on the battlefields of Ukraine. 
Both Russian Geran-2 OWA UAVs and Gerbera
decoy UAVs have reportedly been equipped with 
Chinese-made mesh network modems, which 
could allow them to act as repeaters and also 
enable communications among multiple UAVs. 60
Additional indications of Russian efforts to ena-
ble limited collaborative behaviour have emerged 
in relation to the V2U loitering munition. In one 
incident, multiple V2U UAVs reportedly entered 
a circular loitering pattern prior to engaging their 
target. Imagery of debris of V2Us involved in the 
attack showed distinctive, brightly coloured wing 
markings. These visual identifiers may have been 
intended to facilitate optical deconfliction or rela-
tive positional awareness among UAVs operating 
in close proximity, potentially enabling rudimen-
tary coordination.61
Integrating ISR
Delta has become a central element of Ukraine’s 
C2 architecture and has undergone considerable 
development since its introduction. The platform 
now includes a near-real-time digital map display-
ing the positions of enemy and friendly forces, a 
secure messaging service and a video-streaming 
feature known as DeltaTube, which provides live 
feeds from ISR UAVs and fixed cameras. In addition 
to data from UAVs and front-line observers, Delta
also incorporates ISR from a broad range of other 
sources. These include satellite imagery, inputs 
from supporting foreign governments and vetted 
reports from the Ukrainian civilian population. 62
The system’s modular architecture allows for new 
features to be integrated quickly, while its cloud-
based and platform-agnostic nature enables users to 
access it from a variety of devices, including stand-
ard web browsers. In combination with resilient, 
high-bandwidth and low-latency satellite internet 
services, such as Starlink,this allows for operational 
flexibility.63 Another feature of Delta is that it also 
makes a higher level of situational awareness avail-
able to lower ranks, thereby supporting faster and 
more decentralised decision-making. 
Traditionally, control of small UAVs has relied 
on a direct line-of-sight radio link, with only larger 
MALE UAVs equipped for beyond-line-of-sight

readying an Airlogix 
/f_ixed-wing ISR UAV for 
launch outside Kyiv, 9 
November 2022 
Photo by Paula Bronstein 
via Getty Images
=== PAGE 49 ===
CHAPTER  THREE
AN IISS STRATEGIC DOSSIER48
(BLOS) satellite communications (SATCOM). 
Both Ukrainian and Russian innovations, as well 
as wider technological advances, however, are 
enabling new types of BLOS communications that 
are viable for smaller platforms. One approach 
employed by both sides involved the use of air-
borne relays mounted on UAVs maintained 
within line of sight of a ground control station 
and another UAV, effectively repeating the signal. 
This allows loitering munitions and FPV drones 
to maintain stable control links even at very low 
altitudes, where ground clutter would other-
wise degrade signal quality, thereby improving 
accuracy by reducing the risk of link loss before 
impact. It also extends the effective communica-
tions range of these systems. Both sides have fur-
ther developed fixed-wing UAVs that function as 
FPV launch platforms and as airborne relays. This 
increases the communications and the effective 
range of the FPVs.64
Technological advances in satellite commu-
nications and satellite internet technology have 
also enabled their wider application in UAVs. 
Previously, high-bandwidth SATCOM for BLOS 
was only available to large MALE UAVs due to 
limitations posed by antenna size as well as sat-
ellite capacity. Now, low Earth orbit (LEO) con-
stellations, using beamforming technology like 
Starlink, provide affordable, high-bandwidth 
and low-latency connections via relatively com-
pact terminals. This enables not only their wide-
spread use for C2 and communication among 
UAV units, including through internet-based 
situational-awareness platforms like Delta, but 
also the integration of SATCOM capabilities into 
much smaller uninhabited systems.  
Ukraine has mounted Starlink terminals on 
heavy multicopter UAVs such as the Nemesis, 
allowing for their operational range to be limited 
by battery life rather than line-of-sight commu-
nications range. 65 Russian forces, in turn, have 
experimented with installing Starlink on fixed-
wing UAVs. At least one such modification was 
observed on a Shahed, although the modification 
does not appear to have been widely adopted. 66
While the use of compact SATCOM systems on 
aerial vehicles remains limited, these systems 
have already become essential to the operations of 
Ukrainian uninhabited surface vessels. As the size, 
cost and power consumption of LEO SATCOM 
continue to decrease, further integration into UAV 
operations is only expected to accelerate.
Both Russian and Ukrainian UAVs have 
made use of civilian long-term evolution and 
3G cellular infrastructure within enemy terri-
tory. Open-source reporting indicates that OWA 
UAVs have been equipped with cellular modems 
and SIM cards, enabling them to access local net-
works.67 The extent to which this practice has 
been adopted, as well as the role cellular connec-
tivity plays in navigation, remains unknown. It 
is unclear whether the mobile networks are used 
only for rough geolocation through the pinging 
of cell-phone towers, low-bandwidth transfers of 
basic telemetry data or more advanced datalinks 
that could support video feeds.
War in the electromagnetic spectrum
With UAVs now representing the single largest 
source of casualties, and Russia’s armed forces 
having maintained a long-standing focus on 
EW, it is unsurprising that Ukraine has argua-
bly become the most heavily contested electro-
magnetic environment in the world. Measures 
undertaken by Kyiv and Moscow to disrupt 
adversary UAV and other operations include jam-
ming and spoofing of GNSS, as well as jamming 
UAV datalinks. 
Navigation services such as GPS are often 
effectively unavailable near the front line. As a 
result, even advanced Western precision-guided 
weapons that rely on GPS, including the version 
of the Ground Launched Small Diameter Bomb 
initially delivered to Ukraine, have proven largely 
ineffective in contested areas. The same issue was 
reported with several Western UAV designs deliv-
ered to Ukraine. 68 In its efforts to harden UAVs 
against GNSS jamming and spoofing, Russia 
has made extensive use of controlled reception 
pattern antennas (CRPAs). These use multiple 
antenna elements to identify the direction of inter-
fering signals and filter them out, helping UAVs 
maintain access to satellite navigation. Initially, 
Russian Shahed-type UAVs used Iranian-made 
four-element Nasir CRPA modules, which were 
later replaced by the Russian-made four-element 
Kometa module. While jamming these systems 
is difficult, Ukraine appears to have made some 
progress. This is evidenced by reports of Shaheds 
being spoofed, crossing back into Russia or los-
ing signal, as well as by Russian modifications to 
Shaheds that now include more capable Chinese-
made 16-element CRPAs.69
=== PAGE 50 ===
THE UNINHABITED  WAR IN UKRAINE
49UAVS: ISR, DETERRENCE AND WAR 
Another approach to navigating in GNSS-
denied environments involves the use of non-
GNSS systems, particularly visual navigation 
systems. These compare inputs from electro-
optical sensors with stored reference imagery to 
estimate the UAV’s position. While similar nav-
igation modes, such as Digital Scene Matching 
Area Correlation, have been used for cruise-
missile guidance for decades, recent advances 
in hardware and software have made the devel-
opment and deployment of such systems signif-
icantly easier. This, in turn, has enabled more 
widespread use on smaller, low-cost platforms. 
Currently, these systems face challenges, includ-
ing uncertainties introduced by terrain changes 
and difficulties associated with low-level flights. 
Work to overcome these issues is undoubtedly 
ongoing, given the value of being able to operate 
in a GNSS-denied environment.
In addition to GNSS denial, the jamming of 
radio frequency (RF) datalinks has become a 
feature of the electronic battlespace in Ukraine. 
Jammers are now deployed at scale, includ-
ing fixed positions, portable infantry systems, 
vehicle-based platforms and airborne assets. The 
contest between measures to protect RF links and 
efforts to disrupt them has evolved into a contin-
uous cycle of adaptation and counter-adaptation. 
Innovation occurs at high speed, with technical 
updates and tactical responses often emerging 
within days. While software upgrades can tem-
porarily extend the life of existing UAV systems, 
hardware constraints impose hard limits. Once 
these are reached, platforms are withdrawn from 
service and replaced with new ones. This cycle 
drives the short operational lifespan of small 
UAVs such as FPVs.
The operational impact of jamming has put 
Russian EW systems among the top Ukrainian 
targeting priorities. It has also resulted in the need 
for complex deconfliction. Both Ukrainian and 
Russian UAVs often use overlapping frequencies, 
which increases the risk of fratricide through EW. 
Ukraine is developing software-based tools to 
manage this process, but challenges persist, par-
ticularly due to inconsistent coordination among 
separate units.70
Uninhabited building
Both the enormous quantities of UAVs used, as well 
as the rapid cycles of innovation and obsolescence, 
pose challenges to the broader development and 
production industry for these systems. Ukraine 
and Russia have taken differing approaches.
In Ukraine, research and development (R&D) 
and production are organised in a predominantly 
horizontal fashion, involving a wide range of 
state entities, private companies, start-ups, non-
profits and volunteer groups. The system quickly 
Attendees at an exhibition 
of Ukrainian company 
Kvertus’s electronic-
warfare systems in Lviv 
oblast, 28 May 2024 
Photo by Y ury Dyachyshyn/
AFP via Getty Images
=== PAGE 51 ===
CHAPTER  THREE
AN IISS STRATEGIC DOSSIER50
evolved in response to Russia’s full-scale inva-
sion, with only around 10% of companies cur-
rently active in UAV production having operated 
in this or related sectors before 2022. Ukraine’s 
decentralised approach to UAV development 
and manufacturing has strengths and disadvan-
tages. The Ukrainian UAV industrial pathway 
has demonstrated a high degree of adaptability 
and innovation, but its fragmented nature has 
resulted in reduced standardisation and greater 
variation in the quality of the systems produced 
compared to Russia.71
Russia’s UAV industry, by contrast, appears 
more vertically integrated and state-controlled. 
This allows Russia to scale up production more 
quickly and achieve greater levels of standard-
isation. This approach, however, is viewed by 
Ukrainians as constraining innovation. This 
disadvantage, however, is offset at least in part 
because innovations introduced by one side 
often can be copied by the other. Russia’s greater 
financial strength also allows it to make larger 
investments into experimentation, for example, 
fibre-optic cable-guided FPV development.72
The war has also revealed distinct R&D indus-
tries emerging for small FPV and multicopter 
UAVs, as well as larger OWA UAVs. In Ukraine, 
the first two are often produced by start-up com-
panies and volunteer initiatives; many of the 
more complex and larger OWA UAVs have been 
designed and manufactured by larger companies. 
These include enterprises dating back to Soviet 
times, like Antonov, which specialised in trans-
port aircraft. Whereas FPVs can become obsoles-
cent relatively quickly, large OWA UAVs are more 
like traditional platforms, whose design can be 
upgraded rather than replaced.73
Current levels of UAV mass production 
observed in the conflict would likely be unsus-
tainable without the supply of affordable, off-the-
shelf components, including microelectronics, 
motors and batteries. Both Ukraine and Russia face 
challenges in this area. Russia has been affected 
by Western sanctions aimed at restricting access 
to critical components. Ukraine, meanwhile, has 
faced limitations from China on the direct export 
of certain items. Both countries have sought to 
overcome such constraints through efforts to pro-
duce key subcomponents domestically and using 
intermediaries to acquire foreign parts.74
In Ukraine, the provision of a highly diverse 
range of foreign UAVs has added a further layer 
of complexity. While some systems have been 
effective in combat, others have proven to be of 
low quality or poorly suited to the operational 
environment. To address these issues, Ukraine 
established a formal testing and certification pro-
gramme for both domestic and foreign UAVs, 
required for entry into military service. At the 
same time, the intensity of the Ukrainian theatre 
Volunteers assembling 
FPV drones in Lviv, 
Ukraine, 29 February 2024 
Photo by Pavlo 
Palamarchuk/SOPA 
Images/LightRocket 
via Getty Images
=== PAGE 52 ===
THE UNINHABITED  WAR IN UKRAINE
51UAVS: ISR, DETERRENCE AND WAR 
has provided Western manufacturers with an 
opportunity to test their systems in combat and 
integrate Ukrainian feedback.
Precedent-setting?
A combination of factors has driven the rapid 
and widespread adoption of small UAVs along 
the front line. These include the availability of 
affordable UAV platforms and components on 
the global market, the relatively high state capac-
ity for procurement, production and deployment 
demonstrated by both Russia and Ukraine, and 
the positional stasis that has characterised much 
of the front line, prompting both sides to seek new 
tactical advantages.
The unprecedented scale of UAV use in 
Ukraine, including their ubiquitous use for ISR 
and strikes, has had a marked tactical impact. 
Broader implications, however, remain subject 
to debate, particularly concerning how far UAVs 
have fundamentally shaped the character of the 
conflict and what lessons other armed forces and 
organisations, including NATO, should draw 
from the Russian and Ukrainian experience.
Some observers have argued that the wide-
spread availability of affordable UAV-based ISR 
and strike assets has played a central role in turn-
ing the conflict into a static war of attrition. 75 The 
availability of ISR is such that it has resulted in 
what some analysts have described as a ‘transpar-
ent battlefield’. In such an environment, the con-
cealed or protected build-up of forces needed to 
achieve breakthroughs in heavily defended front 
lines has become extremely difficult to achieve.76
A further argument is that the widespread 
distribution of UAV-based reconnaissance and 
strike capabilities has rendered traditional mech-
anised warfare, long considered the primary 
method for exploiting breakthroughs in static 
defensive lines, increasingly challenging. 77 The 
mix of ISR and attack UAVs now deployed can 
be used to detect and engage armoured vehicles, 
with the former available in far greater numbers 
than the armoured platforms themselves. Russia’s 
response has been to abandon concentrations of 
armour for assaults in favour of more dispersed 
and survivable approaches, including small-unit 
infantry actions and motorcycle-mounted raids. 78
They have also fielded heavily protected vehicles 
known as ‘turtle tanks’, designed to withstand 
UAV attacks.79 None of these adaptations has so 
far reintroduced a meaningful degree of massed 
tactical mobility on the battlefield.
The cumulative effect of UAV use in Ukraine 
has led some analysts to draw comparisons with 
the early phases of the First World War, before 
the large-scale introduction of tanks, when the 
growing firepower produced an operational 
imbalance that favoured defence and resulted 
in a static battlefield. 80 The impact of low-cost 
UAVs, it has been argued, has been so profound 
that it could eventually render obsolete entire 
classes of legacy systems. 
This assessment typically refers to two 
dynamics. The first concerns platforms that have 
become increasingly vulnerable to UAV-based 
attacks, such as main battle tanks, which some 
argue are no longer survivable in an environ-
ment characterised by dispersed and persistent 
reconnaissance-strike capabilities. 81 The second 
relates to traditional strike systems, including 
artillery and crewed combat aircraft, that may 
struggle to remain relevant as UAVs offer compa-
rable effects at significantly lower cost and with 
greater flexibility. 82 A frequently cited example 
is the extreme cost asymmetry observed during 
Ukraine’s Operation Spiderweb, in which inex-
pensive uninhabited systems were used to attack 
expensive combat aircraft.
Others have cautioned against drawing erro-
neous conclusions from an incomplete picture of 
the war in Ukraine, given the partial and selective 
nature of available data. While videos of inexpen-
sive FPV drones destroying high-value systems 
often go viral, they can offer a distorted picture 
of a particular system’s effectiveness. FPV opera-
tors regularly emphasise the limitations of these 
systems, including low hit rates, modest destruc-
tive power, sensitivity to weather conditions, slow 
engagement timelines and vulnerability to EW. 
Operators have also noted that, in many cases, 
precise artillery fire, if available, would be faster, 
cheaper and more effective than employing FPVs.83
Similar uncertainties surround the use of 
OWA UAVs. The narrative of exceedingly cheap 
systems, sometimes made from materials like 
plywood, overcoming advanced air defences 
to strike critical facilities in Russia’s depth can 
appear compelling. At the same time, there is very 
little public, comprehensive and verified data on 
the actual cost–benefit balance of these attacks or 
how they compare to more advanced systems. 
Officials involved in OWA operations, much 
=== PAGE 53 ===
CHAPTER  THREE
AN IISS STRATEGIC DOSSIER52
like FPV operators, often stress the limitations 
of these weapons. These include small payloads, 
the inability to strike hardened targets, the need 
for complex mission planning and relatively low 
numbers reaching their targets. While FPV oper-
ators tend to contrast their systems with precise 
artillery fire, OWA operators more often compare 
their missions to cruise-missile strikes, noting 
that in many cases the use of advanced cruise 
missiles or a high–low mix of cruise missiles 
and cheap OWA platforms would likely prove 
more effective.84
Another caveat is that the war in Ukraine 
reflects a specific operational context shaped by 
the conditions of the theatre. In Ukraine, the rapid 
increase in small-UAV use only occurred after 
front lines had already been fortified, suggesting 
that UAVs may have reinforced an existing oper-
ational reality rather than creating one. Ukraine’s 
August 2024 incursion into Russia’s Kursk oblast, 
although ultimately unsuccessful in holding ter-
ritory, demonstrated that with careful planning, 
surprise and momentum remain feasible, even in 
a battlespace saturated by UAVs.85 Russia’s subse-
quent reconquest of the area further underlined 
this point. 
Some have also argued that the static nature of 
much of the front line reflects, at least in part, the 
strategic cultures of the forces involved. Despite 
extensive reform efforts, the Russian military 
reflects a Soviet legacy that favours rigidity and 
relatively static modes of operation. Ukraine, 
however, has made progress in adopting a more 
agile military culture that incorporates elements 
of mission command, but it too remains influ-
enced by aspects of the same inheritance. This 
stands in contrast to NATO’s strategic culture, 
which has traditionally placed a strong emphasis 
on mobility and would therefore require a funda-
mentally different approach to the employment 
of UAVs.86
Furthermore, this war involves two actors 
with industrial, financial, technological and oper-
ational limitations. These constraints have shaped 
how UAVs are employed and may have precluded 
alternative forms of UAV use from emerging or 
being tested in practice.
A case in point is the interplay between UAVs 
and crewed combat aircraft, or, more often, the 
lack thereof. Facing effective GBAD, neither 
Russia nor Ukraine has currently achieved air 
superiority. Consequently, both have relied on 
using UAVs to conduct strikes that might other-
wise be carried out by crewed combat aircraft. In 
this sense, UAVs have sometimes served as a sub-
stitute for traditional airpower. 
This distinction is illustrated by comparing 
Israel’s use of small UAVs against Iranian air 
defences in the opening phase of the Twelve-Day 
War in 2025 and Ukraine’s Operation Spiderweb
against Russian strategic bombers, conducted 
only weeks earlier. Although both operations fea-
tured similar technologies, their underlying logic 
was almost entirely reversed. Ukraine utilised 
such systems to compensate for the limited com-
bat radius of its crewed combat aircraft, thereby 
addressing its inability to use these to strike tar-
gets deep within Russia. Israel, by contrast, used 
UK Prime Minister Keir 
Starmer inspecting a 
Ukrainian UAV in Kyiv, 16 
January 2025  
Photo by Carl Court via 
Getty Images
=== PAGE 54 ===
THE UNINHABITED  WAR IN UKRAINE
53UAVS: ISR, DETERRENCE AND WAR 
similar systems to suppress enemy air defences to 
enable conventional air operations.
Limitations in the current Russia–Ukraine con-
flict also extend to the types and configurations 
of UAVs in use. Capabilities such as MALE UAVs 
equipped for persistent airborne early warning, 
long-range stand-off ISR using ground-moving 
target indicators, or maritime patrol might be of 
great value to both sides, but remain unavailable 
to either. The same also applies to capabilities that 
are still in development, such as collaborative 
combat aircraft or remote carriers operating in 
human–machine-teaming configurations. These 
systems are a focus of Western UAV development 
and, in large part, are intended to enhance sur-
vivability in contested environments, such as the 
one observed in Ukraine.87 Yet being neither oper-
ational nor available to either side, their wider 
operational impact remains untested.
Finally, the war in Ukraine represents a 
specific moment not only in the evolution 
of UAV technology but also in the evolution of 
counter-UAV technologies, as well as associated 
operational concepts. Even assuming the cur-
rent balance indeed favours the defender on the 
ground, thereby contributing to the emergence 
of static wars of attrition, this state might not 
persist. Governments, as well as industries, are 
investing in improved and novel counter-UAV 
capabilities, including directed-energy weapons, 
automated gun turrets and interceptor UAVs. 
Advances in these areas could restore greater 
freedom of movement for ground forces and 
better manage the threat to manoeuvre-based 
offensive operations.
Conclusion
The scale of the use of UAVs in the Russia–Ukraine 
war is unprecedented. This alone would mark 
the conflict’s significance regarding uninhabited 
systems. Further notable, however, was the mil-
itary adaptation exhibited by the combatants in 
the use of UAVs. FPV UAVs have been predom-
inant along a mainly static front line in a war of 
attrition, while OWA UAVs have also gained far 
greater prominence. Conversely, the use of the 
current generation of MALE UAV designs has 
been constrained by their vulnerability when 
operated within the engagement zone of GBAD 
systems. Changes in operation, defensive aids and 
increased sensor ranges will allow the threat to be 
managed to some extent. Still, in a highly con-
tested air environment, survivability will remain 
an issue if operated within range of air defence.
The prevalence of UAVs above and around the 
battlefield has led to increased attention on EW. 
The capacity to utilise the electromagnetic spec-
trum in a peer-on-peer fight will need to be con-
tinually defended, as will the ability to operate in 
a degraded or denied environment.
One constant in the war has been the demand 
for ISR, and UAVs will continue to offer a key 
capability in addressing such needs.

59UAVS: ISR, DETERRENCE AND WAR 
Uninhabited  Middle East
CHAPTER FOUR
The Middle East remains important 
when considering uninhabited aerial 
vehicle (UAV) development, acqui-
sition and operational experience. 
Several states are pursuing ambitious 
indigenous UAV development pro-
grammes covering multiple classes, 
ranging from small tactical intelli-
gence, surveillance and reconnais-
sance (ISR) to one-way att  ack (OWA) 
and medium-altitude long-endurance 
(MALE) UAVs. At the same time, off  -
the-shelf procurement from foreign 
suppliers continues to play a criti-
cal role for smaller states lacking a 
defence-industrial base as well as for 
states whose indigenous UAV devel-
opment programmes cannot yet satisfy 
national requirements, particularly for 
larger MALE platforms. Persistent 
geopolitical tensions in the region, 
combined with high defence expendi-
tures and a history of acquiring and 
operating modern military technology, 
all suggest that collaborative combat 
aircraft (CCA) and jet-powered UAVs 
could emerge as the next frontier in the 
regional UAV competition.
Decades of confl  ict have provided 
Middle Eastern armed forces with 
diverse combat experience. Many 
states have made heavy use of UAV 
platforms, particularly MALE UAVs, 
in counter-insurgency operations in 
relatively permissive environments, 
mirroring Western experiences in the 
use of such systems. The region has 
also seen UAV operations against 
more capable state actors in contested 
environments. These have produced 
both failures, for instance Iran’s UAV 
use against Israel in 2024 and 2025, 
as well as notable successes, such as 
Israeli combined operations against 
Syrian air defences in 1982 and 
against Iranian air defences and mis-
sile launchers in 2025.
Regional UAV development
Israel, Iran, Saudi Arabia and the 
United Arab Emirates (UAE) have all 
made indigenous UAV development a 
pillar of their military-industrial ambi-
tions. The outcomes, however, vary.
Israel remains the region’s most 
advanced manufacturer of UAVs. 
Active in the fi  eld since the 1970s, 
it pioneered the use of tactical ISR 
UAVs, was among the fi  rst countries 
to develop MALE UAVs and loitering 
munitions, and continues to advance 
capabilities such as networked small 
UAVs and potentially low-observable 
(LO) ISR platforms.
Iran can also be regarded as an 
early mover. Iranian development 
began in the 1980s during the Iran–
Iraq War. Its MALE and tactical sys-
tems generally remain inferior to 
American, Israeli or Turkish equiva-
lents, yet Tehran has been a pioneer in 
KEY TAKEAWAYS
REGIONAL ACTIVITY 
The Middle East was an early adopter of uninhabited 
aerial vehicles (UAVs) and remains a source of UAV 
innovation while also seeing continued UAV use. 
Alongside national inventories, the region was 
the /f_i  rst to see UAVs provided to and employed 
by non-state actors. 
NEIGHBOURHOOD WATCH 
Supporting intelligence, surveillance and 
reconnaissance remains an important role for 
uninhabited systems in the region.  
TEHRAN’S TRIALS 
Iran’s development of one-way attack UAVs heralded a 
wave of wider use in the region and beyond, while its 
provision of such systems to Russia and non-state actors 
has further raised the pro/f_i  le of this class of weapon.  
BUILDING CAPACITY 
While harbouring ambitions for greater sovereign 
development and production capability, the region 
continues to import UAV systems from a range of 
providers. However, acquisition in itself can include 
technology transfer.

long-range OWA UAVs, with the Shahed 136 estab-
lishing a widely copied model for this category.
Establishing a local UAV industry is a priority 
for the UAE and Saudi Arabia, even though both 
only recently entered the UAV field. The two are 
pursuing broad portfolios encompassing multi-
ple UAV classes, combining local development 
with extensive technology transfer from various 
sources. While smaller systems have entered ser-
vice, it remains uncertain whether they will be 
able to deliver more complex platforms.
Iran
The origins of Iranian UAV efforts can be traced 
back to the Iran–Iraq War, like many of Iran’s 
domestic arms development programmes. 
Confronted with limitations in tactical aerial 
reconnaissance provided by crewed fighter air-
craft, Iran sought alternative means of conduct-
ing airborne ISR. Initial efforts were driven by 
universities, military institutions and volunteer 
groups, and remained modest, consisting mainly 
of radio-controlled (RC) model aircraft fitted with 
analogue cameras and, in some cases, rocket-
propelled grenades. Only towards the end of 
the war did Iran begin to field more advanced 
designs equipped with datalinks capable of 
transmitting real-time video.1
Iran continued to develop UAVs after the war, 
focusing its efforts on Qods Aviation Industries 
and the Iran Aircraft Manufacturing Industrial 
Company (HESA). Early designs included the 
tactical Qods Mohajer 2 platform, as well as the 
smaller HESA Ababil 2 and Ababil T, which were 
produced in reconnaissance, aerial-target and 
loitering-munition variants. However, unlike 
Iran’s missile programme, which emerged as a 
central pillar of Iran’s national-security strategy 
following the Iran–Iraq War, UAVs remained a 
comparatively marginal capability, receiving nei-
ther the same level of funding nor political priori-
tisation as other types of systems.2
This began to change in the late 2000s and 
early 2010s, when UAVs became a focus of 
Iranian military development. According to 
Iranian officials, one driver for this change 
was the need to meet the ISR requirements of 
Iran’s expanding and increasingly accurate 
missile arsenal, which its limited space-based 
capabilities could not adequately support. At 
the same time, commanders within the Islamic 
Revolutionary Guards Corps (IRGC) Air Force 
grew increasingly interested in employing 
UAVs for combat operations. The systems avail-
able at the time, such as the Mohajer 2 and Ababil, 
proved insufficient for either role. 3
To acquire more capable UAVs, the IRGC 
became active in UAV design and production 
through Shahed Aviation Industries Group 
(SAIG), an organisation that had originally 
specialised in the refurbishment and modifica-
tion of light helicopters. In the late 2000s, SAIG 
reverse-engineered an Israeli Hermes 200 ISR 
UAV that had crash-landed in Iran, a project 
that eventually produced the Shahed 123. Buoyed 
by that success, SAIG scaled up and modified 
the design to produce the country’s first MALE 
UAV, the Shahed 129, which first flew in 2012. 4
The Shahed 129 was later fitted with a range of 
small air-launched munitions. After a US RQ-170 
crash-landed in Iran in 2011 under circumstances 
that remain not fully explained, SAIG introduced 
several smaller designs inspired by the RQ-170 
for both ISR and combat roles.5
UAV development activity also acceler-
ated among other producers subordinate to the 
Ministry of Defence and Armed Forces Logistics.  
In 2013, HESA unveiled the Fotros, a MALE plat-
form inspired by Israel Aerospace Industries’ 
(IAI) Heron, although it remains unclear whether 
the design was widely adopted. 6 The company 
also developed the Karrar, a simple jet-powered 
high-speed UAV derived from the Beechcraft 
MQM-107 Streaker, which was acquired before 
the 1979 revolution. Qods Aviation Industries 
began producing the Mohajer 6 light MALE in 
2018, a design that was subsequently adopted 
widely across Iran’s armed forces for both 
ISR and combat ISR roles. 7 In the 2020s, Qods 
Aviation and HESA introduced more advanced 
MALE designs in the form of the Mohajer 10 and 
the Ababil 5.
Iran’s success in building a domestic UAV 
development ecosystem has produced designs 
that have been widely adopted across multiple 
branches of the Iranian armed forces and have 
seen combat in various theatres. At the same 
time, Iran’s technological base and international 
isolation impose limitations on its ambitions 
for larger, more capable UAVs. Existing MALE 
designs, such as the Mohajer 6 and the Shahed 129, 
continue to rely on Austrian-built Rotax engines, 
procured through illicit supply chains and com-
plex transshipment networks. 8 Performance is 
=== PAGE 62 ===
UNINHABITED  MIDDLE EAST
61UAVS: ISR, DETERRENCE AND WAR 
also constrained by limited access to high-quality 
sensors. Also, unlike Turkiye and Israel, the coun-
try does not yet produce and operate more capa-
ble turboprop-powered MALE UAVs, although it 
has been testing such a system in the form of the 
Shahed 149 Gaza.9
In its UAV development, Iran has placed par-
ticular emphasis on the development of OWA 
UAVs and loitering munitions. Complementing 
more expensive missile systems, these relatively 
simple and affordable weapons have become 
a central element of Tehran’s asymmetric, or 
mosaic, military strategy. The result is a develop-
ment ecosystem in which state-owned conglom-
erates, as well as the research and development 
organisations of multiple military branches, have 
produced an array of competing designs.
Just as in Iran’s MALE UAV programme, SAIG 
played a central role in building Tehran’s mod-
ern OWA arsenal. In the mid-2010s, the group 
produced a copy of Israel’s Harpy anti-radiation 
UAV and equipped it with satellite navigation, 
dubbing the system Shahed 131. The design was 
subsequently scaled up and fitted with a more 
powerful engine, producing the longer-range 
Shahed 136.10 This established a pattern in which 
the IRGC, after successfully developing a UAV or 
cruise missile, proceeded to scale up the design 
for increased performance. The same iterative 
approach produced the lighter Shahed 101 and the 
heavier, longer-range Shahed 107. Apart from the 
development of UAVs themselves, Iranian compa-
nies, such as MADO, have also begun producing 
copies of high-performance UAV engines, such as 
the Limbach 550.11
Iran has exported OWA-UAV systems on a 
large scale. Recipients have included non-state 
actors in Iraq, Lebanon and Yemen that form part 
of Tehran’s ‘ Axis of Resistance’, and state actors 
such as Russia, which acquired Shahed 131 and 
Shahed 136 UAVs for use in its war in Ukraine. 
Tehran transferred not only finished airframes 
but also manufacturing know-how, enabling 
Russia to produce a copy of the Shahed 136 under 
the designation Geran-2.12 This transfer of technol-
ogy was helped by the versatility of Iran’s UAV 
arsenal. Whereas Russia mastered the more com-
plex Shahed 136 design, Ansarullah (Houthis), 
with more limited industrial capacity, was only 
able to localise production of simpler designs 
such as the Sammad.13
Beyond existing and widely deployed designs 
such as the Shahed 136, Iran also appears to be 
exploring more advanced OWA-UAV designs 
and concepts. The country has already put a 
high-speed, jet-powered variant of the Shahed 136, 
named Shahed 238, into service. 14 Iranian market-
ing material presented to Russia and subsequently 
leaked to the public also details more refined tac-
tical OWA-UAV concepts, including loitering var-
iants of the Shahed 136 and Shahed 101 operating 
in conjunction with airborne relays. There is also 
some evidence to suggest that Tehran is work-
ing on jamming-resistant navigation systems, 
including visual navigation. In 2025, an Iranian-
made Shahed, which was likely combat-tested in

Ukraine, was reported to carry an NVIDIA Jetson

Israel 
Israel has been a pioneer of UAV development 
and remains a leader in this field. Its success in 
developing UAVs in a variety of classes, includ-
ing tactical ISR platforms, small UAVs, loiter-
ing munitions and MALE UAVs, has not only 
equipped the country with a capable UAV arsenal 
but also enabled significant export successes. 
Israel’s interest in UAVs can be traced back 
to its confrontation with Egypt over Sinai in the 
early 1970s. Egypt had amassed not only a sig-
nificant ground force but also a dense network of 
surface-to-air missiles. The latter sparked Israeli 
interest in decoy UAVs that could be used to dis-
tract Egyptian air defences. The country acquired 
Northrop BQM-74 Chukar and Ryan Firebee target 
drones to be used as decoys.16
Beyond decoys, the situation also required 
new forms of ISR relaying live video feed, both 
to spot troop movements across the Suez Canal 
and to locate mobile air-defence systems as part of 
the suppression/destruction of enemy air defence 
(SEAD/DEAD) kill chain. By the late 1970s, Israel 
had developed and fielded two such systems 
– the Tadiran Mastiff and IAI’s Scout, effectively 
creating the first tactical ISR UAVs. The positive 
results of the systems’ use during the 1982 Israeli 
invasion of Lebanon generated both international 
interest and the first export agreements, with the 
United States’ Navy adopting the Mastiff and a 
customised version of the Scout under the desig-
nation RQ-2 Pioneer.17
Continued Israeli interest in UAV use for 
SEAD/DEAD missions also led to the adoption of 
loitering munitions and OWA UAVs. A German 
anti-radiation OWA UAV called Drohne Anti-
Radar was eventually produced by IAI under 
the designation Harpy. Apart from incremental 
improvements to the Harpy, IAI also developed 
a loitering version of the system, equipped with 
a datalink and electro-optical sensors, under the 
designation Harop.18
By the early 1990s, IAI decided to leverage its 
experience with the Scout to design a much larger 
and longer-endurance platform. Work on the 
Heron began in 1993, with the first flight report-
edly occurring in 1994. Thus, Israel, in parallel 
with the US, which was working on the General 
Atomics Predator at the same time, became one of 
the first producers of modern MALE UAVs.19 The 
Heron itself underwent further upgrades and new 
configurations, including new sensor suites such 
as signals intelligence payloads and maritime-
patrol radars, as well as beyond-line-of-sight 
(BLOS) satellite communications. IAI also devel-
oped a more powerful turboprop-powered suc-
cessor model named Eitan/Heron TP that first flew 
in 2004 and appears to have achieved initial oper-
ational capability around the time of Operation
Cast Lead in 2008–09. 20 The company has started 
advertising the Tactical Heron, comparable to light 
tactical MALE systems such as the Bayraktar TB2 
and the Hermes 450.21
Technicians from the Israel 
Defense Forces working 
on a Heron MALE UAV at 
Palmachim Air Base in
