UNINHABITED  MIDDLE EAST
63UAVS: ISR, DETERRENCE AND WAR 
In parallel with IAI’s activities, Elbit also 
began to become active in the field. In the 1990s, 
the company developed the Hermes 450 UAV 
(known as Zik in Israeli service), designed to 
provide ISR on the division and corps level. 22
The system was later complemented by the large 
piston-engine Hermes 900 MALE. Currently, the 
company is working on the Hermes 650 Spark as 
a successor to the Hermes 450.23
For many years, the MALE UAV market was 
characterised by a limited number of suppli-
ers, with only China, Israel and the US fielding 
mature designs. The US restricted exports due to 
its adherence to the Missile Technology Control 
Regime (MTCR), while Chinese systems faced 
concerns over quality and political sensitivity. 
This benefitted Israel, which was not an MTCR 
signatory, enabling it to achieve export success 
by selling and leasing Hermes and Heron UAVs 
to customers in Africa, Asia, Europe and Latin 
America. Only with the recent emergence of 
Turkiye as a major UAV producer, and the United 
States’ relaxation of its MTCR interpretation, has 
the market become more competitive again. 
Israel appears to have armed its MALE UAVs 
from early on, though less detail exists on Israeli 
armament options than on US or Turkish systems. 
Long treated as a classified but publicly well-
known capability, the Israel Defense Forces (IDF) 
only formally acknowledged the use of armed 
MALE UAVs in 2022.24 Post-strike remnants indi-
cate that a small glide bomb, Mikholit, is among the 
most frequently recovered munitions, although 
it is likely that additional munitions have been 
developed for use by MALE platforms.25 Imagery 
of Hermes 450 UAVs equipped with underwing 
munition containers suggests that the type is rou-
tinely employed for strike missions.
Apart from further refinement of MALE 
capabilities, in the 2010s and 2020s, Israel also 
developed small UAVs, including quadcopters, 
fixed-wing small ISR UAVs and loitering muni-
tions. This included not only well-established 
players like Elbit and IAI, but also new private 
companies such as UVision, Aeronautics and Blue 
Bird Aero Systems. Systems such as Aeronautics’ 
Orbiter 2 ISR UAV or the UVision Hero series of 
loitering munitions managed to achieve export 
success and were widely used as a template for 
comparable programmes in other states.
Although details on UAV development pro-
grammes are often classified, there is evidence 
suggesting that Israel is working on new UAV 
concepts as well as improving existing ones. 
One case in point is Aeronautics’ Orbiter 4 and 
5 systems, which represent an attempt at minia-
turising traditional MALE capabilities. With a 
take-off weight of only 75 kilograms, the Orbiter
5 is claimed to have an endurance of up to 25 
hours and is capable of carrying electro-optical/
infrared, electronic intelligence, electronic war-
fare (EW), communications and maritime radar 
payloads.26 Upon the introduction of the Orbiter-
related Nitzoz system into service in 2022, the IDF 
described it as a gateway to the fifth generation 
of UAVs.27 The service also stated that the 144th 
UAV squadron operating the UAV was part of the 
Storm Cloud project, which other reporting has 
described as aimed at integrating collaborative 
UAVs into the Israeli kill chain. 28 In 2021, press 
reports had claimed that Israel’s experimental 
Ghost Unit had deployed networked swarms of 
UAVs over Gaza to detect Hamas and Palestinian 
Islamic Jihad missile launchers, although details 
remain scant.29
Similar to other countries, Israel is also pur-
suing the integration of advanced automation 
and artificial intelligence (AI) into its UAV fleet, 
enabling both onboard (edge) and ground-based 
data processing. While much of this work remains 
classified, press leaks related to the war in Gaza 
have provided some insights into Israeli off-
board processing programmes, which, though 
not exclusively focused on UAVs, almost certainly 
incorporated UAV-derived ISR data and were 
very likely employed to support target selection 
for UAV strikes. According to press reports, Israel 
operationally deployed at least three such pro-
grammes: ‘Lavender’, used for identifying indi-
viduals assessed to be members of armed groups; 
‘Gospel’, designed to determine structures of 
concern; and ‘Where’s Daddy?’, used for track-
ing mobile phones. 30 While these systems were 
designed with a human in the loop, they have 
nevertheless raised ethical concerns and ques-
tions about what constitutes meaningful human 
control.31 Additionally, Israel was reported to 
have employed AI-enabled systems to integrate 
data from multiple sources, including UAVs, to 
generate comprehensive situational awareness.32
Various Israeli companies are also working 
on integrating advanced forms of onboard pro-
cessing directly with UAVs. Loitering munitions 
such as the Hero series are already equipped with 
=== PAGE 65 ===
CHAPTER  FOUR
AN IISS STRATEGIC DOSSIER64
target-lock and tracking mechanisms.33 New soft-
ware firms are also offering additional solutions. 
For example, Axon Vision has developed the 
EdgeUAV system, which includes automatic tar-
get recognition, multi-object tracking and video 
motion detection. 34 The company has already 
signed contracts to provide the system to an 
unnamed producer of loitering munitions.35 Other 
Israeli firms, such as Sightec, are developing 
visual-navigation systems for use in global nav-
igation satellite system-denied environments. 36
Further activities related to AI use for UAVs as 
well as work on more autonomous systems are 
likely to be taking place in the classified realm.
Saudi Arabia
Domestic UAV development and localisation 
of mature foreign UAV technology remains 
fragmented across Saudi Arabia, and includes 
state-owned enterprises, private companies 
and university-affiliated research centres. This 
diverse domestic landscape is mirrored by the 
multiple sources of UAV technology that Saudi 
Arabia has attempted to acquire, with planned, 
completed or failed transfer of technology from 
Belarus, China, Germany, Turkiye and the US. 
The opaque nature of Saudi Arabia’s defence 
sector makes it difficult to gauge the success or 
current status of most projects, several of which 
have faced lengthy development delays. On occa-
sion, foreign-made UAVs are presented as Saudi 
designs, without clarity on whether technology 
transfer actually occurred.
Nonetheless, signs of progress are emerging. 
The Prince Sultan Defense Studies and Research 
Center, associated with King Saud University and 
the Royal Saudi Air Force, has developed the tac-
tical SkyGuard UAV, reportedly ready for produc-
tion. It is also building a prototype of the larger 
Qaswaa MALE UAV, with a maiden flight planned 
for late 2025, and is designing the smaller vertical 
take-off and landing Shaheen, which is currently 
in preliminary stages. Laser-guided munitions for 
these UAVs are also under development.37
King Abdulaziz City for Science and 
Technology (KACST) has collaborated with 
UAVOS, which appears to be related to the 
Belarusian company Kvand IS, to develop the 
Saqr UAV family. The Saqr-1, a MALE UAV, 
was unveiled in 2017, followed by the Saqr-
1B.38 Although the Saqr-1C, a third variant, was 
announced in 2020 and development report-
edly continued through 2023, none appear to 
have entered service. 39 The Saqr largely reflects 
a UAVOS design, with some limited input from 
KACST. The two have also worked on the Albatross
UAV.40 In parallel, KACST partnered with China 
Aerospace Science and Technology Corporation 
in 2017 to establish a UAV-manufacturing plant 
in Saudi Arabia, but this initiative seemingly 
never materialised.41
INTRA Defense Technologies (INTRA), a 
new Saudi defence company, inaugurated a fac-
tory in Riyadh in November 2025 with a claimed 
annual production capacity of 120 aircraft.42 It has 
also operated the Turkish Karayel for the Saudi 
A Saudi Saqr-1 MALE UAV 
on display at the Saudi 
Air Show in Riyadh, 12 
March 2019 
Photo by Ryan Olson/
Bloomberg via Getty Images
=== PAGE 66 ===
UNINHABITED  MIDDLE EAST
65UAVS: ISR, DETERRENCE AND WAR 
military, including its use over Yemen. Reports 
have surfaced about INTRA developing a local-
ised version of the Karayel called Haboob, although 
it is unclear if it has entered production. Some 
sources claim Saudi Arabia began licensed manu-
facturing of the Haboob around 2021, while others 
say INTRA ultimately abandoned the project. 43
Indeed, while INTRA’s website does not list the 
Haboob among its products, a recent video shows 
technicians working on what appears to be a 
Haboob fuselage at its facilities.44
INTRA’s flagship project is the Samoom, a 
MALE twin-engine UAV with 40-hour endur-
ance, developed by leveraging experience from 
operating the Karayel with the Royal Saudi Air 
Force and Navy. Work on the prototype began in 
2021, with a mock-up presented in 2022 and a full-
scale version showcased in 2024. INTRA hoped 
the Samoom would enter Saudi service by late 
2025, and while no contract has been announced, 
it is probable that if successful the UAV 
would be acquired by the Saudi armed forces. 45

CHAPTER  FOUR
AN IISS STRATEGIC DOSSIER66
While INTRA claims Saudi engineers exclusively 
developed the UAV, it is a result of international 
collaboration: Hensoldt helped develop a gimbal, 
Brazilian engineering firm Akaer contributed to 
design, and Belgium’s ULPower Aero Engines 
supplied the piston engines.46
Notably, the Saudi national defence cham-
pion, SAMI, does not have an indigenous MALE 
UAV programme, unlike its Emirati counterpart, 
EDGE. In 2024, SAMI displayed a prototype of 
Baz, a light surveillance UAV developed to ful-
fil a Saudi military requirement, along with a 
LO uninhabited combat aerial vehicle (UCAV) 
mock-up, though no development timeline was 
announced. SAMI’s real initiation into UAV 
manufacturing is likely to come through the 
co-production of Turkish Akıncı UAVs. In 2023, 
Saudi Arabia signed a multibillion-dollar con-
tract with Baykar to acquire dozens of these 
UAVs, with plans to localise up to 70% of pro-
duction. This includes manufacturing the UAV’s 
structure, electrical systems, ground equipment, 
final assembly, systems integration, flight testing 
and maintenance. 47
SAMI will lead the localisation efforts, hav-
ing signed a memorandum of understanding 
(MoU) with Baykar. Meanwhile Saudi company 
NCMS has signed MoUs with other Turkish com-
panies, Aselsan and Roketsan, to localise electro-
optical systems and smart munitions – likely 
the MAM-L and MAM-C. 48 To prepare, SAMI 
is building production lines for the Akıncı and 
has sent 300 employees to work with Baykar to 
build expertise. 49 As SAMI navigates a potential 
reorganisation, it is considering establishing an 
uncrewed-systems subsidiary to consolidate its 
UAV programmes.
UAE
UAV development in the UAE is concentrated 
within the EDGE Group, primarily its ADASI sub-
sidiary. EDGE is developing a variety of loitering 
munitions, notably the Shadow series, as well as 
rotary UAVs for ISR and combat missions. More 
recently, it has begun expanding into the MALE 
UAV market. These include the Reach-S, Reach-M 
and Jeer MALE UAVs.
A Reach-S (small) mock-up debuted in 
November 2021; flight testing has been under 
way since 2023. Although development is still 
ongoing, the UAE ordered 100 units in November 
2023, signalling its confidence in the project. 50
The Reach-S is designed for reconnaissance and 
strike missions, similar to the Turkish Bayraktar 
TB2. ADASI also partnered with Akaer to work 
on its design and engine-performance analysis, 
among others.51 EDGE plans to sell the Reach-S for 
USD1.1 million, which is lower than rival models 
in its category, which sell for between USD3.5m 
and USD5m. The company also plans to integrate 
proprietary payloads on the Reach-S, such as the 
Mirsad R electro-optical system and Desert Sting-
16 glide weapons. 
Two years after the Reach-S debut, EDGE 
unveiled the larger Reach-M (medium), featuring 
a higher ceiling and payload capacity. Final flight 
testing was expected to begin by 2024. 52 At IDEX 
2025, the Reach-M was showcased with EDGE-
made payloads, including the Mirsad R, Halcon’s 
Thunder P-31 smart bombs, Desert Sting-16 glide 
weapons and ADASI’s Rash-2H and Rash-3 
guided munitions.
Additionally, at IDEX 2025, EDGE unveiled 
a third MALE UAV: the Jeer. Although it has 16 
hours of endurance compared to the Reach-S’s 24 
hours, the Jeer’s main advantage is its lower cost, 
at USD499,000 versus USD1.1m, while offering 
comparable payload capacity and ceiling. Citing 
these cost efficiencies, EDGE Chairman Faisal 
al-Bannai announced the Jeer will replace the 
100 Reach-S units originally ordered by the UAE 
armed forces in 2023.53
Regional UAV purchases 
While Israel and Iran have relied almost exclu-
sively on domestically produced UAVs, Saudi 
Arabia and the UAE have taken a twin-track 
approach. Both have sought to establish local 
development and production while also acquir-
ing foreign systems to address immediate capa-
bility gaps. Other regional actors, with more 
limited industrial bases and defence budgets, 
have focused nearly entirely on off-the-shelf 
purchases. Given the relatively small number 
of suppliers, particularly for MALE UAVs, such 
acquisitions have taken place within the interplay 
of military requirements, system quality, geo-
political alignment and opportunities for technol-
ogy transfer and localisation.
Several Gulf states have, over the years, tried 
to purchase MALE UAVs from Washington but 
were hampered by the United States’ adherence 
to the MTCR. 54 During the 2010s, this initially 
=== PAGE 68 ===
UNINHABITED  MIDDLE EAST
67UAVS: ISR, DETERRENCE AND WAR 
led the Gulf states to look to China. Saudi Arabia 
and the UAE, along with other Middle Eastern 
states such as Egypt and Algeria, bought Chinese 
UAVs, predominantly the Wing Loong I and II, as 
well as the CH-4. 55 Dissatisfaction with the util-
ity of these earlier UAV purchases, and a wish 
to avoid single-source dependency, led them to 
seek other sources. 
In 2017, the UAE started sourcing South 
African UAVs. In 2018, shortly after the Qatar 
blockade started, Doha looked to Turkiye for 
UAVs and support. This meant that Turkiye’s rela-
tions with Saudi Arabia and the UAE suffered but 
recovered in 2021, when the blockade was lifted; 
UAVs were an important part of this revitalisation. 
In 2023, Saudi Arabia began investment, sales and 
localisation agreements with Baykar. By late 2023, 
the UAE had begun ordering domestically pro-
duced UAVs, while simultaneously integrating 
Emirati weapons onto Turkish-made UAVs.56
This signalled a shift away from the early 
Chinese-dominated procurement to a more diver-
sified market, consisting of local products, plus 
support from South Africa and Turkiye, amid the 
continued use of previously procured Chinese, 
Turkish and South African UAVs. As Turkish 
and localised production lines augment Chinese 
MALE UAVs, and most recently American types, 
it appears that some of these older Chinese UAVs 
are being outsourced to the battlefields in Libya 
and Sudan, suggesting that Chinese UAVs are not 
always the preferred choice when other suppliers 
are available, and Chinese influence in this mar-
ket can be challenged.
Indeed, demand for American-made UAVs 
appears intact, notwithstanding the loss of MQ-9 
Reapers over Yemen. Campaigns against Iranian 
proxies resemble the counter-insurgencies for 
which the MALE UAVs were specifically adapted. 
As a consequence, US moves to ease the foreign 
purchase of its US-designed UAVs may see further 
displacement of China’s influence in the regional 
UAV market. US President Donald Trump vis-
ited Saudi Arabia, Qatar and the UAE in May 
2025. During this visit, the US and Qatar signed 
an agreement for eight MQ-9B Sky Guardians for 
almost USD2 billion. Saudi Arabia and the US 
are negotiating over a far larger package, poten-
tially encompassing up to 200 UAVs as part of the 
US–Saudi arms deal from that visit. 57 The UAE 
received unarmed MQ-9 versions after an earlier 
agreement dating back to President Trump’s first 
term, and negotiations for the armed Sky Guardian
variant are ongoing. US defence start-up Shield 
AI saw increased interest at IDEX 2025 in Abu 
Dhabi. Its V-BAT system could be utilised by

the Emirati navy and coastguard in a maritime 
situational-awareness function and become a 
force multiplier when the Iranian threat consists 
of fleets of small boats that are often difficult to 
track by radar. 
Allowing Middle Eastern partners to purchase 
US UAVs may also affect the trajectory of Emirati, 
Saudi and Turkish indigenous production capa-
bilities. Should governments opt to buy American 
because the products offer superior overall tech-
nology or even to score points with the US admin-
istration, it could leave their own producers facing 
reduced contracts and looking to adjust, for exam-
ple, by focusing on underserved export markets.
CCAs over the Middle East?
The Middle East’s combination of high national 
wealth in the Gulf states, advanced technological 
capacity in Israel and persistent security 
challenges has long made it a prime arena for 
the operational debut of new and advanced 
weapons systems. As leading UAV producers 
such as China, South Korea, Turkiye and the 
US move towards next-generation combat air 
systems that integrate high-performance UAVs 
in manned–unmanned teaming (MUM-T) with 
fourth-, fifth- and eventually sixth-generation 
fighters, it remains to be seen whether the

69UAVS: ISR, DETERRENCE AND WAR 
same dynamic will extend to CCAs and 
stand-alone UCAVs.
Israel, for its part, appears well positioned 
to become an operator or even developer of 
advanced UCAVs and CCAs. The country has a 
long history of sophisticated and innovative air 
operations, a highly advanced industrial base, and 
a mature IT and AI sector that could be leveraged 
to develop the software architectures required for 
autonomous or teaming operations. At the same 
time, Israeli companies have released almost no 
information on MALE development activities 
beyond the current Heron TP and Hermes 900 plat-
forms. This may reflect either the absence of such 
development activity or the classified nature of 
existing programmes.
There is some evidence that the country 
might be undertaking classified work on more 
advanced, high-performance UAVs. A 2022 
press report claimed that the Israeli govern-
ment had asked industry to develop a new type 
of armed UAV with longer endurance, higher 
speeds and LO features, with three such systems 
under development at the time of reporting. The 
article also mentioned Israeli interest in UAV 
aerial-refuelling capabilities.58
Further hints at undeclared Israeli UAV devel-
opments came in 2024. Leaked US intelligence 
documents outlining Israeli preparations for a 
strike on Iran mentioned a system referred to as 
RA-01 being used for covert reconnaissance mis-
sions from a special section at Ramon Air Base. 
This led to speculation as to whether Israel might 
already operate a LO, long-endurance UAV for 
ISR purposes. IAI is known to have explored sim-
ilar concepts as far back as the 1990s, leaving open 
the possibility that Israel might have matured such 
a capability without announcing it to the public.59
Iran might be in the weakest position to real-
ise a functioning UCAV or CCA capability in the 
near future. Both political considerations and a 
United Nations arms embargo limit the country’s 
access to advanced foreign designs. Furthermore, 
it has neglected traditional air operations in 
favour of missile and OWA-UAV operations, has 
not updated its ageing or obsolete fleet of fighter 
jets in decades, and does not possess a traditional 
aviation industry comparable to countries like 
Turkiye or Israel.
Nevertheless, Iran has at least signalled inter-
est in the development or acquisition of LO, 
high-performance UAVs. After the US Lockheed 
Martin RQ-170 Sentinel crash-landed in Iran 
under unclear circumstances, the IRGC’s SAIG 
not only developed smaller designs inspired by 
the RQ-170 but also announced its intention to 
build a full-scale copy of the RQ-170. This design 
was subsequently introduced as Shahed 171, and 
Iran published footage of what it claimed was a 
test flight of the system.60 The current status of the 
programme, however, remains unknown. 
Iran has also announced plans to convert its 
self-described Qaher-313 stealth-fighter demonstra-
tor into a UCAV. Originally unveiled in 2013, the 
Qaher-313 has long been met with a high degree 
of scepticism from external analysts who have 
questioned its technical viability. 61 Thus, any 
reports about a UAV variant of the design have 
to be viewed with caution. Even though Iran has 
claimed that Qaher-313-derived UAVs would be 
operated from its UAV carriers in the future, avail-
able flight footage has so far only shown a subscale 
demonstrator resembling a small RC aircraft.62
Gulf states possessing sufficient funds, more 
advanced fighter jets and excellent relations with 
the countries developing CCA capabilities appear 
in a better position to adopt such systems in the 
foreseeable future. Nevertheless, they are still 
facing challenges. 
Saudi Arabia has expressed interest in join-
ing the Italian, Japanese and UK Global Combat 
Air Programme (GCAP). Each of the three part-
ners has CCA ambitions. As of late 2025, how-
ever, no GCAP agreement with Saudi Arabia had 
been reached. 
In the UAE, EDGE has been pursuing the 
Jeniah, first unveiled as a mock-up in February 
2023.63 In 2023, EDGE was optimistically pro-
jecting a four-year development timeline for the 
Jeniah. In March 2024, an EDGE video showed a 
small demonstrator undergoing flight testing. 
However, a 2025 media report suggested the 
Jeniah is not expected to fly for another three to 
four years, pushing the date towards the end of 
the decade.64 The Jeniah airframe design, should it 
ever be fully developed, could be used as the basis 
for a CCA or a UCAV. 
UAV operations 
The Middle East’s complex geopolitical environ-
ment has provided the region’s principal UAV 
operators with extensive and varied combat 
experience. Iran, Israel, Saudi Arabia and the 
=== PAGE 71 ===
CHAPTER  FOUR
AN IISS STRATEGIC DOSSIER70
UAE have all employed MALE UAVs in counter-
insurgency operations, including repeated Israeli 
UAV use in Gaza, Iranian operations against 
Iranian separatist groups, as well as Syrian and 
Iraqi armed groups, and Saudi and Emirati cam-
paigns against Yemen’s Houthi movement. The 
UAE has also made the deployment and supply 
of MALE UAVs a central element of its power-
projection efforts on the African continent. 
Both Israel and the Iranian-led Axis of 
Resistance have also employed UAVs against 
capable state adversaries, albeit in markedly dif-
ferent operational contexts and employment.
Iran and its non-state-actor allies largely used 
OWA UAVs alongside ballistic and cruise mis-
siles as stand-alone assets to conduct denial-by-
punishment campaigns. While this type of 
employment achieved results in grey-zone cam-
paigns, it largely failed in the confrontations with 
Israel in 2024 and 2025. Israel, by contrast, used 
UAVs against state adversaries in sophisticated 
combined attacks alongside more conventional 
assets, achieving significant results. 
Israel 
Limited operational Israeli UAV use had already 
begun before the 1973 Arab–Israeli War, when 
repurposed RC planes were used on an exper-
imental basis to conduct reconnaissance on 
Egyptian troops. During the conflict, Israel then 
used various US-supplied target drones as decoys 
against Egyptian air-defence networks.65
The progress Israel had made in UAV develop-
ment enabled more extensive use of these systems 
during the country’s 1982 invasion of Lebanon. 
UAVs played a role in Operation Mole Cricket 19, 
which saw the suppression and destruction of 
Syria’s Soviet-supplied air-defence network in 
Lebanon through a coordinated operation involv-
ing UAVs, crewed fighter aircraft, EW assets and 
long-range artillery. UAVs were employed as 
decoys to encourage Syrian air-defence crews to 
activate their radars or to provoke Syrian fighter 
scrambles. They were also used for ISR to locate 
mobile Syrian air-defence systems, cue strike 
assets, conduct battle damage assessment and 
monitor Syrian air bases. Additionally, UAVs 
were reportedly also used to carry out reconnais-
sance on the Palestine Liberation Organization.66
While SEAD/DEAD missions continued to 
influence Israeli UAV development, as evidenced 
by the development of systems like Harpy, UAV 
operations over the following decades were pri-
marily shaped by Israel’s fight against various non-
state actors, particularly in Lebanon and Gaza. 
In Lebanon, Israel used UAVs as part of the 
continuous counter-insurgency campaign it 
waged against Lebanese Hizbullah during the 
Israeli occupation of southern Lebanon from 1982 
to 2000. It was here that Israel used UAVs for the 
An Israeli Hermes 450 
/f_lying over Gaza, 
30 October 2025 
Photo by Jack Guez/AFP via 
Getty Images
=== PAGE 72 ===
UNINHABITED  MIDDLE EAST
71UAVS: ISR, DETERRENCE AND WAR 
targeted killing of militant leaders for the first time, 
when in February 1992 a Scout UAV detected the 
convoy of Hizbullah leader Abbas al-Musawi and 
monitored its destruction by AH-64 Apache heli-
copters.67 During the 2006 war against Hizbullah, 
Israeli UAV operations reached a new level of 
capability, with heavier UAVs such as the Hermes
450 extensively used to detect Hizbullah missile 
launchers and conduct armed strikes. Reportedly, 
Israel had also made further advances in integra-
tion, enabling the streaming of UAV video feeds 
into the cockpits of crewed fighters.68
During Israeli counter-insurgency operations 
in Gaza, UAVs were employed in roles similar 
to those seen in US campaigns in Afghanistan 
and Iraq, providing persistent ISR coverage and 
ground-attack capabilities. Considerable UAV 
use was apparent during Operation Cast Lead in 
2008–09 when the IDF reportedly deployed its 
MALE platforms at scale, including the Hermes 
450, Hermes 900, Heron and Heron TP, and made 
significant use of some of them for strike mis-
sions.69 Reportedly, integration of UAV-based 
capabilities had already advanced to an extent 
that UAV-generated ISR could be used to order 
strikes by artillery or crewed assets within less 
than a minute.70
The major war in Gaza that erupted in 2023 
as a result of the 7 October attack by Hamas on 
Israel, once again, saw large-scale use of UAVs 
for both ISR and strike missions. UAV-based ISR 
during the conflict had become so pervasive and 
effective that no-fly periods for UAVs (demanded 
by Hamas) were included in temporary-ceasefire 
and hostage-exchange arrangements. 71 UAV use 
also extended beyond the war itself. The US has 
reportedly deployed UAVs to monitor the cease-
fire in Gaza agreed in October 2025.72
The aftermath of 7 October 2023 also saw activ-
ity in Lebanon, where Hizbullah initiated low-
intensity combat along Israel’s northern border 
shortly after the Hamas attack. From September 
to November 2024, the conflict escalated to high-
intensity warfare, including an Israeli air cam-
paign and a ground incursion, both of which were 
supported by Israeli UAVs. Once again, Israeli 
UAVs were used for both ISR and strike mis-
sions, with MALE UAVs reportedly being used 
to locate and engage Hizbullah rocket and missile 
launchers as well as the targeted killing of indi-
vidual commanders. Compared to 2006, Israeli 
UAV use had to contend with a more contested 
environment. Hizbullah, which had received var-
ious surface-to-air missiles from Iran, including 
the 358 loitering missile, claimed to have shot 
down at least six UAVs, with footage showing the 
loss of several Israeli Hermes 450s.73
The most innovative and significant use 
of Israeli UAVs, however, occurred within the 
context of Operation Rising Lion, Israel’s 12-day 
campaign against Iranian nuclear and missile 
capabilities in June 2025. Following more than 
four decades of Israeli UAV employment against 
non-state actors in permissive environments, the 
operation marked a return to complex, coordi-
nated UAV use in combined attacks against a 
capable state adversary.
Israel began its campaign with an attack 
against Iranian air-defence systems. These 
included small loitering munitions as well as 
long-range anti-tank guided missiles launched by 
Mossad from within Iranian territory. 74 Iran later 
released footage of alleged covert UAV work-
shops it had seized, raising the possibility that 
the systems were not only smuggled into Iran but 
potentially also assembled there. 75 The destruc-
tion of Iranian air defences created a permissive 
environment for conventional Israeli air opera-
tions, with Israeli combat aircraft being operated 
freely over Tehran and Israeli jet pilots claiming 
that not a single missile had been fired at them. 76
This operational freedom allowed Israel to deploy 
its MALE UAVs to perform both ISR and strike 
missions. MALE UAVs appear to have been par-
ticularly prevalent in the Israeli campaign to elim-
inate Iranian mobile ballistic-missile launchers 
over western Iran.77 Nevertheless, Israel did incur 
some UAV losses during the campaign. Visual 
evidence suggests Israel lost at least three Hermes
900s, two Herons and, potentially, a Heron TP 
over the country.78 The operation of MALE UAVs 
over Iran at considerable distance from Israel 
has raised some questions as to whether some of 
Israel’s UAV operations may have been launched 
from third countries. Iranian officials have repeat-
edly accused Azerbaijan of hosting Israeli UAVs 
during the war, but so far, no independent proof 
of this allegation has emerged.79
Iran
What distinguishes Iran, and by extension its 
associated non-state actors, from many other 
regional and international UAV operators is 
the dual orientation of their UAV employment. 
=== PAGE 73 ===
CHAPTER  FOUR
AN IISS STRATEGIC DOSSIER72
Unlike other states, where counter-insurgency 
missions dominated UAV use for an extended 
period, Iranian UAV development and opera-
tions from an early stage were directed not only 
at counter-insurgency but also at missions against 
state adversaries.
In the counter-insurgency role, Iran made 
extensive use of smaller UAVs and MALE plat-
forms during its interventions in Syria and Iraq, 
employing them against both rebel groups oppos-
ing the Assad regime and jihadist organisations 
such as the Islamic State. Iranian officials have 
described these operations as a catalyst for UAV 
development, particularly in accelerating the adop-
tion and refinement of armed MALE systems.80
Beyond smaller ISR platforms such as the 
Shahed 121 and Shahed 123, the IRGC also deployed 
the Shahed 129 MALE UAV in Syria. From 2015 
onwards, the system was used in combat opera-
tions, with Iranian sources claiming it conducted 
more than 700 sorties during the conflict. IRGC 
officials have stated that these deployments 
helped address early technical shortcomings in 
the design and accelerated the broader adoption 
of UAVs across Iran’s armed forces, which came 
to value the systems primarily for their real-time 
ISR capabilities.81
Iran’s counter-insurgency employment of 
UAVs was not limited to its expeditionary oper-
ations abroad. Smaller ISR platforms and armed 
MALE systems have been used extensively in 
domestic campaigns against Baloch and Kurdish 
armed groups, as well as in counter-smuggling 
operations along the country’s borders.82
Iran also employed both small ISR platforms 
and MALE UAVs to conduct surveillance against 
state adversaries. Missions included monitoring 
US naval deployments in the Gulf and conduct-
ing reconnaissance over US bases in neighbouring 
countries, with imagery and videos recorded dur-
ing these sorties sometimes being used for stra-
tegic communications. 83 In direct confrontations 
with Israel and the US, however, Iran and its non-
state allies favoured OWA UAVs. These systems 
offered an inexpensive but accurate deep-strike 
option for Iran’s non-state partners and allowed 
Iran to add an element of mass to its more sophis-
ticated missile arsenal.
In Yemen, the Houthis began using Ababil 
T-derived OWA UAVs against the Saudi-led 
coalition as early as 2016. Subsequently, the 
range of Houthi OWA capabilities was gradually 
expanded with ever longer-range versions of 
the Sammad, allowing the group to strike deep 
into Saudi Arabia and the UAE. In September 
2019, an attack including Shahed 131 OWA UAVs 
and Quds low-cost cruise missiles precisely tar-
geted key petrochemical facilities operated by 
Saudi Aramco in Abqaiq and Khurais. 84 While 
the Houthis claimed responsibility, it remains 
unclear where the systems were fired from, with 
some reports indicating that the attack originated 
in Iraq or in Iran itself.85 The attack, its geopolitical 
repercussions and the challenges associated with 
UAV strike attribution underscored the value of 
UAVs in grey-zone conflicts. 
In the aftermath of 7 October and the sub-
sequent high-intensity conflicts, Iran’s UAVs 
and those employed by its non-state allies 
demonstrated mixed operational performance. 
While some actors within the Axis of Resistance 
achieved notable success in UAV employment, 
others experienced limited or failed outcomes. 
This divergence in effectiveness largely reflected 
theatre-specific factors, including geography, the 
nature and density of adversary air defences, and 
differing levels of adversary cost tolerance.
The most successful UAV employment dur-
ing the conflict was likely that of the Houthis. 
The Yemen-based group made extensive use of 
UAVs in its campaign against merchant shipping 
in the Red Sea and the Gulf of Aden. UAVs were 
employed for reconnaissance and target identifica-
tion as well as for kinetic attacks. Merchant ships 
without counter-UAV capabilities proved highly 
vulnerable to Houthi systems. While Western 
naval vessels deployed to the area were able to 
intercept a significant number of UAVs, they 
faced operational challenges. Small, low-flying 
Houthi UAVs with minimal infrared signatures 
presented a qualitatively different threat from the 
more conventional anti-ship cruise missiles which 
many Western navies have been primarily trained 
to counter. 86 The Houthis also used long-range 
OWA UAVs alongside cruise missiles for strikes 
against targets in Israel. While most of these were 
intercepted, a few managed to penetrate Israeli 
defences and score highly publicised hits.87
The Houthis appear to have extensively uti-
lised the Iranian-designed Sammad family of 
UAVs. These systems were deployed in multiple 
variants, differing in size, payload, endurance 
and mission profile. Loitering types such as the
Shihab were used in anti-ship roles, while OWA 
=== PAGE 74 ===
UNINHABITED  MIDDLE EAST
73UAVS: ISR, DETERRENCE AND WAR 
variants such as the Sammad 3 and Yafa were used 
for strikes against Israel. The simplicity and low 
cost of the design facilitated sustainment, with the 
Houthis reportedly able to manufacture structural 
components locally and procure largely Chinese 
commercial off-the-shelf components through 
their own supply networks.88
Despite its overall limited military perfor-
mance, Lebanese Hizbullah achieved some suc-
cess with OWA UAVs. Due to the close proximity 
of launch sites in Lebanon to targets in north-
ern Israel, the group was able to employ small 
piston-engine systems, such as the Shahed 101 and 
Ababil T, as well as electrically powered designs, 
such as the Sayyad 107. Their small visual foot-
print, low flight altitudes and, particularly in the 
case of electric systems, reduced infrared and 
acoustic signatures made detection difficult, espe-
cially in the complex terrain of southern Lebanon 
and northern Israel. As a result, the group’s UAVs 
achieved some notable penetration of Israeli air-
space. On 13 October 2024, a Hizbullah UAV 
struck IDF barracks near Binyamina, killing four 
soldiers and injuring 58. 89 Only a few days later, 
on 19 October 2024, a Hizbullah UAV managed 
to damage Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu’s 
home in Caesarea.90
Iran’s own use of OWA UAVs appears to have 
been largely unsuccessful. Tehran first employed 
such systems, primarily Shahed 136s, during 
OperationTrue Promise 1, launched on 13 April 2024 
in response to an Israeli strike on its embassy in 
Damascus. The operation followed Iran’s stated 
strategy of conducting combined attacks, with 
the strike package comprising approximately 170 
OWA UAVs, 120 ballistic missiles and 30 land-
attack cruise missiles. 91 According to Israeli 
sources, none of the UAVs penetrated Israeli air-
space. Israel and its partners made extensive use of 
crewed aircraft to counter the threat, with French, 
Israeli, Jordanian, UK and US fighter jets all 
engaging and destroying incoming UAVs.92 Iran’s 
strike also showed the limitations of combined 
strikes using OWA UAVs and ballistic missiles. 
Due to their slow flying speed, Shahed 136s had 
to be launched several hours earlier than ballistic-
missile barrages, thereby providing ample warn-
ing time for Israeli ballistic-missile defences. 
During Operation True Promise 2 on 1 October 
2024, Iran refrained from using either cruise 
missiles or OWA UAVs, limiting itself to ballistic 
missiles. Iranian OWA UAVs once again went into 
action during the Twelve-Day War from 13 to 25 
June 2025. Iran retaliated for Israeli strikes on sites 
linked to the country’s nuclear and missile pro-
grammes, as well as facilities linked to domestic 
security, by a sustained ballistic-missile and OWA-
UAV campaign it dubbed Operation True Promise 
3. Iran launched a total of about 1,000 OWA UAVs 
over the course of the conflict, including Shahed
Shahed 131 OWA-UAV 
debris recovered after 
the attacks on Saudi 
Aramco facilities in 
Abqaiq and Khurais,

CHAPTER  FOUR
AN IISS STRATEGIC DOSSIER74
107s, Shahed 136s and Shahed 238s. 93 However, 
the results proved nearly as unsuccessful as the 
OWA-UAV attacks in Operation True Promise 1. 
According to Israeli officials, only one or two 
OWA UAVs launched from Iran actually impacted 
in Israel. 94 Once again, Israel and allied forces 
made significant use of crewed assets, including 
fighter jets and helicopters, to counter Iranian 
OWA UAVs. Iranian officials have also repeat-
edly stated that during the various True Promise
missions, they were surprised by Israeli EW capa-
bilities, which might have also contributed to the 
failure of Iranian OWA-UAV attacks.95
Saudi Arabia and the UAE
Saudi Arabia appears to have made extensive use 
of MALE UAVs during its intervention against the 
Iranian-backed Houthi movement in Yemen from 
2015 to 2022. Systems employed during the con-
flict seem to have included the Chinese-made Wing 
Loong II and CH-4, as well as the Turkish Karayel. 
While both video footage and satellite imagery 
confirm their operational use, few details about 
their deployment have been made public so far. 
Some sources seem to suggest that Chinese-made 
MALE UAVs were underperforming in Saudi ser-
vice and experiencing significant crash rates.96
Saudi Arabia also faced an increasingly con-
tested air environment in Yemen as the Houthi 
movement began deploying a range of surface-to-
air missile systems. These included legacy Yemeni 
stocks of air-to-air missiles adapted for ground 
launch, Iranian-supplied surface-to-air systems 
and the jet-powered Iranian 358 loitering missile, 
which was specifically designed to counter UAVs. 
The latter appears to have been responsible for 
several widely reported Saudi MALE UAV losses. 
The experience the Houthis gained in engaging 
Saudi systems was later applied to confront US 
MQ-9 Reapers with success.97
Among Gulf states, the UAE might so far have 
gathered the most operational experience with 
MALE UAVs and advanced furthest in their inte-
gration into joint operations. Beginning in 2018, 
the UAE deployed Chinese-made Wing Loong II 
UAVs to Libya in support of the Libyan National 
Army (LNA) of the Tobruk-based government. 
Emirati UAVs appear to have seen intense combat 
service in the country, with reportedly more than 
800 UAV strikes conducted on behalf of the LNA 
between April 2018 and November 2019 alone. 98
Evidence from the conflict also indicates that 
Emirati MALE UAVs were also used to provide 
ISR support to crewed aircraft, including weap-
onised AT-802 Air Tractors operated by Emirati 
special forces.
While UAV deployments enabled the UAE 
to project power in Libya with reduced risk to 
Emirati personnel and a degree of deniabil-
ity, they did not have a decisive impact on the 
course of the conflict. Turkiye, which backed the 
rival Tripoli-based government, soon deployed 
air-defence assets that succeeded in downing 
several Emirati-operated UAVs. Turkish forces 
also introduced their own MALE UAV capability, 
operating Bayraktar TB2 systems in conjunction 
with KORAL ground-based jammers. These assets 
played a significant role in halting the LNA’s 
advance on Tripoli in 2020.99
Despite the mixed outcomes achieved during 
the Libyan campaign, the UAE has not halted its 
use of UAVs as tools of local power projection. 
In Africa, the UAE appears to have provided 
Chinese-made MALE UAVs to local allies in vari-
ous conflicts, including the Ethiopian government 
and the paramilitary Sudanese Rapid Support 
Forces.100 The latter also seems to have received 
Emirati OWA UAVs, which it has used for several 
long-range strikes. 101 Details about these actors’ 
UAV use remain scarce, however, and it remains 
unclear whether the transfers merely repre-
sented Emirati provision of materiel or actual 
Emirati deployments. 
The UAE appears to be ahead of other Gulf 
countries not only in terms of combat experience 
related to UAVs but also in their overall integra-
tion into operations. The country’s armed forces 
are known to have invested in Forward Air 
Controllers and Joint Terminal Attack Controllers, 
which would support UAV use for close air sup-
port.102 Being one of the few countries in the region 
to own a sovereign satellite capacity, the UAE is 
also further ahead in the establishment of a BLOS 
communications backbone for its UAV operations. 
The UAE upgraded its satellite-communication 
systems for manned aircraft and UAVs through 
Yahsat (now merged under Space42) and the air-
craft support company AMMROC. The Emirati 
space conglomerate Space42 revealed an MoU 
with the Angolan military-intelligence and secu-
rity service in which they promise to collaborate 
on, inter alia, satellite-communications technol-
ogy, high-altitude platforms, UAVs for national 
security and command-and-control centres.103  
=== PAGE 76 ===
UNINHABITED  MIDDLE EAST
75UAVS: ISR, DETERRENCE AND WAR 
However, the extensive air exercises in the 
region lack any mention of MALE UAVs. Although 
the absence of reporting should not be equated 
with the absence of these platforms in exercises, it 
is noteworthy that there is barely any mention of 
UAVs in Desert Flag, hosted by the UAE, or Spears 
of Victory 2025 in Saudi Arabia. Static displays, 
such as Stand of Loyalty in 2024, feature UAVs, but 
not in flight.104 When Emirati Minister of Defence 
Prince Hamdan bin Mohammed visited Al Dhafra 
Air Base in May 2025, UAVs were not featured, 
while the Dassault Mirage 2000, Lockheed Martin 
F-16, Saab GlobalEye Airborne Early Warning 
and Control system and AT-802 Air Tractor were. 
The lack of visible UAV use in exercises stands in 
contrast to the promotion they receive in market-
ing for national defence industries or at defence 
shows in the region.
Contrary to the images of extensive UAV 
development and production at the defence exhi-
bitions, the Gulf countries’ usage of UAVs remains 
moderate. Gulf Arab militaries have broadly yet 
to adopt MALE UAVs on a wider scale, integrate 
them into joint operational concepts, overcome 
their stovepipe structures and build their own 
reconnaissance-strike complexes. 
Organisationally, Gulf Arab militaries still 
favour crewed aircraft, and when they have used 
UAVs, the results were indecisive, as in Yemen or 
Libya. While they have acquired UAVs of size, 
payload and endurance, they may still lack the 
backbone of large intelligence organisations to 
analyse the data and integrated communications 
solutions to allow for a coherent command, con-
trol, communications, computers, intelligence, 
surveillance and reconnaissance network that 
makes the best use of these ISR capabilities. 
UAVs are often perceived as ‘unmanned’ and 
thus requiring less manpower; however, their oper-
ation and maintenance still do require personnel. In 
the absence of strongly developed AI capabilities, 
ISR also requires a significant number of personnel 
to make sense of the data. With small populations, 
the Gulf states may yet struggle to field all the UAVs 
and analyse all the data they gather efficiently. 
Consequently, some UAVs have been outsourced to 
Gulf allies and proxies, such as in Sudan or Libya. 
This reproduces the way Iran proliferated UAVs to 
its proxies, albeit in the Iranian case, the emphasis 
was on large numbers of cheap, nimble OWA UAVs. 
Throughout their conflicts with Israel as well 
as Saudi Arabia and the UAE, Iran’s proxies 
learned to combine UAVs and missiles in bar-
rages. Gulf states have thus invested in integrated 
air and missile defences and are now looking to 
foster counter-UAV solutions. However, inte-
grating a new generation of sensors, networks 
and effectors may yet replicate or exacerbate the 
existing organisational divides between air forces, 
air-defence forces and land forces, and could, in 
parallel, negatively impact inter-operability. 
Conclusion
The Middle East serves as both a laboratory for 
the combat use of UAVs and as an increasingly 
important developer and producer of these sys-
tems. However, operational doctrines and the 
level of technological sophistication vary signifi-
cantly across the region. 
Iran and Israel illustrate two markedly differ-
ent models of UAV development and deployment. 
Iran has developed UAVs as a central component 
of its asymmetric military strategy alongside bal-
listic and cruise missiles. As a result, it has pri-
oritised the production of affordable OWA UAVs 
and has actively proliferated these capabilities to 
state and non-state partners. While these systems 
have proven useful in grey-zone operations and 
lower-intensity conflicts, they failed to deliver 
decisive effects in the high-intensity environment 
of the Twelve-Day War. 
Israel, by contrast, has successfully integrated 
a broad range of UAV capabilities into its already 
advanced conventional force structure. These 
include MALE platforms, loitering munitions 
and likely LO systems. This approach has proven 
highly effective both against non-state actors like 
Hamas and Hizbullah and in direct confrontation 
with Iran. Israel also remains the region’s most 
advanced developer and producer of UAV sys-
tems in the region. 
The Gulf states have demonstrated a strong 
interest in a wide range of UAV types. In pro-
curing these systems, Gulf states have pursued a 
mixed approach, combining off-the-shelf acqui-
sitions from various suppliers with technology 
transfers to enable local production and domestic 
development. The long-term effectiveness of this 
approach remains to be seen. Beyond the immedi-
ate operational value, the acquisition, deployment 
and provision of UAV systems by Gulf states has 
also become an increasingly important element of 
regional geopolitics. 
=== PAGE 77 ===
CHAPTER  FOUR
AN IISS STRATEGIC DOSSIER76
The Middle East is likely to remain a key area 
for UAV development and employment in the 
foreseeable future. In addition, the region’s mix 
of frequent conflict, a growing local technologi-
cal base and states with the financial capacity to 
procure advanced systems suggests that Middle 
Eastern states will likely become early adop-
ters of emerging UAV technologies. These could 
include systems that incorporate higher levels of 
AI-enabled autonomy, as well as CCAs.

Uninhabited Indo-Paci/f_i  c
CHAPTER FIVE
The strategic environment in the Indo-
Pacifi  c is becoming more complex, 
ranging from a potential Taiwan con-
tingency to increasingly frequent grey-
zone activities by China in the South 
and East China seas, North Korea’s 
continuing development of weapons 
of mass destruction, and the recent 
confl  icts between Cambodia and 
Thailand and between nuclear pow-
ers India and Pakistan. Governments 
are being forced to adapt as a 
result. Planning documents not only 
acknowledge these dynamics but also 
outline how existing deterrence pos-
tures, defence doctrine and military 
strategies are being revised to address 
a more demanding environment.
The region’s geography alone sets 
the bar high for intelligence, surveil-
lance and reconnaissance (ISR), with 
range and endurance at a premium. 
In peacetime, situational awareness, 
from tactical to strategic, is recog-
nised increasingly as a fundamental 
element of deterrence and security. 
In the case of a peer-on-peer war, ISR 
will be a critical element of military 
capability. Thus, uninhabited sys-
tems for ISR and combat are of ever 
more importance in the Indo-Pacifi  c.
In most, if not all, planning docu-
ments examined below, uninhabited 
systems are seen as key, providing a 
means to respond more rapidly to a 
dynamic and disconcerting security 
landscape. This chapter considers 
how Australia, Japan, South Korea, 
Taiwan, India and Pakistan are 
approaching the development, acqui-
sition and use of uninhabited aerial 
vehicles (UAVs) for ISR, developing 
requirements for collaborative com-
bat aircraft (CCA), and integrating 
these into their broader posture, doc-
trine and strategy.
In this regard, three themes 
emerge: the renewed emphasis on 
persistent land and maritime ISR; a 
focus on precision strike and the use 
of UAVs in such roles; and the empha-
sis on manned–unmanned teaming 
(MUM-T), not only to improve com-
bat capability but also to address 
manpower constraints.
Policy and strategy
Australia published its latest National 
Defence Strategy (NDS) in 2024, 
along with a supporting Integrated 
Investment Program, assessing what 
the government described as the 
‘most challenging strategic envi-
ronment’ the country had faced 
since 1945.1
The NDS outlined a shift in forces 
from those designed for contin-
gency operations to a ‘focused force’ 
structured and equipped to face the 
country’s ‘most signifi  cant strategic 
risks’, with defence policy based on a 
KEY TAKEAWAYS
UNCOMFORTABLE ENVIRONMENT 
An increasingly tense security environment is 
pushing regional players in the Indo-Paci/f_i  c to bolster 
their military capabilities, with particular interest in 
uninhabited air systems. 
LONG/hyphen.capDISTANCE DRIVERS 
The regional geography demands range and 
endurance which favours uninhabited systems, 
not least of all for intelligence, surveillance and 
reconnaissance. 
BETTER BUY  
Domestic industrial ambitions have been tempered 
by production limitations, requiring oﬀ  -the-shelf 
procurements to meet military requirements. 
INCREASING VOLUME 
Collaborative combat aircraft are likely to be 
increasingly adopted in the region as air forces look to 
supplement crewed /f_i  ghter aircraft.

CHAPTER  FIVE
AN IISS STRATEGIC DOSSIER82
strategy of denial. 2 The ability to engage threats 
at greater range and with improved lethality are 
two of the underpinning pillars of the strategy. 
Long-range ISR was highlighted as a core mis-
sion for the air force as one element of the strat-
egy of denial. 3 Other key missions for the air 
force include integrated air and missile defence 
and long-range strike capabilities against land 
or maritime targets. Uninhabited platforms will 
be or already are supporting such missions. The 
Royal Australian Air Force (RAAF) already oper-
ates the Northrop Grumman MQ-4C Triton in the 
long-range maritime-surveillance role.
Japan is looking to reinforce its defence pos-
ture and overall security architecture to better 
enable ‘deterrence by denial’, with the country 
facing a security environment described as the 
most ‘severe and complex … since the end of 
World War Two’.4
In 2022, the government released three plan-
ning documents – the National Security Strategy 
(NSS), the National Defense Strategy (NDS) and 
the Defense Buildup Program (DBP). These all 
heralded a change to Japan’s historical defence 
posture. The administration of Prime Minister 
Takaichi Sanae, who took office in October 2025, 
said it would revise the three strategic docu-
ments in 2026.5
The NDS emphasises a dual approach to 
bolster national defence, including the develop-
ment of counterstrike/stand-off capabilities and 
improved inter-operability with the United States.6
The document lists uncrewed systems as one 
of the seven ‘functions and capabilities necessary 
for the defense of Japan’, describing the combi-
nation of artificial intelligence (AI) and crewed 
platforms as game-changing technologies that 
it claims can transform the conduct of warfare. 7
Under the NDS and the DBP, Japan is prioritising 
the procurement of UAVs for ISR roles, primarily 
maritime surveillance, for peacetime and con-
tingency operations, as well as for more kinetic 
roles.8 Subsequent budget documents announced 
that of the JPY43.5 trillion (USD330.8 billion) 
total DBP investment, JPY1trn (USD7.6bn) would 
be allocated to uninhabited defence capabilities 
between FY 2023 and FY 2027. 9 However, little 
has been said about the specific UAV models or 
quantities being acquired. Research and platform 
trials are ongoing, with procurement expected 
to proceed in phased stages over various fis-
cal years, given the accumulation of UAV pro-
grammes in Japanese defence-budget requests 
released since 2022. 
South Korea has integrated UAVs into its 
so-called Three Axis strategy. This was outlined 
in the country’s 2022 Defense White Paper and is 
intended to counter ‘North Korea’s increasingly 
advanced nuclear and missile threats’ and to ‘sig-
nificantly strengthen the military’s ability and 
readiness to attack, defend, and retaliate’. 10 The 
strategy comprises the Kill Chain, Korea Air and 
Missile Defense, and Korea Massive Punishment 
and Retaliation.

UNINHABITED  INDO/hyphen.capPACIFIC
UAVS: ISR, DETERRENCE AND WAR 
Kill Chain refers to South Korea’s plan to 
pre-emptively strike at launch sites, command 
centres and supporting infrastructure relevant 
to North Korea’s nuclear or ballistic-missile kill 
chain in the event of an imminent nuclear or 
ballistic-missile attack. Korea Air and Missile 
Defense refers to South Korea’s defensive meas-
ures against nuclear or ballistic-missile attacks. 
Korea Massive Punishment and Retaliation refers 
to mass retaliatory strikes targeting North Korean 
leadership and infrastructure in the event of a 
North Korean attack.11
Furthermore, in recognising the country’s 
growing personnel challenge, owing to declining 
birth rates, South Korea is investing in MUM-T 
programmes across all services to try and main-
tain a credible deterrence posture.12
Taiwan’s 2025 Quadrennial Defense Review 
and 2025 National Defense Report build on the 
2023 National Defense Review, which first high-
lighted the importance of asymmetric capabil-
ities.13 The 2025 Quadrennial Defense Review 
outlines a new ‘multi-domain denial and resilient 
defense’ posture against potential invasion and 
warns of the difficulty in identifying early warn-
ing signs of a possible Chinese invasion and the 
increasing sophistication of Chinese capabilities.14
Moreover, the review highlights the 
new emphasis on four areas: exploiting geo-
graphical advantages; degrading, denying 
and disrupting the enemy; layered defences 
throughout the different stages of invasion; and 
asymmetrical capabilities. 15
The four-day conflict between India and 
Pakistan, from 7–10 May 2025, highlighted how 
both have developed their deterrence postures and 
doctrinal principles, and how they hope to employ 
specific platforms in support of these concepts.
Following a terrorist attack on 22 April 2025 in 
Pahalgam, Jammu and Kashmir, India launched 
a series of airstrikes on 7 May as part of Operation 
Sindoor, targeting alleged terrorist camps. In retal-
iation, Pakistan deployed combat aircraft against 
India’s own systems and subsequently launched 
attacks on Indian territory on the evening of 7 
May and into the early hours of 8 May, employing 
UAVs to carry out the attacks. Tit-for-tat attacks, 
including by UAVs, continued until 10 May.
In the immediate aftermath of Operation 
Sindoor, persistent ISR was identified by Indian 
analysts as a ‘non-negotiable’ capability which 
required further bolstering.16
Persistent land and maritime ISR 
Persistent ISR enables regional players to min-
imise an adversary’s first-mover advantage. Of 
course, it also allows the monitoring of borders 
and waters, where potential threats, incursions 
or even conflict are most likely to occur. All gov-
ernments studied in this chapter are prioritising 
the acquisition of high-altitude long-endurance 
(HALE) and medium-altitude long-endurance 
(MALE) UAVs, along with shipborne UAVs to 
improve overall maritime-domain awareness. 
Electro-optical/infrared (EO/IR) payloads are 
no longer sufficient; UAVs with maritime search 
radars, synthetic aperture radars (SAR) and/or 
signals-intelligence (SIGINT) payloads are also 
being acquired. However, in some cases, the 
study of low-observable (LO) uninhabited combat 
air vehicles (UCAVs) may signal some recognition 
of the survivability issues faced by HALE and 
MALE UAVs in a highly contested environment. 
Long-range ISR was highlighted as a core 
air-force mission in Australia’s NDS, comprising 
an element of the country’s ‘strategy of denial’. 17
Three of four Triton HALE UAVs on order for 
Australia have now been delivered, with the final 
aircraft to arrive in 2028. 
On the other hand, Tokyo’s renewed empha-
sis on maritime security likely comes amid what it 
sees as a strategic challenge in the form of Chinese 
grey-zone activities in the South and East China 
seas and increasingly assertive military activi-
ties around Japan. 18 Any Taiwan contingency, in 
particular, is a concern for Tokyo. For instance, 
former prime minister Abe Shinzo stated that 
‘a Taiwan contingency is a Japan contingen-
cy’.19 Taiwan straddles sea lines of communica-
tion through which pass 32% of Japan’s imports 
and 25% of its exports. 20 Consequently, Tokyo is 
investing in UAVs capable of persistent ISR and 
maritime surveillance to allow it to monitor its 
home and archipelagic waters. Russia’s military 
cooperation with China and the missile threat 
from North Korea have likely further elevated the 
importance of robust ISR for Japan.
Currently, the Japan Ground Self-Defense 
Force (JGSDF) operates UAVs such as the Subaru 
Flying Forward Observation System, Subaru 
Flying Forward Reconnaissance System, Hitachi 
JUXS-S1, Aeryon JDXS-H1 SkyRanger and Insitu 
Scan Eagle 2.21 These are relatively small plat-
forms focused more on tactical ISR and disaster

relief. The only HALE/MALE UAV operated by 
the Japan Air Self-Defense Force (JASDF) is the 
Northrop Grumman RQ-4B Global Hawk Block 30. 
The decision to acquire three Global Hawks in 2014 
was in part informed by the need to ‘conduct infor-
mation gathering in areas relatively remote from 
Japan’, following North Korea’s third nuclear test 
in 2013.22 Although there were concerns about the 
system’s high cost, all three were delivered.23
Japan’s Global Hawks, as well as South 
Korea’s, do not, however, carry the advanced 
Multi-Platform Radar Technology Insertion 
Program active electronically scanned array 
(AESA)/ground moving target indicator (GMTI) 
radar found on the newer variant, the Block 40, 
operated by the US Air Force. 24 However, they 
do carry the Enhanced Integrated Sensor Suite 
package, which includes EO/IR, SAR/GMTI and 
SIGINT sensors.25
To further strengthen the Japan Self-Defense 
Force’s (JSDF) ISR capacity, Tokyo is acquir-
ing additional long-endurance UAVs, as well as 
armed reconnaissance, and shipborne UAVs. In 
December 2024, the Japan Maritime Self-Defense 
Force (JMSDF) selected the General Atomics 
MQ-9B Sea Guardian for its Long Endurance 
Unmanned Aerial Vehicle programme, the same 
UAV the Japan Coast Guard had been operat-
ing under lease since 2022. 26 The JMSDF plans to 
acquire 23 units by FY 2032, with initial deliver-
ies expected in FY 2028. 27 New dedicated UAV 
units will operate the platform, taking over 
some of the missions currently carried out by 
Kawasaki P-1 maritime-patrol aircraft. 28 The
UAV can fly for more than 30 hours – three 
times longer than the P-1 – and can integrate 
sonobuoy management and control systems, 
potentially enhancing the JSDF’s future anti-
submarine warfare (ASW) capabilities.29
ISR UAVs are also central to Japan’s stand-off  
defence strategy under the 2022 NSS. Their long 
endurance is expected to support continuous sur-
veillance of targets for effective long-range missile 
operation. Mitsubishi Heavy Industries is testing 
the Fuji Imvac E-5L, capable of operating for 40 
hours, for this role.30
Meanwhile, the JMSDF signed a contract with 
Shield AI for its V-BAT vertical take-off UAV in 
January 2025.31 The JMSDF plans to deploy them 
primarily on patrol vessels to perform ISR missions 
in the vicinity of its surface vessels.32 The V-Bat has 
a maximum endurance of over 13 hours.33
HALE and MALE UAVs are key enablers in 
the Kill Chain leg of South Korea’s Three Axis 
strategy. These platforms’ ability to provide per-
sistent ISR missions allows South Korea to mon-
itor developments and important targets, such as 
nuclear and ballistic-missile launch sites, com-
mand centres and supporting infrastructure.34
South Korea’s HALE and MALE UAVs 
include the air force’s RQ-4B Global Hawk and the 
army’s Israel Aerospace Industries (IAI) Heron, 
respectively. South Korea bought four RQ-4B 
Global Hawk Block 30s, facing some hurdles dur-
ing the acquisition process. The US Congress 
approved the acquisition in 2012, according to

the US Defense Security Cooperation Agency 
listing, and the contract was officially signed in 
2014. However, deliveries were delayed by two 
years (from 2017 to 2019) due to issues relating to 
the UAV system’s cyber security. Moreover, price 
increases initially raised concerns among South 
Korean planners.35
The RQ-4Bs are currently operated by a UAV 
squadron based at Sacheon Air Base, along the 
country’s southern coast, likely enabling it to con-
duct maritime-surveillance missions and monitor 
neighbouring countries, in addition to persistent 
ISR over North Korea.
The army’s three Herons were initially acquired 
in 2014 and introduced into service in 2016. They 
were intended to monitor South Korea’s north-
western islands and the Northern Limit Line. 
However, of the three Herons acquired, one Heron
was lost in 2024 due to North Korean global posi-
tioning system (GPS) jamming.36 Another was lost 
in 2025 after colliding with a Korean Aerospace 
Industries (KAI) KUH-1 Surion while landing. 
The third Heron is reportedly grounded due to 
maintenance issues.37
Recognising the limitations of its HALE and 
MALE UAV fleets, the South Korean government 
has contracted Korean Air Aerospace Division 
(KAL-ASD) to develop a MALE UAV for the 
air force, presently dubbed the KUS-FS or alter-
natively the MALE UAV. 38 Development began 
in 2008, and a first flight took place in 2012. 39
Development was completed by 2022, and a con-
tract was signed the following year for an undis-
closed number of KUS-FS, with deliveries to be 
completed by 2028. 40 It will have a maximum 
take-off weight of 4,760 kilograms and will be 
able to operate for 24 hours. It will have EO/IR, 
SAR/GMTI, electronic warfare (EW) and SIGINT 
payloads and can be used as a communications 
relay for other assets. 41 There are also plans to 
arm the KUS-FS.
South Korea places importance on its current 
and future UAVs having SIGINT payloads. The 
2022 Defense White Paper highlights the role of 
SIGINT capabilities in detecting imminent North 
Korean missile launches. 
In peacetime and in the preparation phases 
against any invasion attempt, Taiwan’s MALE 
UAVs will be required for persistent ISR over 
the Taiwan Strait and for early warning of any 
impending attack. 42 Should an invasion begin, 
MALE UAVs would be employed to provide 
commanders with theatre-level situational 
awareness and would be used to conduct target-
acquisition missions alongside other ISR UAVs at the 
operational and tactical levels.43 Taiwan, however, is 
only now in the process of acquiring MALE UAVs. 
The National Chung-Shan Institute of Science and 
Technology (NCSIST) began work on the Teng Yun
in 2015, Taiwan’s first indigenous armed MALE 
UAV. The Teng Yun 2 – an improved variant with 
an enhanced flight-control system, an American 
engine, a 20-hour endurance and a range of 1,100 
kilometres – was first unveiled in 2019.44 However, 
it failed a combat-readiness test in 2023 and had 
to undergo testing again in 2024. 45 As of writing, 
the Teng Yun 2 is still in development and has not 
entered production.

UAVS: ISR, DETERRENCE AND WAR 
Recognising the importance of long-endurance 
UAVs, however, Taiwan is set to acquire the 
MQ-9B Sky Guardian, having signed the initial 
contract for four MQ-9B Sky Guardians in 2023. 
Deliveries were scheduled for completion by 
2025, but Taiwan has yet to receive any.46 A second 
contract was signed in 2024, amending the initial 
agreement. Deliveries are now set to commence in 
2026 and be completed by 2027.47
In addition to the air force’s UAVs, shipborne 
ISR UAVs may also play a role. 48 The navy is 
reportedly interested in the vertical take-off and 
landing (VTOL) AeroVironment JUMP 20. 49 The 
coastguard already operates the JUMP 20 from its 
ships alongside the Avix Tech AXH-E230RS and 
is looking to expand its UAV fleet further.50 While 
not part of the armed forces, the coastguard is 
directed alongside the navy to respond to Chinese 
military and government vessels. 51 Furthermore, 
the domestic Drone National Team, which will be 
discussed below, as part of its mass UAV acquisi-
tion mandate, is likely to acquire additional UAVs 
and small uninhabited aerial systems (sUAS) 
capable of shipborne operations and operational 
or tactical ISR.
However, it is unlikely that Taiwan will rely 
solely on MALE, shipborne and ISR UAVs to 
maintain watch over the Taiwan Strait. As the 
battlespace becomes increasingly contested, con-
cerns have arisen about the survivability of these 
UAVs. This may be why NCSIST announced 
in 2022 that it was developing an unnamed LO 
UAV.52 This programme may be a UCAV pro-
gramme similar to South Korea’s cancelled KAL-
ASD KUS-FC, and a separate, small, unnamed 
KAL-ASD UCAV; however, the exact nature of 
NCSIST’s LO UAV remains unclear.
It is also unclear as yet what role MALE UAVs 
played in the four-day 2025 conflict between India 
and Pakistan, though it was likely that MALE 
UAVs were used for ISR missions near the Line of 
Control (LoC).
India currently operates an extensive fleet of 
MALE UAVs. The air force, army and navy oper-
ate approximately 14, 34 and ten IAI Heron 1/2s, 
respectively, making it the most widely operated 
UAV within the Indian armed forces. The lessons 
offered by the 1999 Kargil War with Pakistan 
prompted the drive to improve border surveil-
lance and persistent ISR capabilities along the 
LoC.53 The Herons were consequently acquired 
in 2002 alongside the IAI Searcher Mk II and 
introduced in 2003. 54 The Heron’s performance 
during the 2004 Indian Ocean tsunami prompted 
the acquisition of more Herons in 2005, 2006 
and 2011.55
Indian planning is also undoubtedly oriented 
towards China. The 2020–21 border skirmishes 
between China and India prompted a rethink of 
army UAV operations. UAVs previously under the 
control of the artillery corps were reassigned to the 
army aviation corps, intended to improve the coor-
dination and tasking of MALE and ISR UAVs.56
Despite having an endurance of 24 hours and 
a line of sight datalink range of 200–250 km, the 
lack of a satellite communications (SATCOM) link 
limits the operational range and therefore efficacy 
of the Heron 1. Consequently, in 2020, the armed 
forces announced the planned upgrade of the 
Heron 1, to include a SATCOM link to extend the 
potential maximum beyond-line-of-sight range, 
weapons integration and new sensors. 57 While 
the plan was set to commence in 2022, reports 
emerged in 2023 stating that the plan to arm the 
Heron 1 had been cancelled. 58 The programme’s 
current status is unclear. On the other hand, the 
Heron 2 was contracted in 2021 and introduced 
into service in 2022.59
Navy Heron 1/2s are primarily oriented 
towards maritime-surveillance missions, given 
that 342 Squadron is based in Kochi, Kerala, along 
the south coast, 343 Squadron is based on the west 
coast in Porbandar (with detachments operating 
from the Andaman Islands) and 344 Squadron is 
also based along the southern coast in Uchipuli, 
Tamil Nadu. The navy also operates MQ-9B Sea 
Guardians from this base.
These UAVs have been operated under lease 
by the navy since 2020, though they are unarmed. 
Following the signing of a 2024 contract with 
General Atomics, the navy is due to acquire an 
additional 15 Sea Guardians, with the air force 
and army each due to receive eight MQ-9B Sky 
Guardians.60 Unlike the current Sea Guardians, the 
new UAVs will be armed with AGM-114R Hellfire 
II missiles and GBU-39 Small Diameter Bombs. 
Twenty-one of the 31 new UAVs will be locally 
assembled, with deliveries scheduled for 2029–30.61
The ongoing challenges with indigenous UAV 
programmes and pressing operational require-
ments have likely influenced the decision to 
acquire UAVs from the US. Domestic develop-
ment, however, remains a goal. 62 Initial attempts 
to develop an indigenously designed MALE UAV 
=== PAGE 89 ===
CHAPTER  FIVE
AN IISS STRATEGIC DOSSIER88
began in the 2000s with the Defence Research and 
Development Organisation’s (DRDO) Rustom
1.63 However, despite boasting at least 65 flights, 
interest in the Rustom 1 – with a maximum take-
off weight of 800 kg and a ceiling of 6,096 metres – 
has abated because of factors likely including the 
limited ten-hour endurance and significant pro-
ject delays. 64 Nonetheless, the DRDO has devel-
oped an armed variant of the Rustom 1.65
A new UAV, the Rustom 2, later renamed the 
Tactical Airborne Platform for Aerial Surveillance 
Beyond Horizon-201 (TAPAS BH-201), was devel-
oped from 2011 with the ambition to fix the limi-
tations of its predecessor and deliver 76 airframes, 
mostly to the army. 66 The first flight took place 
in 2016, the year originally planned as the pro-
gramme deadline. But while TAPAS BH-201 was 
intended to have an endurance of 24 hours, and 
a ceiling of 10,668 m, the system only achieved 
18 hours and 8,534 m. 67 Furthermore, the pro-
gramme also faced numerous delays and signif-
icant cost overruns due to engine-related issues. 
Consequently, in January 2024, the programme 
was shelved.68
Recognising the challenges faced by the TAPAS 
BH-201 programme, even before its cancellation, 
the Aeronautical Development Establishment (a 
laboratory of the DRDO) launched its own MALE 
UAV programme in 2022, initially referred to as 
the Single Engine Twin Boom (SETB) programme. 
In 2023, the SETB was renamed the Archer-NG, 
and a new airframe using lighter aerostruc-
tures was adopted. It has now completed engine 
ground run tests and low- and high-speed taxi 
trials, with a first flight planned in 2025. It will 
reportedly have an endurance of at least 18 hours 
and a ceiling of 9,144 m.69
In 2023, the army and navy each acquired at 
least two Adani Drishti 10 UAVs. This is the desig-
nation for the locally manufactured Elbit Systems 
Hermes 900 Starliner. Deliveries began in 2024. 70
Adani Defence and Aerospace and Elbit had pre-
viously agreed to form a joint venture with a 51:49 
share split, which led to the establishment of the 
first private UAV-manufacturing facility in India 
in 2018, based in Hyderabad. The facility was 
specifically established to manufacture aerostruc-
tures for the Hermes 900 and to assemble Hermes 
900 kits provided by Elbit. 71 However, the Drishti 
10 programme has also had issues. While under-
going acceptance trials with the navy, a Drishti 
10 crashed in January 2025 after communication 
with the UAV was disrupted mid-flight.72
Beyond MALE ISR UAVs, recognising the 
increasingly contested operational environment, 
India has two LO and high-speed armed UCAV 
projects – the Stealth Wing Flying Testbed (SWiFT) 
and the Ghatak. The SWiFT was flown in 2022 as a 
technology demonstrator powered by the Saturn 
36MT turbofan engine, intended to test capabili-
ties including stealth technologies, autonomous 
flight and high-speed landing. In 2025, the air force 
decided to transform the technology-demonstrator 
programme into a development programme, and 
it is reported that further design changes are likely 
for the intake, exhaust and weapons bay on the
