one-tonne airframe, as well as the integration of a 
new domestically developed engine.73
The SWiFT programme was initially supposed 
to feed into the Ghatak programme, which would 
have seen the development of a far larger, LO, 
high-speed UCAV weighing between 13 and 15 
tonnes.74 However, likely owing to the challenges 
of developing such a large UCAV, the SWiFT has 
been prioritised as a stopgap solution until the 
Ghatak is ready.75 In contrast to the SWiFT, reports 
indicate that the Ghatak is intended to fulfil a large 
number of different roles, including ISR, target 
acquisition, air-to-air combat, air-to-surface com-
bat, suppression of enemy air defence (SEAD) and 
even MUM-T, implying it could operate alongside 
current and future fleets of combat aircraft like 
India’s other planned CCAs.76
For its part, Pakistan’s first MALE UAV was 
almost certainly the China Aerospace Science 
and Technology Corporation’s (CASC) CH-3A, 
known locally as the Burraq, in the 2010s. Pakistan 
possibly began operating the Burraq in 2013, when 
they were reportedly deployed to conduct ISR 
missions over the Federally Administered Tribal 
Areas, where al-Qaeda and Taliban militants were 
known to operate.77
Possibly drawing inspiration from the Burraq, 
Pakistani company Global Industrial Defence 
Solutions (GIDS) developed the smaller Shahpar-I, 
which shares visual similarities with the Burraq. 
Its most recent variant is the larger Shahpar-II. 
First shown at the Pakistan Day Parade in 2021, its 
service status remained unclear until early 2024, 
when it was shown in a video released by the 
Pakistan Air Force (PAF).78 A further variant, the 
Shahpar-III, is currently under development, offer-
ing several performance improvements, includ-
ing greater endurance and payload.
The United States’ restrictive export policy 
on UAVs led Pakistan to look to China to meet its 
emerging requirements.79 As well as the CH-3A, 
by the mid-2010s Pakistan had also received the 
Chengdu Aircraft Industrial Group (CAC) Wing 
Loong I, evidence of which appeared in 2016 when 
crash footage of the type emerged. Wing Loong-
type UAVs were also visible in satellite imagery 
of the PAF air base at Mianwali in 2017 and 2018, 
with a gap until they were next observed in 
2021.80 The more capable Wing Loong II was first 
seen on satellite imagery at the same air base in 
2021. Along with greater endurance, this variant 
may also be fitted with a SAR suite. The UAVs’ 
possible basing in Mianwali in the state of Punjab, 
relatively near to the LoC, is indicative of the role 
the UAVs are intended to fulfil. 
In contrast to the PAF, the Pakistani Army 
opted for the CASC CH-4B, which it began oper-
ating in 2021 from Bhawalpur Air Base. 
However, from 2023, Pakistan also began 
operating Turkish UAVs, likely as a result of 
the deepening defence ties between the two 
countries over the previous two decades. In 
2021, an agreement was signed between Turkish 
Aerospace Industries (TUSAŞ) and Pakistan’s 
National Engineering and Scientific Commission 
(NESCOM) to cover the local manufacturing of 
the TUSAŞ Anka, though it is unclear how far 
this has progressed. 81 The first Baykar Bayraktar 
TB2 was introduced in 2023, followed in 2024 by 
the delivery of the Bayraktar Akıncı. 
It seems apparent that regional governments 
have not, overall, acquired HALE/MALE UAVs 
solely for their persistent ISR capabilities. Many 
of the aforementioned HALE/MALE UAVs 
are armed and capable of conducting ground-
attack missions, able to loiter over a specific area 
for extended periods of time and launch guided 
weapons. Other types of UAVs, such as one-way 
attack UAVs (OWA UAVs), have also been used 
by governmental and non-governmental actors 
within and outside the region to achieve a kinetic 
effect. However, OWA UAVs are also being spe-
cifically developed and introduced for missions 
requiring precision strike and/or the capability to 
overwhelm enemy forces.
Precision strike 
The development of long-range precision strike is 
increasingly being prioritised in the Indo-Pacific. 
Recently, interest in using uninhabited systems 
for long-range attack, suppression or destruction 
of enemy air defence (SEAD/DEAD) using OWA 
UAVs is emerging as a common area of focus. 
Numerous domestic programmes have begun, 
but it remains to be seen how acquiring and/or 
producing these UAVs at scale will be realised.
In addition to acquiring the AeroVironment 
Switchblade 300 in 2024, Australian firm Innovaero 
is developing the One-Way Loitering (OWL) 
series of OWA UAVs which were initially tested 
by the Australian Army in 2023 and 2024. The 
OWL-B was the first type unveiled and has a 200 
km range, a 100-minute endurance and a 6.8 kg 
=== PAGE 91 ===
CHAPTER  FIVE
AN IISS STRATEGIC DOSSIER90
warhead. The OWL-A was first unveiled in 2025 
and has a more limited range of 45 km and a 
smaller 1.5 kg warhead, but has an ISR capabil-
ity. Another system, OWL-X, was also unveiled 
in 2025 but little detail has been released, beyond 
that it is larger than the OWL-B.82
Further north, Japan’s Self-Defense Force 
plans to procure UAVs for operations in line 
with its Synchronized, Hybrid, Integrated and 
Enhanced Littoral Defense (SHIELD) multi-
layered coastal-defence concept. SHIELD seeks to 
integrate uninhabited systems – UAVs, uninhab-
ited surface vessels and uninhabited underwater 
vehicles (UUVs) – under a centralised system to 
better coordinate responses to a potential inva-
sion. As part of this effort, the JSDF plans to 
acquire land-based and shipborne UAVs, includ-
ing OWA UAVs.83
In terms of land-based UAVs, Japanese pro-
curement focuses on three categories of OWA 
UAVs: Type One, designed for attacking ground 
targets; Type Two, for sea and ground targets; 
and Type Three, for longer-range attack missions 
facilitated by a SATCOM link. 84 The JGSDF is 
reportedly considering the IAI Rotem L, UVision 
HERO 120 and Elbit SkyStriker for this require-
ment.85 The JASDF is also expected to procure 
OWA UAVs designed to target adversary ves-
sels.86 For shipborne UAVs, the JMSDF plans to 
acquire two types: an anti-ship OWA UAV sim-
ilar to the Type Two and an armed UAV. 87 The 
latter may include Shield AI’s V-BAT, which, in 
its MQ-35A variant with the US Navy and Marine 
Corps, is capable of launching munitions such as 
Northrop Grumman’s six-pound Hatchet minia-
ture precision-strike weapon.88
In parallel, Tokyo is also conducting opera-
tional demonstrations of larger UAVs capable of 
ISR, EW and ground-attack missions, including 
Turkiye’s Baykar Bayraktar TB2 and Israel’s IAI 
Heron 2. 89 These UAVs are being considered as 
potential replacements for crewed ISR and attack 
helicopters due to the risks to these platforms 
because of a more contested operational environ-
ment.90 At the same time, minimising or remov-
ing the risk to aircrew is a requirement driver for 
Japan, because the country also faces a shrinking 
demography and an increasingly pressing lack of 
human resources. 
Precise procurement planning will be neces-
sary, especially as Japan lags behind other major 
states in UAV development and deployment. 
While Japan has been conducting UAV-related 
research and development (R&D) since the early 
2000s, including with the Fuji TACOM, its UAV 
capabilities have been mainly limited to small 
platforms with restricted payloads. 91 This is 
largely because demand within the JSDF remained 
low, as the tactical value of UAVs was considered 
questionable. The high per unit production costs, 
stemming from the JSDF being the sole buyer, 
also discouraged private-sector participation. 
Japan is now seeking to reverse this through 
domestic UAV production, to support its 
defence-industrial base, bolster supply-chain 
resilience and better ensure sustained combat 
effectiveness. By approximately 2032, Tokyo aims 
to accelerate the development and deployment of 
UAVs tailored to Japan’s geographic features. 92
Achieving this objective will require the govern-
ment to overcome several challenges, including 
barriers to private-sector entry due to low prof-
itability and strict export controls, both of which 
limit customers to the JSDF. The Takaichi admin-
istration is set on reforming Japan’s defence-
export policy to tackle the issue. 93 Japan is also 
eyeing the expansion of UAV exports through 
foreign-assistance schemes such as the Official 
Security Assistance programme.94
In South Korea, UAVs will provide ISR as part 
of the Korea Massive Punishment and Retaliation 
leg of the country’s Three Axis strategy, but they 
are also being considered as strike vehicles. The 
army already operates corps-level, division-level 
and battalion-level UAVs. The corps-level UAV, the 
KAI RQ-101 Songgolmae, is specifically intended to 
provide targeting information for corps artillery 
forces, possibly having supplanted the IAI Searcher
in this role.95 KAI is developing a new corps-level 
UAV. Having been contracted in 2012 and begin-
ning its conceptual-design phase in 2021, the new 
UAV remains a prototype at time of writing. 96 It 
will reportedly have a maximum take-off weight 
of 1,700 kg, an endurance of at least 12 hours and a 
SAR, offering real-time target tracking, a capabil-
ity that is not met by the Songgolmae.97
However, the army is not the only service 
operating UAVs in the target-acquisition role. The 
navy signed a contract with Schiebel in 2024 for 
the delivery of the Camcopter S-300.98 The S-300 is 
intended to complement the Camcopter S-100. It 
may also allow the navy (and the marine corps) a 
new, uncrewed airborne target-acquisition capa-
bility and additional anti-submarine capability, as

the S-300 is capable of carrying ASW sonobuoys. 
This UAV may also complement the army’s 
land-based UAVs in monitoring the northwest-
ern islands, launching from either navy ships or 
marine-corps bases.99
Beyond their role in target acquisition, OWA 
UAVs will also likely be utilised for precision 
strikes. To bolster the air force’s IAI Harpy OWA 
UAVs, new OWA UAV programmes are ongoing. 
OWA programmes began as early as 2020 when 
South Korea signed a fast-track contract with 
LIG Nex1 for the Multi-Purpose Drone/Direct 
Collision Strike Drone, later renamed the Small 
Reconnaissance-and-Strike Drone, an OWA UAV 
with a payload that can be reconfigured for ISR 
and strike missions.100
The KAL-ASD KUS-SX is another OWA UAV 
currently in development. Both UAVs have been 
spotted in military parades and press-release foot-
age from 2024 onwards, but their operational status 
remains uncertain. The KUS-SX, in addition to ful-
filling an ISR and strike role, will also fulfil a SEAD 
and EW role.101 The Harpy also fulfils a SEAD role. 
In this capacity, it can be assessed that OWA UAVs 
capable of SEAD missions may play a role in the 
Kill Chain and the Korea Massive Punishment and 
Retaliation legs of the Three Axis strategy.
The Defense Acquisition Program 
Administration (DAPA) also began a new pro-
gramme titled ‘Development of a Medium-Range 
Loitering Munitions with LEO Satellite Data Link’, 
set to run until September 2026. Highlighting the 
country’s current limited ability to mass-produce 
UAVs at scale, the programme aims to improve 
mass-production capacity and to develop a UAV 
with a maximum take-off weight of 150 kg, a 
range of 1,000 km, a ceiling of 5 km and an endur-
ance of ten hours.102
More recently, KAL-ASD unveiled the 
KUS-FX at ADEX 2025. Blurring the lines between 
long-range OWA UAV and cruise missile, the 
KUS-FX is capable of being launched from a 
multiple-barrel rocket launcher. It has a maxi-
mum take-off weight of 226 kg, an endurance of 
1.5 hours, a ceiling of 6,000 m and a 1,300-newton 
thrust turbojet engine allowing it to reach maxi-
mum speeds of Mach 0.6 to 0.7. The KUS-FX is spe-
cifically intended to be a modular platform, fitted 
with EO/IR sensors for ISR and target-acquisition 
missions, EW systems for jamming missions, 
radar cross-section amplifiers for decoy missions, 
and presumably a warhead for strike missions.
Also at ADEX 2025, two new OWA UAVs were 
unveiled by KAL-ASD – the Small and Medium 
Loitering Munitions. The Small Loitering 
Munition has a maximum take-off weight of 25 
kg, an endurance of 2.5 hours and a 50 km range. 
It has an image-based seeker and is reportedly 
intended to be capable of swarming and satu-
rating the airspace and enemy defences. The 
Medium Loitering Munition is heavier at 150 kg, 
with a longer endurance of ten hours and a longer 
range enabled by SATCOM.103 Alongside domes-
tic development programmes, South Korea is also 
=== PAGE 93 ===
CHAPTER  FIVE
AN IISS STRATEGIC DOSSIER92
looking to acquire foreign-design OWA UAVs. In 
2024, a contract was signed with WB Group for 
200 Warmate 3s.104
In addition to the Three Axis strategy, South 
Korea stood up the tri-service Drone Operations 
Command in 2023. Following repeated intrusions 
by North Korean UAVs, the Drone Operations 
Command will be responsible for ‘responding 
to enemy UAVs through surveillance, reconnais-
sance and potential strike operations, among 
other military actions’. 105 The Drone Operations 
Command operates a variety of sUAS and OWA 
UAVs along the border with North Korea, includ-
ing the Sungwoo S-Bat and the KUS-SX.106 Despite 
its small size, the S-Bat has reportedly been flown 
deep into North Korean airspace and may have 
even been captured by North Korean forces.107
KAL-ASD is also developing a small, LO flying-
wing UAV. The designation of this system remains 
unknown, but it has been spotted in recent mili-
tary parades and in press-release footage related 
to the Drone Operations Command.108
A similar, but far larger, LO UAV, the KUS-FC, 
was being developed by KAL-ASD. This UAV 
shares some visual similarities with the unnamed 
LO flying-wing UAV.109 The KUS-FC was intended 
to operate both as a CCA and as an independ-
ent UCAV, with a small-scale demonstrator, the 
Kaori-X, being developed from 2010 onwards. 110
However, funding for the programme was even-
tually withdrawn in 2024.111
For Taiwan, denial, asymmetric and precision-
strike capabilities will become important should 
an invasion attempt begin. 112 ‘[Taiwan’s] Armed 
Forces is continuously refining the joint opera-
tional command mechanism, developing various 
long-range precision strike weapons, and inte-
grating ISR measures to form a kill chain, to attack 
the centres of gravity and key nodes of the ene-
my’s landing operations, thereby disrupting and 
impeding their planned operations and tempo 
and ultimately thwarting their missions.’113
In this regard, MALE, shipborne and ISR 
UAVs continue to be used for ISR and target-
acquisition missions, while attritable UAVs are 
useful for attempting to overwhelm enemy forces. 
The 2025 National Defense Report highlights the 
role of UAVs in precision strike.114
The Air Force Air Defense and Missile 
Command’s NCSIST Chien Hsiang OWA UAV has 
been developed with this role in mind, intended 
to perform SEAD/DEAD missions – equipped 
with a radio-frequency (RF) seeker – as well as 
decoy missions. In its SEAD/DEAD capacity, the 
Chien Hsiang reportedly does not require a human 
in the loop and can fly to a preset location and 
automatically search for RF emissions. 115 The 
Chien Hsiang was unveiled in 2017, with a con-
tract signed in 2019. 116 Some reports suggest it 
may have an endurance of five hours and a range 
of 1,000 km and that 12 Chien Hsiangs can be car-
ried and deployed from a single truck-mounted 
launcher system.117
Further variants of the Chien Hsiang are 
currently being developed with imaging- and 
GPS-guidance capabilities, intended to compli-
cate possible countermeasures. 118 The imaging-
guidance variant will be capable of image tracking 
and is intended to conduct attacks against fixed 
and mobile targets. The gimbaled camera likely 
enables it to conduct human-in-the-loop attacks 
as well as ISR missions if needed. 119 Meanwhile, 
the GPS-guidance variant is intended to conduct 
attacks on large, fixed or slow-moving targets. 
These variants, still in development, may have 
larger warheads intended to destroy high-value 
assets such as enemy vessels.
Alongside the Chien Hsiang, Taiwan is working 
more broadly to greatly increase its stockpile of 
medium- and short-range UAVs. These UAVs are 
likely to play a role in attempting to saturate the 
airspace over the Taiwan Strait and overwhelm 
enemy vessels. While the NCSIST is responsible 
for manufacturing 700 ‘military-grade’ UAVs, the 
Drone National Team has the crucial role of over-
seeing the mass acquisition of ‘commercial-grade’ 
UAVs for military use and coordinating relevant 
industrial partners and public–private partner-
ships to ensure the required quantities can be 
delivered to end users. Taiwan arguably increas-
ingly recognises the need to shore up its industrial 
base and build stockpiles through domestically 
produced platforms, as well as the inherent lim-
itations of relying on foreign-sourced systems. 120
Created in 2022, the Drone National Team has 
been tasked with facilitating an annual produc-
tion rate of 180,000 UAVs by 2028. However, only 
8,000 to 10,000 UAVs were manufactured in 2024. 
Production capacity was limited by struc-
tural challenges, owing to high manufacturing 
costs and few domestic or foreign orders, which 
prevented the realisation of economies of scale. 
In the 2023 National Defense Review, despite 
the pledge to allocate 80% of UAV production 
=== PAGE 94 ===
93
UNINHABITED  INDO/hyphen.capPACIFIC
UAVS: ISR, DETERRENCE AND WAR 
to its manufacturing sector, the government had 
by then only planned to acquire approximately 
8,000 UAVs between 2022 and 2028. In real terms, 
reports suggest the government had only ordered 
4,000 UAVs by 2024.121
Taiwan struggles to manufacture sensors, 
communications systems, positioning systems 
and flight-control systems, and remains reliant 
on foreign suppliers. 122 Moreover, despite being 
a global leader in semiconductor manufacturing, 
Taiwan has to rely on semiconductor chips sup-
plied by foreign firms such as Qualcomm and 
Nvidia, often at higher prices, when manufac-
turing UAVs. The historical lack of domestic or 
foreign demand has limited growth in the UAV 
industry and disincentivised other Taiwanese 
firms from investing in the sector and expanding 
capacity to manufacture chips or components for 
UAVs, thereby keeping costs high. 
Where other governments building up their 
UAV industry can and have sourced Chinese com-
ponents to help keep manufacturing costs down, 
especially when it comes to critical components 
such as payloads and communications systems, 
Taiwan cannot afford to build a supply chain 
around Chinese goods and Chinese critical raw 
materials – crucial for the manufacturing of bat-
tery cells and electric motors.123 However, Chinese 
components have already reportedly been found 
in Taiwanese UAVs. In May 2025, reports emerged 
of Chinese communications modules and SD 
cards integrated onto the NCSIST Albatross II, also 
known as the Rui Yuan II. 124 At the time, Taiwan 
had yet to acquire any Albatross IIs, but they have 
since ordered 36 Albatross IIs, presumably after 
the Chinese components were replaced, and these 
are set to be delivered between 2026 and 2029. 
The Albatross II will have electronic intelligence 
sensors, reaffirming the importance now placed 
on SIGINT capabilities. It has an endurance of 
16 hours and a maximum range of 2,000 km. Its 
frequency-hopping communications system and 
multi-band navigation antenna are both intended 
to improve its resistance to jamming and its 
overall survivability.125
Recognising the limitations of its current UAV 
industry, several initiatives are being implemented. 
The Ministry of National Defense established the 
Defense Innovation Office, with uncrewed sys-
tems, counter-uninhabited aerial systems, AI and 
information security as priority investment areas. 
NCSIST, alongside the Ministry of Economic 
Affairs’ Asia UAV AI Innovation Application R&D 
Center (which offers companies a flight-testing 
centre), is constructing the Aerospace and UAV 
Industrial Park to accommodate prospective 
companies.126 The Ministry of Economic Affairs 
has also created the Taiwan Excellence Drone 
International Business Opportunities Alliance to 
promote technology sharing within the industry, 
provide subsidies for R&D on chips necessary for 
UAVs, such as for AI imaging and flight control, 
and boost export opportunities.127
Exports could be a promising avenue to pro-
mote further demand in the Taiwanese UAV 
industry. The US remained the primary export 
destination for Taiwanese UAVs between 2023 
and 2025, but from late 2024 onwards, there 
was a spike in orders from the Czech Republic, 
Germany and Poland. However, as with domes-
tic opportunities, export opportunities remain 
limited by high production and unit costs which 
hinder price competitiveness, limit production 
capacity and create uncertainty surrounding 
long-term demand.128
Importantly, however, in a sudden shift, 
government-procurement tenders in 2025 indi-
cated the Ministry of National Defense now plans 
to acquire 48,750 UAVs by 2027, ranging from 
short-range and long-range first-person view 
UAVs to medium-range and long-range fixed-
wing OWA UAVs, and long-range VTOL 
fixed-wing UAVs, all of which are likely intended 
for ISR and strike missions.129 The plan to increase 
UAV acquisition by more than tenfold in the next 
two years may help firms achieve greater econ-
omies of scale. Whether the industry can scale 
production fast enough to meet the expanded 
number of orders remains to be seen.
In 2025, NCSIST revealed the Mighty Hornet
series. The Mighty Hornet II appears to be a modi-
fied Chien Hsiang, seemingly the new designation 
for the imaging-seeker variant of the OWA UAV, 
with its RF seeker replaced by an EO/IR camera on 
a gimbal.130 On the other hand, the Mighty Hornet 
III is an X-wing VTOL OWA UAV intended to 
destroy armoured vehicles. The Mighty Hornet III 
also features an imaging-seeker, a range of more 
than 50 km and an endurance of more than 40 
minutes.131 The Mighty Hornet IV is a collaboration 
between NCSIST and the US company Kratos. 
The Mighty Hornet IV is essentially a Kratos 
MQM-178 Firejet target UAV fitted with exter-
nal, underwing warheads and an EO/IR payload 
=== PAGE 95 ===
CHAPTER  FIVE
AN IISS STRATEGIC DOSSIER94
developed by NCSIST. It is primarily intended to 
engage helicopters and landing craft, as well as 
to conduct decoy missions. In its anti-ship role, 
the Mighty Hornet IV will likely be used alongside 
anti-ship missiles in a ‘high-low’ mix. 132 Blurring 
the lines between OWA UAVs and cruise missiles, 
the Mighty Hornet IV has a reported range of up 
to 1,000 km.133
While the Chien Hsiang is intended to be oper-
ated within enemy airspace to saturate or disable 
enemy air defences and attack vessels moving 
through the Taiwan Strait, smaller OWA UAVs 
such as the Anduril ALTIUS-600M-V and the 
AeroVironment Switchblade 300 (both acquired in 
2024) will likely be used on enemy forces land-
ing on Taiwan’s beaches in the post-landing stage 
alongside other small OWA UAVs, given their 
shorter range.134
Indian operations on 8 May 2025 saw the 
armed forces flying IAI Harpy and Harop OWA 
UAVs towards major cities in Pakistan such as 
Karachi and Lahore. Pakistan then launched its 
own attacks on 9 May, targeting Indian military 
sites. Tit-for-tat attacks continued until 10 May. 135
Pakistan may have utilised the Baykar-NASTP 
YIHA-III OWA UAV, built from an anti-tank 
guided missile, with an EO/IR camera added for 
targeting and wings and stabilisers for improved 
flight characteristics.136 Both countries likely used 
their OWA UAVs and decoy UAVs to saturate air 
defences. Both countries also used the BrahMos
and Fattah missile systems. Press reporting indi-
cated that Indian attacks damaged the various 
Pakistani air bases targeted on 9–10 May, with 
UAVs possibly involved in the attacks.137
Even before the 2025 conflict, there was some 
Indian recognition of the need to prioritise OWA 
UAVs. In 2023, the Indian company Economic 
Explosives Ltd was contracted by the army to 
deliver 480 Nagastra-1R OWA UAVs, with deliv-
eries completed by late 2024.138 The Nagastra-1R, a 
recoverable OWA UAV, is equipped with an EO/
IR sensor and GPS guidance, has an endurance of 
60 minutes, a ceiling of 4.5 km and a range of 15 
km (with operator control) or 30 km (with human-
on-loop).139 It is unclear whether Nagastra-1Rs 
were utilised during the conflict, but new variants 
of the UAV are currently being developed. 
On the other hand, in 2024, Pakistan unveiled 
its GIDS Blaze series at IDEAS 2024, though it is 
unclear if the OWA UAVs are operational. The 
Blaze-25 is the smallest in the series, weighing 25 
kg, with an endurance of 60 minutes and a range 
of 75 km. The Blaze-50 has the same endurance but 
weighs 50 kg and has a range of 180 km, while the 
Blaze-75 has a longer endurance of 240 minutes, 
a heavier weight of 75 kg and a more extended 
range of 500 km.140
In much the same way that UAVs allow 
regional players an additional means of preci-
sion strike, CCAs can augment existing combat 
aircraft. Though the technology and concepts are

still in their infancy, regional players have pur-
sued these programmes, with ambitions to intro-
duce CCAs in the near future. 
Manned–unmanned teaming
CCAs offer regional players not only the oppor-
tunity to increase their combat aircraft’s lethality 
but also more flexible mission planning, given the 
modularity of CCAs and their payloads. However, 
for Japan and South Korea, CCAs are a necessity, 
a means to maintain combat effectiveness amid 
severe demographic decline. South Korea has 
particularly focused on the MUM-T concept and 
begun numerous MUM-T programmes with the 
aim of having UAVs operating alongside combat 
aircraft but also alongside helicopters, ships and 
combat vehicles. However, the various CCA pro-
grammes are still in their infancy and will only 
see initial flights and integration work begin 
within the next few years at the earliest.
The ability to engage threats at greater range 
was a central element of Australia’s 2024 NDS, 
and uninhabited systems, in particular CCAs, will 
likely play an increasing role in providing such 
capability. When it comes to CCAs, Canberra is 
arguably among the most advanced in the region. 
The RAAF’s Project Air 6015 covers the acquisi-
tion of a CCA, and the Australian Department 
of Defence has been funding the development 
of the Boeing Australia MQ-28 Ghost Bat since 
2019. Originally known as the Airpower Teaming 
System, the RAAF had initially rejected the Boeing 
proposal, but political support overrode the lack 
of service interest. So far, AUD1bn (USD640 mil-
lion) has been invested in the programme, cover-
ing the development of eight Block 1 and three 
Block 2 MQ-28s. 141 As of November 2025, 126 
flights had been carried out, totalling 126 hours. 
As of late 2025, planning was also under way for 
an air-to-air engagement using a Raytheon AIM-
120 Advanced Medium-Range Air-to-Air Missile 
carried on an external pylon, since the Block 1 
and 2 MQ-28s do not have an internal weap-
ons bay. The next steps in Air 6015 may include 
further orders for the MQ-28, but other CCAs 
could additionally figure in air-force thinking. A 
yet-to-be-ordered Block 3 version with a larger 
wing and greater combat radius is also being dis-
cussed. Government and industry are also keen to 
export the MQ-28, with the CCA being pitched in 
the US and Europe, including Germany and the 
United Kingdom. 
Japan has begun initial development of CCAs, 
with the Acquisition, Technology & Logistics 
Agency – an agency of the Japanese Ministry of 
Defense – in the lead since 2022. 142 These UAVs 
are intended to operate alongside next-generation 
combat aircraft and, potentially, in-service com-
bat aircraft types.143
Several domestic companies are develop-
ing their own concepts: the Mitsubishi Heavy 
Industries ARMDC-20X, the Kawasaki Heavy 
Industries Collaborative Support Aircraft and 
Subaru’s experimental remote-controlled sup-
port aircraft. 144 These projects, however, are at a 
comparatively early stage, with performance and 
roles still to be clarified.

Tokyo is also pursuing CCA development 
with the US and other partners, including a 
reported partnership with the US to join its CCA 
programme in January 2024 and building on the 
December 2023 US–Japan agreement to integrate 
AI and machine learning into UAV systems. 145
Tokyo is also taking part in a trilateral CCA initi-
ative with the US and Australia, having signed up 
in May 2024.146
South Korea has heavily invested in MUM-T. 
DAPA established a new MUM-T research cen-
tre in Changwon City in November 2025 focused 
on further development of the autonomous, 
command-and-control (C2), sensor fusion, target 
identification, and datalink and communications 
technologies necessary to enable MUM-T.147
Across the three services, the army’s 70th 
Brigade has been tasked with testing uninhab-
ited ground vehicles, UAVs and sUAS alongside 
the 1st Division of the marine corps. The navy’s 
5th Flotilla will test mine-searching UUVs and is 
currently planning to develop a UAV carrier. The 
air force, meanwhile, has several MUM-T and 
CCA programmes.148
KAI was awarded a contract in 2021 to develop 
MUM-T systems to allow helicopters such as the 
KUH-1 Surion to operate alongside UAVs. 149 The 
technology developed for the Surion will then be 
adapted for the KAI FA-50 Fighting Eagle, which 
will serve as the initial demonstrator for CCA tech-
nology, to be followed by the KAI KF-21 Boramae. 
The goal is to have operational CCAs by 2030.150
KAI will test its Adaptable Aerial Platform-150 
(AAP-150), an attritable type of CCA, with the 
Fighting Eagle between 2025 and 2028.151 The AAP-
150, unveiled in 2021, is a turbojet-powered 150 
kg-class airframe. Its operational concept is cen-
tred on entering contested battlespaces ahead of 
crewed aircraft as well as fulfilling a SEAD/DEAD 
role. It will reportedly have a maximum take-off  
weight of 150 kg, a 24 kg payload capacity, a 1,112-
newton thrust turbojet engine allowing it a maxi-
mum speed of Mach 0.6, and a range of 400 km.152
The AAP-150 is designed with a modular pay-
load, enabling it to carry out ISR, EW, decoy and 
battle-damage assessment missions. Initial plans 
will see the AAP-150 being operated directly with 
crewed aircraft, but future iterations foresee four 
AAP-150s teamed with larger, fighter-equivalent 
CCAs, with four operating along with the crewed 
aircraft.153 Other attritable CCAs being developed 
include the KAL-ASD KUS-RP, with a maximum 
take-off weight of 200 kg. The KUS-RP is meant 
to be launched from an aircraft, rather than a 
launcher like the AAP-150, and is also planned to 
operate alongside larger CCAs. 154 However, the 
status of this platform is unclear.
There are also several larger, fighter-
equivalent CCAs currently under consideration. 
KAL-ASD unveiled a full-scale prototype of its 
Low Observable Wingman Unmanned System 
(LOWUS) in 2025, having begun development in 
2021.155 While its exact specifications have yet to 
be revealed, KAL-ASD has previously showcased 
the KUS-LW concept, which shares some visual 
similarities with the LOWUS. The LOWUS has a 
maximum take-off weight of 5,700 kg, a payload 
capacity of 500–600 kg allowing it to carry two air-
to-ground weapons, an endurance of over three 
hours and a maximum speed of Mach 0.85. It is set 
to begin flight-testing in 2026 and MUM-T trials in 
2027. The Block 2 version of the CCA, currently in 
development, will be larger, carrier-capable, and 
fitted with an engine capable of afterburning.156
KAI also has its own larger, fighter-equivalent 
UAV project, known simply as the Unmanned 
Combat Aerial Vehicle (UCAV), which is intended 
to operate alongside the AAP-150 as part of the 
Next Generation Aerial Combat System (NACS). 
NACS-1 will see AAPs operating alongside 
crewed aircraft; NACS-2 will see AAPs and the 
KAI UCAV operating alongside crewed aircraft 
with communications satellites facilitating com-
munications and data exchanges; and finally, 
NACS-3 will see enhancements to the AI and 
data-fusion systems, as well as improvements to 
the crewed aircraft (the Boramae) and the CCAs’ 
overall stealth.157
The UCAV, which is unlikely to be its final 
name, will reportedly have a maximum take-off  
weight of 5,420 kg, an operational range of 2,590 
km and an 18,237-newton thrust turbofan, allow-
ing it to reach Mach 0.85. The plan is that the 
UCAV can conduct the same missions as the AAP-
150, as well as conduct air-to-air and air-to-ground 
combat, with its AESA radar and electro-optical 
targeting system (EOTS). 158 Its internal weapons 
bay may allow it to carry two air-to-air missiles, 
four GBU-39 Small Diameter Bomb-sized guided 
bombs, or potentially even AAP-150s.159
A new, comparatively large CCA was 
unveiled by KAI at ADEX 2025, designated the 
Medium CCA (MUCCA). It will reportedly have 
a maximum take-off weight of 5,420 kg, a payload

capacity of 1,200 kg, a ferry radius of 2,590 km, a 
combat radius of 926 km, and be powered by an 
18,237-newton thrust engine. Listed capabilities 
include air-to-air and air-to-ground combat, with 
the system reportedly capable of being fitted with 
an AESA radar, an infrared search-and-track sen-
sor, or an EOTS. The MUCCA is intended to oper-
ate with the Small Unmanned Combat Aircraft 
(SUCA), a smaller, modular and attritable CCA 
with a maximum take-off weight of 220 kg. 160
Given the similar specifications of the KAI UCAV 
and KAI MUCCA, and the AAP-220 designation 
given to the SUCA, these may be new iterations of 
the UCAV and AAP-150.
Outside of the air force, the marine corps 
may also operate OWA UAVs dubbed the Air 
Launched Effect alongside its MAH-1 Marineon
light attack helicopter. In 2021, KAI announced 
plans to launch OWA UAVs from canisters fitted 
onto the Marineon, with up to 17 launchable from 
the helicopter (two canisters on the pylons, capa-
ble of carrying four each, plus nine individual 
launchers on the helicopter’s fuselage). The devel-
opment will begin in earnest in the mid- to late 
2020s. The OWA UAVs will have an endurance of 
two hours, a maximum speed of 150 km per hour 
and a 3 kg warhead.161
Given India’s recent combat experience, 
CCAs may, in the future, play a role in New 
Delhi’s ‘deterrence-by-punishment’ posture, pos-
ing a greater air-to-air challenge, be it against 
China or Pakistan. India currently has two major 
CCA programmes – the air force’s Combat Air 
Teaming System (CATS) and the navy’s Naval 
Collaborative Combat Air Vehicle (N-CCAV).
Hindustan Aeronautics (HAL) began scoping 
work for the CATS programme in 2017, though 
work accelerated after 2019.162 That year saw HAL 
showcase the CATS concept, with the British–
French SEPECAT Jaguar as the crewed C2 air-
craft for the CATS Air Launched Flexible Assets 
(CATS ALFA) swarm/CCA UAV (under devel-
opment by HAL in association with NewSpace 
Research and Technologies). The primary C2 air-
craft was referred to as the CATS Mothership for 
Air Teaming Exploitation (CATS MAX), while the 
ALFA-S has a 5–8 kg warhead and a role more sim-
ilar to that of an OWA UAV.163 At Aero India 2021, 
HAL revealed that the HAL LCA Tejas Mk1A will 
be the designated CATS MAX aircraft, capable of 
carrying three pods with four ALFA-Ss each. 164
Other aircraft such as the Sukhoi Su-30MKI Flanker 
H could operate alongside the ALFA-S and India’s 
other CATS CCAs, though this is unconfirmed.165
The centrepiece of the CATS programme, how-
ever, is the CATS Warrior, which will be the larger, 
fighter-equivalent CCA. The CATS Warrior scale 
model was also first unveiled at Aero India 2021.166
Initial plans for a 2024 first flight were missed, 
with the CATS Warrior full-scale demonstrator 
having only conducted a first engine ground 
run in January 2025. 167 Moreover, further tests 
have uncovered additional issues – the airframe 
is larger, heavier and less stealthy than initially 
planned, owing to its large tailplane and promi-
nent dorsal intake.168

Two versions of the CATS Warrior have been 
shown – the first displayed at Aero India 2021 and 
the second at Aero India 2025. The basic airframes 
of both versions are broadly similar. However, 
the 2021 version has larger wings and stabilisers, 
moderately swept wings, a larger and more for-
ward dorsal intake, and a more angular radome. 
In contrast, the 2025 version is smaller, has mildly 
swept wings, a dorsal intake further back and a 
more conical radome.169
It is unclear how the different versions’ spec-
ifications have changed, but recent reports sug-
gest the 2025 version has a maximum take-off  
weight of 2,000 kg, a range of 300–800 km (based 
on payload), an approximate ceiling of 9,000 m, 
and two HAL PTAE-7 turbojet engines each 
capable of 3,700-newton thrust and maximum 
speeds of 700 km per hour. 170 The production 
variant of the CATS Warrior may have the 25,000-
newton thrust HAL HTFE-25 turbofan engine. 171
The CATS Warrior will likely have air-to-air and 
air-to-surface roles, capable of carrying weapons 
in its internal bay (with a capacity of 310 kg) and 
on its two external hardpoints (with a total capac-
ity of 340 kg).172
The CATS Infinity is a solar-powered, 
ultra-high-altitude pseudo satellite which began 
development in 2017, capable of operating at alti-
tudes of 20,000 m, in the stratosphere, for up to 
90 days. It is an ISR and communications-relay 
UAV with a SAR, a terrain-mapping camera and 
a 4G/5G wireless network system, with additional 
target-acquisition and tracking roles.173 The CATS 
Infinity and CATS HALE, unlike the aforemen-
tioned UAVs, are unlikely to operate alongside 
crewed aircraft in combat missions or be operated 
by the pilot/weapons-system officer in the crewed 
aircraft. The CATS HALE will likely fulfil a persis-
tent ISR and ground-attack role, feeding targeting 
information to the crewed aircraft and to wider 
forces.174 It will have an endurance of 15 hours, a 
range of 1,500 km and a ceiling of 15,240 m. These 
are likely the specifications for the turbofan vari-
ant. The turboprop variant will likely have more 
limited specifications.175
The navy’s N-CCAV programme began in 
2023.176 However, the actual platform being 
developed by the navy and NewSpace, dubbed 
the Abhimanyu, was first unveiled at Aero India 
2025. It was revealed that the Abhimanyu is cur-
rently four months into its concept-development 
phase, and NewSpace intends to develop a dem-
onstrator by 2026, with development set to be 
complete by 2028. It will be sent ahead of the 
crewed aircraft to detect and engage enemy 
aircraft and will be capable of conducting ISR, 
EW, attack, decoy and communications-relay 
missions. Reports indicate that it will also be 
swarm-capable.177 NewSpace has been working 
on AI/machine-learning-driven swarming tech-
nology for smaller UAVs over the past few years, 
having also unveiled its Shahed equivalent, the 
Sheshnaag OWA UAV, at Aero India 2025, with a 
payload capacity of 25–40 kg, a range of 1,000 km 
and an endurance of five hours.178
Set to operate alongside the in-service MiG-
29K Fulcrum, the recently ordered Dassault Rafale
M and future indigenous carrier-based combat 
aircraft, the Abhimanyu is described as ‘cost-
effective and expendable, with an emphasis on 
rapid production’.179 It will have a maximum take-
off weight of 200 kg, a payload capacity of 25 kg, 
a range of 1,000 km, an endurance of two hours, 
a ceiling of 6,000 m and a 1,100–1,250-newton 
thrust turbojet engine, allowing it a maximum 
speed of 550 km per hour. This has raised con-
cerns about how well the Abhimanyu will be 
able to operate alongside crewed combat air-
craft, which typically operate at faster speeds. 
It is possible that the Abhimanyu will not be a 
single platform, but a family of platforms with 
varied capabilities.180
Pakistan has no known CCA programme; 
however, as it operates Chinese combat air-
craft such as the Chengdu J-10CE Firebird and 
the Chengdu Aircraft Corporation/Pakistan 
Aeronautical Complex JF-17 Thunder, and could 
potentially receive new, next-generation combat 
aircraft if reports are to be believed, future access 
to CCAs could be through Chinese programmes.
Conclusion
The Indo-Pacific has had to adapt to the shift-
ing strategic environment, evident not only in 
the language of new planning documents but 
also in the breadth of ambitious acquisition and 
development programmes being undertaken. 
Uninhabited systems form a considerable body of 
these projects, whether for ISR or combat, across 
a broad range of air-vehicle sizes and capabilities. 
They are also viewed as a growing contributor 
to deterrence and, should deterrence fail, as an 
essential element of a war-fighting inventory.
=== PAGE 100 ===
99
UNINHABITED  INDO/hyphen.capPACIFIC
UAVS: ISR, DETERRENCE AND WAR 
The regional players covered in this chap-
ter are looking to employ, or are already using, 
HALE/MALE UAVs for persistent ISR, maritime 
surveillance, border surveillance and target acqui-
sition. Uninhabited combat air systems, be they 
CCAs or UCAVs, are also being pursued.
However, attempts to indigenise these UAVs 
have seen mixed success. Moreover, questions 
have been raised about the future survivability 
of HALE/MALE UAVs in a contested environ-
ment. These classes of UAVs, if not designed with 
signature-management features, are potentially 
vulnerable if operated against an opponent with 
credible medium- to long-range air defence. In 
part this is why countries such as India and South 
Korea are also looking to develop stealthy UCAVs. 
OWA UAVs are increasingly seen as a viable 
and cost-effective means of conducting preci-
sion strikes, with OWA UAVs also increasingly 
seen as a means for enabling asymmetric over-
match. In fact, the use of UAVs in this context 
has already seen real-world application among 
the case studies. The question remains whether 
governments have the resources to acquire these 
UAVs at the scale required to ensure their max-
imum efficacy and whether industrial bases can 
produce the required quantities. 
Australia, through the MQ-28 programme, 
has and continues to gain experience in devel-
oping a CCA and may well be the first coun-
try, aside from China, to operationally deploy 
this capability in the Indo-Pacific. Japan has 
begun developing CCAs to operate alongside 
new aircraft, while South Korea plans to have 
CCAs operational by 2030, with attritable, mod-
ular CCAs introduced first, followed by larger, 
fighter-equivalent CCAs. The Indian CCA pro-
grammes are an ambitious, multifaceted  col-
lection that includes attritable CCAs. Such 
ambition, however, has not always been met, 
with domestic defence-aerospace programmes 
sometimes running into substantial delays.

109UAVS: ISR, DETERRENCE AND WAR 
Conclusion
Uninhabited systems in one guise or 
another are becoming ubiquitous in 
the inventories of armed forces. As 
such, they are changing the charac-
ter of war. Uninhabited systems are 
replacing a variety of crewed plat-
forms, supplementing others, and 
will be operated in cooperation with 
other piloted aircraft. While uninhab-
ited technology will be increasingly 
important to and widely used by 
armed forces, it will not be ubiquitous 
in replacing crewed platforms, with 
human involvement remaining vital. 
Deterrence, similarly, is an endur-
ing element of confl  ict, either as a 
national or an allied eff  ort to dissuade 
an adversary from taking a course of 
action, such as an armed att  ack, com-
municating that the adversary would 
stand to lose more than it would gain 
from any aggression. Assessing capa-
bility and intent are supporting ele-
ments of deterrence, and intelligence, 
surveillance and reconnaissance (ISR) 
is part of the deterrence toolkit. 
The United States is increas-
ingly looking to space as a domain 
through which to provide ISR. The 
ambition to provide moving-target 
indication from orbit was one rea-
son the US Department of Defense, 
in 2025, att  empted to cancel the pur-
chase of the Boeing E-7 aircraft as the 
air force’s next-generation airborne 
early-warning aircraft.  
Such an approach is far from risk 
free, as evidenced by the counter-
space activities of Washington’s peer 
and near-peer rivals. This is one rea-
son why crewed and uninhabited ISR 
aircraft will continue to play a role in 
deterrence and in war. 
Recent wars, however, have 
underscored the vulnerability of 
many current-generation uninhabited 
aerial vehicle (UAV) designs to even 
modest ground-based air defence 
(GBAD). This is forcing changes to 
tactics, techniques and procedures to 
lower the risk to ISR UAVs. The adop-
tion of increased stand-off  , as seen 
in the war in Ukraine, reduces expo-
sure to GBAD but comes at a cost of 
reduced sensor coverage or image 
resolution. This generation of UAVs 
nonetheless retains utility, including 
in operations other than war, while 
also supporting deterrence through 
intelligence gathering. Moreover, 
signature management, longer-range 
sensors, and the introduction of 
low-cost disposable or att  ritable ISR 
UAVs will all play a part in provid-
ing greater survivability as well as 
acceptable loss rates. 
Much has been made of the use 
of uninhabited systems in Russia’s 
war of aggression in Ukraine. This 
war, however, is not a template for all 
future state-on-state confl  ict, nor for 
drawing one-size-fi  ts-all lessons as to 
=== PAGE 111 ===
AN IISS STRATEGIC DOSSIER110
the employment of uninhabited systems. It does 
demonstrate, however, the utility of uninhabited 
platforms as a vehicle for military adaptation. 
Ukrainian ground forces used small first-person 
view (FPV) UAVs to compensate for a lack of 
artillery munitions. Both Kyiv and Moscow have 
also increasingly used FPVs and loitering muni-
tions to extend the range of the killing zone to 
tens of kilometres either side of the forward line 
of contact.  
FPVs along with one-way attack UAVs are 
likely to be more widely adopted by armed 
forces, though in the case of the former perhaps 
for more limited applications.  
The continuing importance of gaining supe-
riority in the air domain, and the implications if 
that cannot be secured, as is apparent in Ukraine, 
reinforce wider efforts to make even more use of 
UAVs in combat roles. As well as ISR and air-to-
surface attack, the air-to-air role is now an aim.
 Washington’s pursuit of collaborative combat 
aircraft (CCA) is driven primarily by the grow-
ing air threat from China. CCAs both address 
the need for greater combat mass and provide 
greater lethality and survivability. They promise 
lethality by offering additional weapon platforms 
that can be deployed within or close to adversary 
airspace, and survivability by allowing crewed 
combat aircraft to be operated from behind the 
relative security of uninhabited fighter screens. 
For Europe, the attractions are much the 
same, even if the lexicon and the roles some-
times differ. The United Kingdom’s autonomous 
collaborative platform (ACP), or France and 
Germany’s ‘remote carriers’ (RC) or ‘Loyal 
Wingman’, are all intended to provide greater 
combat capability and capacity, while manag-
ing the threat to crewed aircraft. Germany’s 
‘fighter-bomber drone’ requirement, meanwhile, 
echoes earlier notions of the uninhabited com-
bat air vehicle (UCAV). The roles envisaged for 
initial UCAV concepts in the early 2000s were 
air-to-surface, with the air vehicle operating 
independently of crewed combat aircraft.  
Indo-Pacific and Middle East activity further 
reflects the sustained interest in uninhabited sys-
tems. Near-term off-the-shelf purchases are often 
being complemented by longer-term domestic 
development ambitions. 
As with the US, there is increasing interest 
in introducing some of these systems to support 
combat aircraft types already in the inventory, 
rather than developing only ACPs or RCs as a 
complement to the next generation of crewed 
combat aircraft.
Uninhabited systems have emerged from a 
niche air capability limited to ISR to now ranging 
across many of the traditional roles of airpower. 
Their utility is no longer in question, with the 
addition of new designs and capabilities allowing 
their employment in ever wider roles. The ease 
with which the UAV system, nearly regardless of 
size, can be modified, be this in terms of hardware 
or often more likely software, also invites military 
adaptation in war. As such their increasingly 
broad place in military inventories is ensured.

The IISS Strategic Dossier UAVs: ISR, Deterrence and War considers the key role of 
intelligence, surveillance and reconnaissance (ISR) uninhabited aerial vehicles (UAVs) in 
both peace and war. This volume reviews how select states in Europe, the Middle East 
and Asia employ UAVs for ISR in support of deterrence and combat. It also assesses 
UAV use in Russia’s war in Ukraine and the broader lessons this may oﬀ  er. The volume, 
additionally, explores how the selected countries are approaching the development 
and/or acquisition of what are commonly known as collaborative combat aircraft (CCA). 
Space is of ever-growing importance for ISR, but UAVs will continue to oﬀ  er a 
key utility. For some, they provide a supporting capability to space-based systems, for 
others, they may be the primary source of ISR provision. 
The war in Ukraine has underscored the utility of UAVs while also exposing their 
vulnerability. Emerging classes of UAVs have been employed by both belligerents, 
including ﬁ  rst-person view and one-way att  ack UAVs. Medium-altitude long-endurance 
UAVs continue to be used, though the tactics, techniques and procedures for their 
employment have been adapted to reduce their exposure to ground-based air defence.   
This volume provides a valuable resource for understanding the critical role 
UAVs play in ISR and how these platforms are increasingly used to deliver other 
capabilities. It oﬀ  ers insights and emerging lessons from the use of uninhabited 
systems in a peer-on-peer war. Additionally, it assesses the roles now being 
considered for CCAs, as these countries each look to augment their inventories of 
crewed combat aircraft.
